LAL SHAHBAZ QALANDAR (SEHWAN SHARIF) — shrine entry (holistic)
Sheet rows: "Lal Shahbaz Qalandar" (Jamshoro) AND "Sehwan Sharif" (Sehwan) — this entry serves both (same saint/shrine).
NOTE: no dedicated book or field survey; grounded in the Tazkirah Awliya-e-Pakistan compendium + established history.
Copy the block from "## Overview" to the end of "## Legacy" into the sheet's "Description".
=====================================================================================

## Overview

At Sehwan Sharif, in Sindh, stands the great shrine of Hazrat Lal Shahbaz Qalandar (c. 1177–1274) — perhaps the most ecstatically beloved saint in all of Pakistan, and the object of one of its largest and most exuberant pilgrimages. A wandering _qalandar_ — a mystic of the free, rapturous, rule-breaking kind — Lal Shahbaz drew Sindh into the world of Sufi devotion, and his memory is celebrated in the thunder of the _dhamal_, the trance-dance beaten out on the great drums of his shrine, and in the anthem _Dama Dam Mast Qalandar_, which has carried his name across the world. His golden-domed darbar is the heart of Sehwan, and its urs one of the defining festivals of the Sindhi year.

## The Life of the Saint

Sayyid Usman Marwandi — known to the world as Lal Shahbaz Qalandar — was born around 1177 at Marwand, in the Persian-speaking lands to the west (in the region of present-day Afghanistan/Iran), into a Sayyid family tracing descent from the Prophet through the Imams. Steeped from youth in learning and devotion, he took to the path of the wandering dervishes and travelled widely through the Muslim world — through Iraq, the Hejaz, and Central Asia — before coming to Sindh and settling at Sehwan, an ancient town with a deep pre-Islamic sacred history. There he lived as a _qalandar_, drawing crowds by his ecstatic sanctity, and there he died around 1274. He is called _Lal_ (“the Red”) for the crimson robe of his order, and _Shahbaz_ (“royal falcon”) for the soaring freedom of his spirit; and he is counted, in Sindhi and Punjabi tradition, among the “four friends” (_chaar yaar_) of the qalandari way, alongside such saints as Baba Farid and Bahauddin Zakariya, with whom legend links him in companionship.

## The Poetry and the Dhamal

Lal Shahbaz composed in Persian, and among the verses attributed to him is a ringing declaration of his identity as an intoxicated lover of God and devotee of the Prophet’s family:

حیدریم قلندرم مستم ٭ بندۂ مرتضیٰ علی ہستم

[“I am a Haidari, a qalandar, and intoxicated; I am the slave of Murtaza Ali.”]

But it is not chiefly his own verse that keeps his memory: it is the great devotional song made in his honour, the _Dama Dam Mast Qalandar_, whose refrain rings out wherever South Asian Sufi music is sung —

دَمادَم مست قلندر، علی دَم دَم دے اندر

[“At every breath, the intoxicated Qalandar! Ali is in every breath.”]

— and, above all, the _dhamal_: the ecstatic, whirling, drum-driven dance performed at his shrine, especially on Thursday evenings and at the urs, in which devotees lose themselves in rhythm and remembrance. To the beat of the huge _naqqara_ drums, before the blaze of lamps, the dhamal of Sehwan is one of the most electrifying expressions of devotion in the Muslim world.

## The Shrine and its Devotion

Lal Shahbaz’s shrine at Sehwan, with its gilded dome and mirrored interior, is among the most important pilgrimage sites in Pakistan, thronged year-round and overwhelmingly so at the annual urs, held in the Islamic month of Sha‘ban, when hundreds of thousands of devotees converge for days of dhamal, qawwali, and _langar_. The shrine has always been a strikingly inclusive place, drawing Muslims and Hindus alike and women in great numbers, and famed for a devotion that is joyous, bodily, and unrestrained. That openness has also made it a target: in February 2017 a suicide bombing at the shrine during the evening dhamal killed scores of worshippers — yet, in a gesture that became famous, the dhamal was danced again at the shrine the very next evening, in defiance of the terror. The shrine is administered by the Sindh Auqaf department and remains the beating heart of Sehwan.

## Legacy

Lal Shahbaz Qalandar embodies the ecstatic, boundary-crossing pole of South Asian Sufism — the qalandar who dances his way to God and gathers all comers, of every faith and station, into the rhythm of divine love. Through the song that bears his spirit and the dhamal of his shrine, his name is known far beyond Sindh, and his darbar at Sehwan remains one of the great living centres of devotion in Pakistan, a place where sanctity is celebrated not in silence but in drums, colour, and dance.

## Bibliography

- Alam Faqri, _Tazkirah Awliya-e-Pakistan_ (Lahore) — compendium of the saints of Pakistan.
- Verses attributed to Lal Shahbaz Qalandar and the traditional _Dama Dam Mast Qalandar_ (quoted in standard published wording).
- General established histories of the qalandari tradition and of Sehwan Sharif.

The book names him "Lal Shahbaz", "Lal · Shahbaz"[text-layer?], "Lal Shahba_z"[text-layer?],
"Sayid Usman 'Shah[text-layer?] Marwandi", "Jivelal, the ' living Pir '", "Shah Lal Baz" and,

nothing else, and that material is converted into a place-level bullet below rather than left here.**

**Saint.**
- **His personal name, given once.** "The best-known early one was Sayid Usman 'Shah[text-layer?]
  Marwandi who became famous as Lal Shahba_z[text-layer?] with a popular shrine at Sehwan."
  (p. 298, folio 289). **This matches the archive row's parenthetical "(Usman Marwandi)" and is the
  first time the book gives it.** **The row's death year 1274 and its silsila "Qalandari" are NOT
  in this book**: it gives only "the thirteenth century when Lal Shahbaz flourished" (p. 299,
  folio 290) and "He was a Kalandar[text-layer?]" (p. 299, folio 290) — **(not stated)** for both.
- **His reputation.** "He was a Kalandar[text-layer?] an9-[text-layer?] attained a great reputation
  for sanctity in his lifetime and · after his . death." (p. 299, folio 290).
- **He arrived with a party, inside a dated wave of immigration.** "From the thirteenth century
  onwards there was a great immigration into Sind of learned and religious-minded men from the
  centre of the Muslim world. **Lal Shahbaz, who came with several companions, some of whom made
  names for themselves elsewhere in India, was a man of this kind.** The ancestor of Abul
  Fazul[text-layer?] was another. The great ascendancy of the Sayids in Sind doubtless dates from
  about this . time." (p. 302, folio 293). **None of the companions is named** — **(not stated)**.
- **He was an author, and his works were school texts — a striking and citable fact.** "He was the
  author of a number of works used for the instruction of youth in a later age, as noted by
  Burton." (p. 298, folio 289) — **no title is given there** — **(not stated)** — and the
  attribution is to Burton, not to Sorley's own inspection. Four titles are given elsewhere, as
  the first four books of the Sindhi madressa syllabus: "The works studied were the Mizan-i-sarf by
  Lal Shahbaz, the famous scholar and saint of Sehwan�[text-layer?]"; "Then followed study of the.
  Ajnas or Munshaib rules for the formation of the increased derivatives; and the
  Kisim�i-d.oyyuni.[text-layer?] for the ii-regular[text-layer?] verbs. . Both these works ·were by
  Lal ·Shahbaz but were written in Persian. The next book to be read was the Akd, also by. Lal
  Shahbaz, in Arabic and P�rsiari[text-layer?] mixed, dealing with.the permutation of letters"
  (both p. 223, folio 214). **Four works — "Mizan-i-sarf", "Ajnas or Munshaib",
  "Kisim�i-d.oyyuni"[text-layer?], "Akd" — with their languages specified.** **This chunk attaches
  the saint to Sehwan by name ("the famous scholar and saint of Sehwan"), not merely the toponym.**
- **The modern source for his life, and Sorley's verdict on it.** "Jethmal Parsram has given some
  account of him and his companions in his book on ' Sind and its Sufis ' ; but the facts are
  meagre." (p. 299, folio 290).
- **His shrine is the earliest fixed point in Sorley's chronology of Sind Sufism, and the five
  centuries after him are declared a documentary blank** (p. 299, folio 290; quoted in full under
  `bhit-bhit-shah` above).

**Shrine / Darbar.**
- **The shrine, its location and its annual gathering — the fullest single statement in the book.**
  "The Shahbaz is of course the famous Lal Shahbaz, or Jivelal, the ' living Pir ' whose shrine at
  Sehwan attracts a great gathering of both Mussulmans and Hindus every year in a strange mixture
  of worship ." (p. 257, folio 248). **Four facts: a shrine; a location, "at Sehwan"; an annual
  gathering; and a cross-community congregation which Sorley calls "a strange mixture of worship".**
  **No date for the gathering, no ʿurs, no mela or fair name, no custodian, no offerings, no
  attendance figure, no death year, no silsila and no alternative name such as Usman Marwandi**
  appear there — all **(not stated)** in that chunk.
- **The route by which he enters the book is Baluchi oral poetry, not a Sindhi source.** The verse
  Sorley is glossing reads "also Shahbaz, the generous to his friends, a firm embankment erected by
  the Ruler of the Faithful.'" (p. 257, folio 248, footnote 2 citing "ibid . , p. 146.", i.e.
  Dames's *Popular Poetry of the Baloches*). **"Lal Shahbaz" as a name is Sorley's identification
  of the poem's "Shahbaz", not the poem's own wording** — a transmission fact worth keeping.
- **The index's entry for the saint, with two printed pages no chunk of running prose reaches.**
  "Lal · Shahbaz, 158 (Shah Lal Baz) 159. 2 14[text-layer?], 248, 28 9[text-layer?], 293" (p. 436)
  — **printed pp. 158, 159, 214, 248, 289, 293, of which only 248 is the chunk-016 passage.**
  Printed 158–159 falls inside the index's "privileged classes" and "Sayids (privileged) 157-62"
  ranges, and printed 289 and 293 inside "Siifis[text-layer?] and Sufism … (practice of by ' common
  man ') 289-94" (p. 440). **The entry gives no death year, no silsila, no ʿurs date, no custodian
  and no offering** — all **(not stated)**. The place is indexed separately and at length:
  "Sehwan, 2 1[text-layer?], 4 1 -3[text-layer?], 74, 75, 78, 89, 97, 104, I I 7[text-layer?],
  I I9[text-layer?], 138, 158-60 , 2 16[text-layer?], 248" (p. 439) — **fourteen printed pages, of
  which 138, 158–160 and 248 overlap the saint's own entry and the rest are trade, revenue and
  route material.**

**Practices & Events.**
- **THE ʿURS — the saint-day gathering at the tomb, in Sorley's own voice and in the present
  tense.** "**The gathering at his tomb on his saint-day produced and still produces
  s1ftange[text-layer?] sights. Religion and superstition are mingled and Hindus and Muslims
  assemble together to do honour to · his memory.**" (p. 299, folio 290). **Five facts: the tomb is
  the venue; the occasion is "his saint-day"; the gathering is continuing; it is cross-community;
  and Sorley's own gloss is "Religion and superstition are mingled".** **No date, no month, no name
  for the observance, no duration and no figure are given by Sorley himself** — **(not stated)**.
- **THE FAIR — Jethmal Parsram quoted, with an attendance figure, a camping practice and a musical
  practice.** "Jethmal Parsram remarks, ' **In Sehwan where Lal · Shahbaz[text-layer?] (also called
  Kalandar Lal Marwandi) lived and died you find during the annual fair thousands of Hindus and
  Muhammadans camped round the t<?mh[text-layer?], mixing freely, and singing the Sufi songs that
  ever melt the heart.** Wrong things have also entered into the celebration of these holy days,
  but that is what always happens. '" (p. 299, folio 290, footnote 1 citing "op. cit., p. 93.").
  **Nine separately citable facts: a location; a life-and-death claim ("lived and died" there); an
  alternative name; a periodicity ("the annual fair"); an order of magnitude ("thousands"); two
  named communities; an accommodation practice ("camped round the t<?mh[text-layer?]"); a social
  claim ("mixing freely"); and a musical practice ("singing the Sufi songs"). Plus a disapproving
  aside and a plural, "these holy days", implying the fair runs more than one day.**
  **"t<?mh"[text-layer?] is "tomb" destroyed in the text layer, and it sits in the most quotable
  sentence about this shrine in the whole book. It MUST be checked against the PDF before
  quotation.** **No month, no calendar date, no custodian, no sajjada nashin, no offerings, no
  revenue and no endowment are mentioned** — all **(not stated)**.

**Legends & Miracles.**
- **The epithet "the ' living Pir '", glossing a Sindhi-looking word.** "the famous Lal Shahbaz, or
  Jivelal, the ' living Pir '" (p. 257, folio 248). **The printed string is "Jivelal"; Sorley's own
  gloss for it is "the ' living Pir '". The chunk supplies no story behind it** — **(not stated)**.
  **This collides with the "Zinda Pir (The Living Pir)" the book attaches to Khwajo Khizr at
  p. 178 (folio 169), and with three Jhulelal / Daryalal / Uderolal rows in the archive; no merge
  is made here — see the person-level bullet below and the Doubts list.**

**Significance.**
- **He and Shah Abdul Latif are the two fixed points of Sindhi Sufism in this book, and everything
  between them is declared unverified** (p. 299, folio 290).
- **Two cross-references the index makes into this entry that no page of running prose makes, and
  both are serious.** "Murad Buksh Shah . (See Lal Shahbaz)" (p. 438) — **which collides head-on
  with the same index's separate entry "Sayid Muhammad Husain (Pir Murad) 248, 291" (p. 439), a
  different saint with a tomb at Tatta**; and "Udero Lal (see Lal Shahbaz) 159" (p. 441) — **which
  puts the archive row `shrine-at-odero-lal-udero-lal-teerath-asthan` directly in play.** **Neither
  merge is made; both go to the Doubts worker.**
- **What this book does NOT supply for the row**: a death year, a silsila, an ʿurs date or month, a
  custodian or sajjada nashin, any offering, endowment or revenue, any architecture, and any
  founding date for the shrine — all **(not stated)**.

### Doubts the notes raise on this row

- **The Udero Lal / Lal Shahbaz equation — the book makes it twice and the archive does not make it
  at all.** "the shrine of the Kalandar Shah Lal Baz (or Udero Lal) at Sehwan" (p. 168, folio 159)
  and the index's `Udero Lal (see Lal Shahbaz) 159` (p. 441). The archive carries
  **`lal-shahbaz-qalandar`** (Sehwan Sharif, Jamshoro) and **`shrine-at-odero-lal-udero-lal-teerath-asthan`**
  (Odero Lal village, Matiari District, Sheikh Tahir / Udero Lal (Jhulelal)) as **two separate rows
  at two separate places**, plus `jhollay-lal-mandir` and `darya-lal-mandir-darya-lal-sankat-mochan-mandir`
  in Karachi. **"Sorley's parenthesis is an identification the archive does not make, and this chunk
  must not be used to merge the rows… the identification is the index's, it is made in three words,
  it is contradicted by the archive's own row, and it must not be acted on without a human ruling."**
  The two other Sehwan references in that chunk carry no Udero Lal alias, so **the equation rests on
  one parenthesis on one page** plus one index cross-reference. **A human must decide whether it is
  Sorley's error, a genuine nineteenth-century conflation he is reporting, or a reference to a
  Jhulelal cult present at Sehwan; the chunk supplies no basis for deciding, and none should be
  inferred.** The printed form "Jivelal" (p. 257, folio 248) is a third string in the same knot —
  **"Those three rows must NOT be merged with this passage without a human ruling on whether the
  printed 'Jivelal' is the same word as 'Jhulelal' or a text-layer corruption of 'Zinda Pir' /
  'Jinda Pir'."**

- **"Lal Shahbaz" (p. 223, folio 214) is mapped to `lal-shahbaz-qalandar` because the text attaches
  him to Sehwan by name and calls him a saint, but "The book never prints 'Qalandar' and never
  mentions his shrine, ʿurs, dates or order. A reviewer should confirm that Sorley's four grammar
  textbooks are in fact attributed to the saint of Sehwan and not to a homonymous grammarian; the
  attribution of a standard Arabic morphology primer to a thirteenth-century qalandar is the kind of
  claim that a source can get wrong, and the archive should not repeat it without a check."**

### Province-level material: sindh

*The book's `sindh` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

In some fori:n [text-layer?] [form] or other_ the verses are. known to all classes" (p. 7, folio not stated); the poetry "exercises over Hindus in Sind a spell almost as complete as that exercised by it over Muslims" (p. 175, folio 166), and its appeal lies "deep in the hearts of all classes of Sind's population, Muslim and Hindu, lettered and unlettered" (pp. 178–179, folios 169–170).
- (book's word: "the people of Sind"; refers to: the author's own day set against an unnamed past, period not stated; author's dedicatory verse, "DEDICATED TO THE PEOPLE OF SIND"): "Gone are the days when tempered blade / And matchlocks'. fire laid waste your ·fields. / The drums of peacefulness are struck : / Your land a full abundance yields." (p. 9, folio not stated).

- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: 1592 to "the first decade of the nineteenth century"; book's historical prose): "Sind was added to the Moghul Empire by Akbar in 1592. - (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: the Kalhora and Talpurs, "for one hundred years" up to "the British conquest in 1843"; author's argument): their "combined efforts" established "in Sind a petty Muslim state which preserved, up till the time of the British conquest in 1843,. the characteristic features of Moghul administration and added to that a peculiar brand of Islamic quasi-theocracy ... a policy of splendid isolation" (p. 19, folio 10); "The aim of all Sind policy from 1701 onwards was to make Sind independent of Moghul, Persian and Pathan, to diminish the payment of tribute ... to preserve the land as a closed terrain into which no foreigners of any sort were allowed entrance except with the utmost difficulty"; Sind in 1843 "was then to all intents and purposes a sovereign and petty Muslim state" (pp. 35–36, folios 26–27); "the Kalh6ra [text-layer?] [Kalhora] and Talpiirs [text-layer?] [Talpurs] between them succeeded in keeping Sind shut to the influences of the outside world for a hundred and fifty years" (p. 41, folio 32).

- (book's word: "Scindy"; refers to: Moghul times and "about 35 years agoe" from 1775; quoted source, Mr Callendar's account, P.D.D. 1775): "While the Mogul's government flourished Scindy was a province of the Empire and being considered as an Honorary appointment was annually bestowed on some person of rank whose expences civil and military generally exceeded the revenues" (p. 61, folio 52); "Scindy tho a small track of country is naturally fertile and was rich and populous when it unfortunately fell under the government of this family about 35 years agoe, but has gradually declined ever since"; "Its chief political connections are with the Patan and Siccs" (pp. 61–62, folios 52–53).
- (book's word: "the Sind Government", "Sind"; refers to: 1758–75 and the tribute from Nadir Shah to Timur Shah; author's argument and quoted source, Callendar via the author): the Sind Government regarded the Afghan government at Kabul "as a form of unwarranted alien oppression" and its customs and transit dues were "as unreasonable and oppressive as they had been in the Moghul days" (pp. 57–58, folios 48–49); "In Sind, however, this change never took place. The Company was always very much the timid guest of the Sind Government" (p. 58, folio 49); the tribute was reduced "from 21 lakhs (Nadir Shah l7J9 [text-layer?] [1739]) to 14 lakhs (Ahmad Shah 1747), t o I I [text-layer?] [to 11] lakhs (Ahmad Shah about 1760), to , 7 lakhs (Timiir Shah about . 1775)" and "was always in arrears" (p. 154, folio 145).
- (book's word: "Sind coasts", "the Sind coast"; refers to: the 1750s–1760s; book's historical prose and quoted source, Company letter of 1758): pirates "in those days infested the Gujarat, Kathiawar and Sind coasts" (p. 58, folio 49); "indeed the whole coast are rogues on both s�des [text-layer?] [sides]" (p. 63, folio 54); "The existence o f pirates o n the Sind coast caused continual anxiety to shippers" (p. 71, folio 62).
- (book's word: "Sind", "Sindhi craftsmen"; refers to: 1760–64; book's historical prose from Company records): letters note "the paucity of skilled workmen available in Sind" (p. 67, folio 58) and "The inability of Sind to provide suitable artisans" (p. 72, folio 63); "It was impossible to get Sindhi craftsmen capable of making the kind of article needed" (p. 75, folio 66); "artificers could not be procured in Sind" for saltpetre coppers (p. 105, folio 96).
- (book's word: "Sindhi Muhammadans", "Sind"; refers to: the author's own day, present tense, applied to 1759; author's argument): "As Sindhi Muhammadans are notoriously unhappy away from Sind, it was perhaps their fondness for desertion that made them un­suitable in Bombay" (p. 64, folio 55).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: the 1640s, the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries; author's argument from Company records): "even at that time big business was in the hands of Hindu traders, some of whorn [text-layer?] [whom] did succeed in acquiring considerable wealth" (p. 54, folio 45); brokers, agents and dalals "were mostly Hindus" who could not display opulence (p. 100, folio 91); "In Sind there were many considerable Hindu traders on whom tl�e [text-layer?] [the] Company's agents relied" (p. 113, folio 104).
- (book's word: "Sind", "Synda"; refers to: the seventeenth century; author's argument and quoted source, Fremlen 1636): Sind, "with its internal waterway system of the Indus, its easy connexion with Lahore and Multan ... its thriving cotton industry, and its vast emporium at Tatta with its busy port at Laribunder, was soon envisaged as a profitable centre of trade with Europe" (p. 97, folio 88); Fremlen, 1636: "For of all Indian goods none are in such request as those of Synda" (p. 51, folio 42); "Tatta and Nasarpiir [text-layer?] [Nasarpur] were the main centres of the industry in Sind" (p. 102, folio 93).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries; author's argument): "In Sind itself transport was all by pack animals, mostly camels ... Wheeled traffic was practically unknown"; "The oriental system of markets and fairs on saints' days at saints' tombs was well established ... In fact in this respect Sind's commerce was · on the old medieval system" (p. 98, folio 89); "trade in Sind was on a very well-organized basis, both in respect of land and water j ourneys [text-layer?] [journeys]; the business was soundly established and the government supervision very thorough" (p. 100, folio 91).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "about the time of the British annexation", "after half a century of Talpiir [text-layer?] [Talpur] rule", against "the days of the Moghul and KalhOro rule"; book's historical prose from Postans): "Sind had then for nearly a hundred years ceased to be a source of supply of cottons for the European market"; "In fact Sind was now importing English chintzes and calicoes instead of exporting her own"; exports were "rice, ghi, opium, indigo, dried fish and leather"; "Upper Sind supplying Lower Sind with dry grains ... ghi, cotton and indigo and receiving in return rice, fish, manufactured leather and arms"; this was trade after Talpur rule "during which the chief object had been to shut Sind off from the outside world" (pp. 100–101, folios 91–92).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "Moghul and Kalhoro times", with comparisons to the author's day; author's argument and ethnographic present): "During Moghul and Kalhoro times the chief industries were the manufacture of cotton goods, saltpetre and indigo, leather and fish"; hand flour-milling, oil pressing and the potter's craft "can still be seen in Sind exactly as it was in Moghul and Kalhoro days"; apart from those industries "Sind deserves little special notice industrially" (pp. 101–102, folios 92–93); "the popular modem [text-layer?] [modern] idea of Sind as a desert is as fallacious as the view that seventeenth and eighteenth century Sind was a purely agricultural country ... Sind supported half a dozen thriving industries", with camels and buffaloes supporting much of the population; Crowe (quoted): "Camels are bred in every part of Sind" (p. 110, folio 101).
- (book's word: "Sind", "Middle Sind"; refers to: the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries; book's historical prose): saltpetre production was "estimated up to 10,000 maunds (crude) annually" (p. 104, folio 95); "The supply from Sind was never more than a useful auxiliary of the main supply obtained from Bengal and Bihar", and "The resources of Sind were, however, running low towards the end of the second factory's days" (p. 105, folio 96); of indigo, "Sind was never very important as a source of supply ... Indigo growing was concentrated mostly in Middle Sind ... The quality was, however, inferior" (p. 106, folio 97).
- (book's word: "Sind", "a Muslim land"; refers to: "always", the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries, and the author's day; author's argument and ethnographic present): "Sind has always been a land with large numbers of buffaloes and oxen"; "Sind had always, as it still has, a plentiful supply of hides"; "Sind saddlery and ornamental leather-work ... were famous" (p. 107, folio 98); "In Sind, a Muslim land, this prejudice against leather is not felt by three-quarters of the population" (p. 108, folio 99).
- (book's word: "Sind", "Lower Sind"; refers to: "always", the Ain-i-Akbari's day, and "the days of the second factory"; author's argument and quoted source, Ain-i-Akbari): "Sind has always been noted for the excellence of its fresh and salt water fish"; the Ain says "the staple food in Lower Sind ' consists of rice and fish'"; "The Lower Sind littoral was, with Gujarat, Kanara and Malabar, one of the great areas which supplied this indigenous seafaring population" (pp. 108–109, folios 99–100).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "By 1770", "from 1775 onwards" to "after 1843"; author's argument): "Sind suffered with the rest of cotton manufacturing India", and the later Kalhora turned "Sind into a self-contained Muslim state closed for nearly three­quarters of.a century to Englishmen ... Sind relapsed into a condition of lethargy which it maintained till, -after 1843"; "The decline of Sind's trade from 1775 onwards was not due to the incapacity of the Kalhorn [text-layer?] [Kalhora] or the narrow parochialism ·of the Talpiirs [text-layer?] [Talpurs]" (p. 116, folio 107).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: 1836 and the nineteenth-century British accounts; quoted source, Assistant-Surgeon Heddle, May 1836, and author's argument): Heddle: "The people of Sind have also been much decried ... The large masses of the indigenous population are particularly industrious ... The merchants of Sind are active and intelligent, well protected though heavily taxed by the Government. . . . The people are orderly and obedient and the laws are respected" (pp. 116–117, folios 107–108). The author holds that "many of the criticisms of Victorian writers are unjust" (p. 30, folio 21), that Burton "despised Sind for what he considered its lax Muhammadanism" (p. 120, folio 111), and that Burton's view that Sind "came into British hanas [text-layer?] [hands] in an almost exhausted state" cannot be accepted "without considerable qualification" (p. 143, folio 134).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "Moghul and Kalhoro days" against the author's present, and earlier Arab and Moghul sources; author's argument): "Of Sind it is particularly untrue. Sind is one of those lucky lands that are not seriously troubled if the monsoon·rains fail, because Sind is the creation, the slow geographical accretion, of a river"; its calamities are "unchecked inunda­tion, of drifting sand that covers fertile land, of changing river channels" (p. 118, folio 109); "Sind has always been able within its limits to' [text-layer?] support in modest comfort a tolerably well-fed population"; "The land was considered a sort of Arabia Felix with a quiet, industrious and intelligent people living in isolation from the rest of the Indian continent" (p. 119, folio 110); "famines such as devastated less fortunate parts of India had ordinarily no terrors for the Sindhi peasant" (p. 129, folio 120); "Sind was relatively immune from famines" (p. 143, folio 134).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "the Moghul and Kalhoro days", with figures for 1828, 1832 and 1847 and "the present day"; author's argument and quoted sources): "In the Moghul and Kalhoro days Sind did not support one quarter of the population it now supports" (p. 118, folio 109); Del Hoste "calculated in 1832 that the population was 850,000, of which about one-fourth were Hindus", Burnes in 1828 "about one million" (p. 126, folio 117); "between 1740 and 1 800 [text-layer?] [1800] there was a great shifting of the population of Sind, the chief concentration altering from Tatta to Shikarpiir [text-layer?] [Shikarpur], Hyderabad and Karachi"; the Commissioner in Sind in 1847 put the population "at l,274,732 [text-layer?] [1,274,732]" on "48 ,000 [text-layer?] [48,000] square miles"; "during Moghul and Kalhorn [text-layer?] [Kalhoro] days the population cannot have exceeded one million altogether, or less than a quarter of what it is at the present day" (p. 127, folio 118).
- (book's word: "Upper and Lower Sind", "the Chandookah and the Lar"; refers to: 1847 and "just as they are today"; quoted source, the Commissioner in Sind, 1847): bajri and jowari "more prevalent in the Upper", wheat and barley "in the Lower ·portion. Rice is grown partially to the northward but more e�tensively [text-layer?] [extensively] in the low southern parts" (p. 127, folio 118); "The Chandookah and the Lar were the two great rice producing areas just as they are today"; cultivators "were considered in 1847 as being ordinarily in better circumstances than in most parts of India" (p. 128, folio 119).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "seventeenth and eighteenth century Sind"; author's argument): "The society was an agricultural and pastoral one with cottage industry well-diffused and with town-life, as we understand it today, concentrated in Tatta only" (p. 121, folio 112).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "the earliest days", "Moghul and Kalh6ro [text-layer?] [Kalhoro] times", the Talpurs, Col. Scott's report of 1853, and the author's day; author's argument and quoted source, Lieut.-Col. Scott): "It is impossible to exaggerate the important place irriga­tion in Sind has always played", "where rainfall is scanty and never regular" (p. 121, folio 112); "The Sind canals, Persian wheeis [text-layer?] [wheels] and wells were constructed by the natives of the land without any assistance from the Empire of Delhi" (p. 122, folio 113); "There is not the slightest doubt that' skilful irrigation has existed in Sind since the earliest days" (p. 135, folio 126); "Sind has always been a pioneer of irrigation in India"; Kalhora irrigation was probably "superior to that prevailing during the regime of the Talpiirs [text-layer?] [Talpurs]"; Scott: "the whole system of canals in Scinde is one of makeshifts and expedients" (p. 138, folio 129); lift methods "are still in use today and can be seen all over Sind" (p. 139, folio 130); the canal system "during Moghul and Kalhorn [text-layer?] [Kalhoro] days must, within its limits, have been a very important source of the country's wealth" (pp. 142–143, folios 133–134).
- (book's word: "Sind", "Lower Sind", "rural Sjnd" [text-layer?] [rural Sind]; refers to: Moghul days, the Talpur period and "today"; author's argument, ethnographic present and quoted sources, Pottinger, von Orlich, Lieut. James): the agricultural and pastoral population lived in huts that could be put up and moved with the floods; nineteenth-century accounts "accord very much with present-day conditions in agricultural and pastoral areas in Sind" (p. 123, folio 114); "The Talpiirs [text-layer?] [Talpurs], like true Baliichis [text-layer?] [Baluchis], set little store upon magnificent buildings", and "Baliich [text-layer?] [Baluch] influence wa� [text-layer?] [was] partly at least responsible for the decline in the standard of building" (pp. 123–124, folios 114–115); of von Orlich's 1842 Lower Sind clay houses, "All who are acquainted with Lower Sind today will recognize the truthfulness of this picture" (p. 124, folio 115); wind scoops are "still in universal use in Lower Sind to catch the south-west wind" (p. 49, folio 40); "The Sindhi village must in fact have reminded one strongly of the villages of early medieval Europe ... This description is still true in most particulars of the small agricultural villages scattered throughout the length and breadth of rural Sjnd [text-layer?] [Sind] today" (p. 125, folio 116), with Hindu traders in the bazaar and "the Musulman cultivators" in landhis round it (p. 126, folio 117).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "Moghul and Kalhoro times" against "today"; author's argument): forced labour (begari) "had always been in practice" in Sind, "Indeed several of the biggest canals were dug by labour con­scripted in this way" (p. 130, folio 121); "The labourers, artisans and peasants were therefore relatively little worse off in Moghul and Kalhoro times than they are today" (p. 131, folio 122); the moneylender in Sind advanced "money against a share of the crop ... The system still prevails"; children herding cattle are still "one of the chief obstacles in the education of the peasant class" (p. 132, folio 123); "In Sind no less than in India generally throughout the Moghul and Kalh6ro [text-layer?] [Kalhoro] periods, circumstances continually conspired to grind the poor in their poverty" (p. 135, folio 126).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: Moghul times and 1762; book's historical prose): "three kinds of rupees were in circulation in Sind" (p. 70, folio 61); Moghul coins "except possibly the pagoda, were used in Sind" with laris "in common use in Southern Persia, Sind and Western Asia" (p. 133, folio 124).
- (book's word: "Sind", "the Sindhi", "the Sindians"; refers to: "are, and always have been", Moghul and Kalhoro days, Crowe, and the author's day; author's argument, ethnographic present and quoted source, Crowe): "The peasantry of Sind are, and always have been, a happy-go-lucky people fond of laughter and amusement"; the Sindhi's "fondness ... for conversation and listening to songs and stories", and the Id and Holi/Diwali festivals celebrated with "whole-heartedness" (p. 145, folio 136); Crowe: "The Sindians are excessively fond of singing" (p. 146, folio 137); wrestling is "as popular as it is today, when it has claims to being the most popular sport of the country"; folk dances, "of which Sind possessed and still possesses a great variety"; polo "does not appear to have been played in Sind" (pp. 148–149, folios 139–140); "Intoxication with liquor and drugs was undoubtedly a curse of the country" (p. 146, folio 137), "very prevalent everywhere" by Burton's evidence (p. 147, folio 138).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "the time of the British occupation" compared with "the Moghuls and the Kalh5ra [text-layer?] [Kalhora]", "from 1740 onwards"; author's argument): "the system prevailing at the time of the British occupation of Sind did not differ greatly from the system of the Moghuls"; the state share was taken in kind and "taxation as a whole was extraordinarily comprehensive" (pp. 153–154, folios 144–145); "Sind was then approaching a kind of natural and self-contained economy which argued a great .increase in administrative efficiency from 1740 onwar.ds [text-layer?] [onwards]" (p. 155, folio 146); local cesses "were not abolished" under the Kalhora and "under the Talpiirs the collection was even tightened up" (p. 156, folio 147); tax-farming was "One of the great curses of Moghul, Kalh6ro and Talpiir rule alike", the farmers "mostly Hindus" (p. 157, folio 148); a sar shumari poll tax fell on "nc;m-Baluchi [text-layer?] [non-Baluchi] and Jat Muslims" (p. 155, folio 146); this taxation, "backed by the Baliich [text-layer?] [Baluch] soldiery, made the Government far from popular with the common man" and enriched the ruling house (p. 159, folio 150).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "seven­teenth and eighteenth century Sind", "Moghul and Kalhoro days", the Talpurs, and Burnes (1828 visit); author's argument and quoted sources, James and Burnes): "Sind was a Muslim state and in theory justice was administered according to the tenets of the Koran"; "In Moghul and Kalhorn days every man was to some extent the defender of his own rights"; the police "lamentably insufficient" (p. 159, folio 150); collective village responsibility "might very well apply word for word to the Sind of Moghul and Kalh6ro days"; "Sind contained then many expert thieves, and ... equally expert trackers"; crime "comparatively speaking, so little rife in Sind", save cattle stealing and "the wildness of the Baliichi tdbes [text-layer?] [tribes]" (pp. 160–161, folios 151–152); trial by ordeal in "a semi-barbarous land"; "Un�er [text-layer?] [Under] the �alpiirs [text-layer?] [Talpiirs] there were no regular civil courts" (p. 161, folio 152); Burnes: "As the state religion of Scinde is taken from the E:oran [text-layer?] [Koran], so the system of jurisprudence is derived from the same source"; Hindus settle disputes "by panchayats"; "The Baliichis seem generally to take the law into their own hands" (p. 162, folio 153).
- (book's word: "Sind", "eighteenth century Sind"; refers to: Moghul and Kalhora times, 1847, and the author's day; author's argument and quoted source, the Commissioner in Sind): the Kalhora's deference to the priestly class "led to the extraordinary reverence for pirs, Sayids and holy men, which may be said to have been the predominant characteristic of eighteenth century Sind"; "The system of land tenure in Sind, so far as is known, has always been feudal" (p. 163, folio 154); jaghir and inam land was put in 1847 "at one-tenth of the total area of the Government land"; jaghirs "were held mostly by Baliich chiefs", puttadaris "in Upper Sind especially ... mostly by Afghan settlers dating from the time of Nadir Shah, and even earlier" (p. 164, folio 155); of the jaghirdar tribes "The vast majority of these are Baluchi tribes"; the zamindar's representative "was called Mukhadam or Arbab, a title that has not yet died out in Sind" (p. 165, folio 156).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "Moghul and later still truer of Kalh6ro and Talpiir days", with Burnes, Crowe, MacMurdo and Pottinger; author's argument and quoted sources): "The extent to which holy men ... were reverenced and pampered in Sind is 'almost outside the bounds of credibility"; a Persian proverb about Multan "applied -equally to Sind that it consisted of heat, dust, beggars and tombs"; "The social religion of Sind was built up round the reverence of the murid for the murshid" (p. 166, folio 157); Dr Burnes: "There is no country in Asia or rather on earth that is so perfectly priestridden. It is sai4 [text-layer?] [said] to possess no fewer than one hundred thousand tombs of saints"; MacMurdo put ecclesiastical expense "at one-third of the gross revenue" (p. 167, folio 158); Sir Alexander Burnes: "The mendicants in Sind are more numerous than in any other country in Asia" (p. 168, folio 159); the tendency "became more pronounced. under the Kalhora"; yet "The real form and meaning of Islam in Sind is not to be found in these ebullitions ... but is to be read in the beautiful spiritual poems of Shah Abdul Latif" (p. 169, folio 160).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: Moghul, Kalhora and Talpur rule, with Pottinger's nineteenth-century division; author's argument and quoted source, Pottinger): Pottinger's three classes: the privileged ("Amirs, Chiefs, Baliichis, Sayids and religious mendicants"), "the pastoral agricultural and other Mussulman tribes ... constituting the majority of the population", and the Hindus "tyrannized over in . the most infamous and open manner" (p. 170, folio 161); under the Kalhora "the true Sindhis, the Jats and camelmen, the fishermen and hunters, were fully e_xploited [text-layer?] [exploited]"; "The Hindus' position deteriorated still further", yet they "continued to live in Sind advancing money to the zamind.ar class, financing trade and commerce, farming the revenue, and owning all the petty shops in the villages"; "A quasi-theocratic state" could have taken no other form (p. 171, folio 162).
- (book's word: "Sind", "the Sindhi"; refers to: "seventeenth and eighteenth century Sind", "the mid-eighteenth century", and "the middle age of Islam"; author's argument): "In seventeenth and eighteenth century Sind a great gulf yawned between Islam's ideal and the practical working of Islamic . belief"; "Sind was a Muslim state which struggled to semi-independence in the mid-eighteenth century ... amongst a population composed for the most part of converted Jats ... The majority of the population were followers of the Sunnat. But very influential sections held Shia tenets" (p. 172, folio 163); "The Sindhi is by nature an easy-going being, not given to religious subtlety"; the Sayids "were nearly all Shias"; "The extent to which Sind attracted contemplative and religious-minded persons during the middle age of Islam is well known and is the foundation of its school of Sufi thought" (p. 173, folio 164).
- (book's word: "Sind", "Upper and Lower Sind"; refers to: "the eighth century" to "about A.D.1461"; book's historical prose, citing Ray, Elliot and the Ain): "The indigenous population of the Lower Indus valley in the eighth century con­sisted of Hindu races speaking a Sanskritic language"; "Towards the end of the tenth century Islam in Sind appears to have been influenced by a wave of Qarma­tian heresy" (Ray, quoted); "Most of the population was converted to Islam in the fourteenth and fifteenth centuries"; the Sumra regime ran "from circa ro25 [text-layer?] [1025] to circa 1360", with confusion "as to the authority wielded over Upper and Lower Sind, which have had very different histories" (p. 174, folio 165); under Jam Nizamuddin "about A.D.1461, Islam spread widely over Si.nd [text-layer?] [Sind] an d [text-layer?] [and] the mass of the common people became Muslim" (p. 175, folio 166).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: from the mass conversions "right up to the time of Burton", and the author's day; author's argument): the conversions broke down "the clear-cut distinction between Sunni and Shia" and left Islam among the common people "to a great extent influenced by Hindu pre­dilections"; "Hindus visiting Mussulman shrines and making votive offerings" and "the general easy-going character of Sind orthodoxy" (p. 175, folio 166); "The countryside became in this way the home of an immense number of these petty imperia" of pirs (p. 176, folio 167); of superstitious practices, "Examples of all could be cited from Sind"; the cult of Khwajo Khizr was "linked up with the worship of the river Indus and supported by Muslim and Hindu alike" (p. 177, folio 168), Khizr "called in Sind Zinda Pir"; "The debasement of Islam ... seems undoubtedly to have been due to the popular religion of the indigenous Sindhis, sometimes called Jats. The jats constituted. the majority of the population" (p. 178, folio 169).
- (book's word: "Sind", "Sindh", "Upper Sind"; refers to: "1592" to "1662", Mughal period, as the column dates it; chronological appendix, "Events in Sind History"): "1592 Defeat of Mirza Jani Beg Trirkhan [text-layer?] [Turkhan, as the same table prints "Turkhans" for 1555] and annexation of Sincl [text-layer?] [Sind] by Akbar"; "1605 Rise of the Daiidpota [text-layer?] [Daudpota] in Shikarpiir [text-layer?] [Shikarpur] and Upper Sind"; "1648-5 1 [text-layer?] [1648-51] A.urangzeb [text-layer?] [Aurangzeb] Governor of Multan and Sind"; "1658 Civil War between Aurang­ ze� [text-layer?] [Aurangzeb] and Dara Shikoh �nding [text-layer?] [ending] in defeat of Dara Shikoh and his death after pursuit through Si_nd [text-layer?] [Sind]"; "KalhQ_ra [text-layer?] [Kalhora] b�gin [text-layer?] [begin] to oppose the Moghul troops" (p. 200, folio 191).
- (book's word: "Sind", "Upper Sind", "Upper Sindh"; refers to: "1 70 1" to "1803" as printed (split numbers [text-layer?]), Kalhora and Talpur periods; chronological appendix, "Events in Sind History"): "Yar Muhammad Kalhoro obtains grant from Aurangzeb. Defeat of the Daiidpota [text-layer?] [Daudpota] in Upper Sind" (1701); "Yar Muhammad Kalhoro extends power in Upper Sindh" ("17II" [text-layer?] [1711]); "Niir [text-layer?] [Nur] Muhammad Kalhoro becomes Sii b edar [text-layer?] [Subedar] of Sind" (no year on the line; it stands opposite 1739); "1 740 Nadir Shah invades Sind which becomes subject to Persia"; "1 750 Sind becomes subject to Afghan Kingdom of Ahmad Shah Dtirani [text-layer?] [Durani]"; "1 754 Ahmad Shah Durani invades Sind : flight and death of Niir [text-layer?] [Nur] Muhammad Kalhoro"; "1 757-8 Commotion and civil war in Sind ending in ascendancy of Ghulam Shah Kalhoro" (p. 201, folio 192); "1 775 Misgovernment in Sind"; "1 776 Sarfaraz Khan Kalhoro deposed for misgovernment"; "1 778 Ghulam Nabi Khan Kalhorn [text-layer?] [Kalhoro] becomes ruler of Sind"; "1 778 Civil war between Kalhora and Talpiirs [text-layer?] [Talpurs] begins"; "1803 Ascendancy of Talpiirs [text-layer?] [Talpurs] finally established" (p. 202, folio 193).
- (book's word: "Sind", "the Sind trade", "the Sind Factory"; refers to: "1636" to "1775" as the appendices date it; chronological appendix and book's historical prose on the Bombay records): the English Company's presence in Sind: "1636 English �actory [text-layer?] [factory] established at Tatta"; "1662 English Factory in Sind given up" (p. 200, folio 191); "1 758 English Company's Factory established at Tatta"; "1 775 … English factory at Tatta given up" (p. 202, folio 193). Appendix VII: the press lists show "an enormous gap in the references to the tr.ade [text-layer?] [trade] of the Company in Sind"; after Nicholas Scrivener's letter of "13 October 1657" there is "a complete blank till 25 November 1744"; "references to Sind become more numerous but are not common till 1758, when the Sind Factory was re-established"; there is "a regular record of the Sind Factory from August 1762 to July 1764"; a list of covenanted servants covering "1712 to 1738" makes "no mention of any servant residing in Sind" (p. 204, folio 195). Appendix II lists the vessels "in the Sind trade about 1758-60" (p. 196, folio 187).

## From Annemarie Schimmel, Pain and Grace

*Integrated 10 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/schimmel_pain_and_grace.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

**Named exactly once in the body of the book, and once in the index.** **No death year, no silsila, no
shrine at Sehwan and no observance are given** — all **(not stated)**.

- **Saint** — **He is the starting point of Sind's mystical history, with a place and a century but no
  dates.** "From the day that Lal Shahbaz Qalandar settled in Sehwan in the 13th century … Sind had
  produced an immense number of mystics, whose shrines are scattered all through the Indus Valley, and
  who invented short songs in their mother tongue to express the mysteries of love and union" (p. 25,
  folio 21). **The shrines of Sind's mystics are referred to only collectively, and none is named.**
- **Apparatus** — **"Lal Shahbaz Qalandar 21"** (p. 305, folio 305) — one page figure only. The place
  headword **"Sehwan 21, 161"** (p. 308, folio 308) shares p. 21, **but the index states no link
  between them and carries no cross-reference either way** — **(not stated)**.

### Doubts the notes raise on this row

- **Three of those lines rest on a nisba and nothing more, which is the thinnest evidence any of
  these lines carries**: Sehwan (from "Qazi Qadan of Sehwan", p. 161, folio 161), Rohri (from "Bedil
  of Rohri", p. 202, folio 202) and Bulri (from "Shah Abdul Karim of Bulrri"[OCR?], pp. 29, 161).
  **Flagged so a human can strike them if a nisba is held not to be place-level material.**

### Province-level material: sindh

*The book's `sindh` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Sind", "this province"; refers to: "711" onward, as printed; book's historical prose): "the Arabs conquered Sind, the southern part of present day Pakistan, in 711, and this province never ceased to be a center of Islamic studies and a seat of Islamic mysticism" (p. 6, folio 2).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: period not stated ("has always been praised"); author's argument, then quoted source, "A Sindhi author", footnoted to J. Parsram, 'Sind and its Sufis', p. 78 f.): "Sind has always been praised as the country with a particularly strong inclination towards mysticism"; the quoted author: "Sind has been flattened and has been in a state of negation. This is the cause of the many disadvantages from which it suffers; but it has been also the cause of a great blessing ... something of which India is in need" — "namely, of the mystical tradition" (pp. 24–25, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sind", "the Indus Valley"; refers to: "From the day that Lal Shahbaz Qalandar settled in Sehwan in the 13th century" onward; book's historical prose): from then and from Baha'uddin Zakariya's Suhrawardi center "at the northernmost border of the country, in Multan, Sind had produced an immense number of mystics, whose shrines are scattered all through the Indus Valley, and who invented short songs in their mother tongue to express the mysteries of love and union" (p. 25, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "Ahmad Shah Abdali's invasion, which took place during Shah Abdul Latif's life time"; quoted source, Divan Gidumal, "the minister of the Kalhoro princes", footnoted "Id. p. 54" [Parsram]): Gidumal offered the invader two bags which "contain the most valuable of Sind's gifts; they contain the holy dust from the tombs of numerous saints and Pirs of Sind" (p. 25, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sind", "the Lower Indus Valley", "Upper Sind"; refers to: "since 1595", then "1717" to "January 1718" — as printed; book's historical prose): "Sind was, since 1595, part of the Mughal Empire, and although representatives of the Delhi court came there, the indigenous rulers enjoyed much freedom. The first obvious sign of the struggle for power in the Lower Indus Valley becomes visible in 1717"; "The Kalhora clan from Upper Sind, particularly active in the struggle against Inayat, soon afterwards became independent in Sind"; the army against Jhok "came from various places in Sind and Hind" (p. 25, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Upper Sind"; refers to: "1781" — as printed; book's historical prose): most of Abdur Rahim Girhori's books "were, however, destroyed during the Afghan raids in Upper Sind in 1781" (p. 28, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "18th century Sind", "Sind"; refers to: "18th century", the "14th and 15th centuries", and "still in the 1920's" — as printed; author's argument with quoted sources, H. T. Sorley and J. Parsram, 'l.c.' pp. 75, 84): it would be wrong to regard Girhori "as a typical exponent of Sindhi Sufism. On the whole, there was 'a common basis of understanding, by Muslim and Hindu alike, of the mystical message of Islam,' as H. T. Sorley says"; Sorley's analysis of "the complex religious situation in 18th century Sind" contains "the 'almost incredible veneration for sayyids as such and a great accretion of superstitious practices in the belief of the multitude'"; the understanding between the communities "may perhaps have been facilitated by the activities of the Ismaili missionaries who came to Sind in the 14th and 15th centuries"; "'neither Hindus nor Muslims are orthodox in Sind'" and "still in the 1920's, there were 'numerous Hindus and amongst them some of the best brains of Sind, old and new, who are Sufi by religion'"; "a number of Hindus became devoted disciples of Muslim saints" (p. 28, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sind", "Upper and later in Lower Sind"; refers to: the Kalhoro rise after "Aurangzeb's death", Nadir Shah "1739", Adam Shah Kalhoro executed "1651", the Talpurs "in 1781", the British "in 1843" — as printed; book's historical prose): "the native Kalhoro dynasty took over the rule in Upper and later in Lower Sind, only to become the vassals of the invading Nadir Shah, who first conquered Sind and the Panjab before he reached Delhi in 1739"; Abdali's influence "on the scene in Sind is not as important as it was in the northern areas of present day Pakistan"; the Kalhoros were overthrown by "the former disciples of their saintly ancestor, Adam Shah Kalhoro"; "The Talpurs, who thus came to power in 1781, ruled the country until it was conquered by the British in 1843"; "in Sind, too, the 18th century can be called the most important period for the formation of mystical poetry and prose" (pp. 30–31, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sind" / "rural provinces"; refers to: Shah Abdul Latif's lifetime and "later" Sindhi Sufi poets, period otherwise not stated; author's argument): "Many later Sindhi Sufi poets, mainly those of Baloch origin, used also the northern dialect of Sindhi, Siraiki", and the imagery of the linguistic areas is "very closely related, as is to be expected from the inhabitants of predominatly [OCR?] [predominantly] rural provinces" (p. 154, folio 154).
- (book's word: "both Sind and the Panjab"; refers to: period not stated; author's argument): "Sohni is one of the favorite folk tales in both Sind and the Panjab" (p. 156, folio 156).
- (book's word: "14th century Sind", "Lower Sind"; refers to: the "14th century" and "the 15th century" — as printed; book's historical prose): the Lila Chanesar story "can be dated back to the time of Jam Chanesar, one of the Summa rulers in 14th century Sind"; the Mumal Rano tale "originated in Lower Sind some time in the 15th century" (p. 157, folio 157).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: period not stated; quoted source, E. Trumpp's introduction to Sur Sorathi): "how much even the strong monotheistic deism of Islam has become modified and corrupted in Sind, as in other parts of India, by its contact with Hinduism" (p. 158, folio 158).
- (book's word: "the poetry of Sind and the Panjab", "Sind"; refers to: period not stated; author's argument): the spinning of cotton is "one of the most important occupations of women" and a frequent topic of the region's poetry; "the crow, kang, is the typical messenger bird in Sind, as was the pigeon in high Persian poetry" (p. 160, folio 160).
- (book's word: "Sind", "the country"; refers to: "had, and still has" for the folk poetry, and "shortly after 1530" for the first known Sindhi verses — as printed; author's argument): "Sind, as other countries, had, and still has, a big treasure of folk poetry, ballads, and working songs; folk tales are located in the different parts of the country, be it the hill slopes of Balochistan, the desert region of Thar Parkar, the fertile plains that were watered by the Indus, or the shifting creeks near the Indus mouth. It seems that in Sind, as in other parts of the Muslim world, the Sufis are responsible for the development of the spoken language into a literary one" (p. 161, folio 161).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: period not stated; author's argument): "In Sind there were many pious who, by their recitation of the Mathnawi, 'kept the marketplace of union warm', and Rumi's work was quoted by stern Naqshbandis as much as by members of the more emotional orders" (p. 165, folio 165).
- (book's word: "Sind", "the Sind countryside", "Sindhi shrines"; refers to: "the early 18th century" for Latif's picture, and "still" / "still in our day" for the beds and shrine trees; author's argument and ethnographic present): "The imagery of the swan must have been popular in Sind for a long time"; Latif "offers in his verse a vivid picture of the conditions of the Sind countryside during the early 18th century"; Lila's swinging bed — "these colorful, laquer-work beds are still a specialty of Sind"; the merchant's wife binds cloth on sacred trees (Sam. V 2): "still in our day the trees near Sindhi shrines are decorated with many pieces of cloth, preferably red, in the shape of little flags or tiny cradles, which are attached there for the sake of a vow, as it is usual almost everywhere in the Islamic world" (p. 168, folio 168).
- (book's word: "the western border of Sind"; refers to: present day of writing; author's anecdote): Sassui's run through the desert at sunset (Hus. I 8-11) is "a scene which can be appreciated by those who know the glowing evenings at the western border of Sind, when all of a sudden, darkness overtakes the wayfarer, and nothing is left but the sounds of a few animals" (p. 170, folio 170).
- (book's word: "the Sindhis"; refers to: period not stated, present tense; author's argument): the letter-writing topos "is general among the Sindhis, so that most of the girls who never learned how a pen looks or knew even a single letter always complain that they do not receive letters from their friends, or want to write toward their families. This topic occurs also often in wedding songs" (p. 172, folio 172).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "traditionally", period not stated; author's argument): "The Mohana traditionally belonged to the lowest social strata in Sind, and their women were noted for beauty and not too strict morals" (p. 175, folio 175).
- (book's word: "the inhabitants of Sind"; refers to: Shah Abdul Latif's *Risalo*, period not stated; author's argument): in the *Risalo* "None of the great tarigas [OCR?] [tariqas] is mentioned, nor any of the sanctuaries to which the inhabitants of Sind flocked at many occasions" (p. 190, folio 190).
- (book's word: "Sind", "the last indigenous Sindhi rulers"; refers to: "from 1520 onward" — as printed; book's historical prose): the Turkish word *otaq* [printed "6tdaq" [OCR?]] "points to the Turkish influence that was prevalent in Sind from 1520 onward, when the Turkish dynasty of the Arghun, descending from Afghanistan, overcame the last indigenous Sindhi rulers; they themselves were followed by the Turkish family of the Tarkhan under whom Sind fell to the Mughal Empire" (p. 207, folio 207).
- (book's word: "18th century Sind"; refers to: the 18th century, as printed; author's argument): an Arabic saying "which was very popular in 18th century Sind: al-bala' lil-wala? [OCR?] ka l-lahab lt? [OCR?] dh-dhahab, 'Affliction is for saintliness the same as are flames for the gold'" (p. 222, folio 222).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: the poet's lament, period not stated; translation of the poet's verse, Mar. Mut. 30): "Marui ts [OCR?] [is] no more in Malir, / and there ts [OCR?] [is] no Hamir— / Punhun is no longer in Kech, / nor ts [OCR?] [is] in Sind an Amir; ... There ts [OCR?] [is] in the convents no Pir" (p. 235, folio 235).
- (book's word: "Eastern Sind", "dear Sind"; refers to: Sur Sarang, period not stated; author's argument and translation of the poet's verse): the cloud of mercy, "beginning from the desert area in Eastern Sind, the Thar Parkar", is seen floating "from Africa to China", and the poet, "coming closer and closer to his home region", prays: "My Lord, be kind over dear Sind, / Friend! Sweet darling! You may make the whole world happy!" (p. 259, folio 259).
- (book's word: "the villages in Sind", "native provinces"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; author's argument, the evocative opening of "To Sum Up"): "the villages in Sind are covered with a veil of golden dust; the walls, built of unbaked clay, look like bronze, and the blue and white tiles which cover a lonely saint's tomb match the purity of the blue sky"; Dard's and Latif's approaches are "as different as are the sunsets in their native provinces" (p. 263, folio 263).
- (book's word: "his successors in Sind"; refers to: Shah Abdul Latif's lifetime and after; author's argument): Latif, "as did his Panjabi contemporaries and his successors in Sind, accepted the Hindus who were striving on the way to perfection as true pilgrims in the eternal Light" (p. 267, folio 267).

## From Waris Shah, Hir and Ranjha (Usborne tr.)

*Integrated 10 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/waris_shah_hir_ranjha.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

The book's forms are **"Shahbaz Qalandar"** (p. 10, folio 9), **"Lal Shahbaz"** (p. 11, folio 10;
p. 50, folio 49), **"Lal Shahbaz Qalandar"** (p. 11, folio 10, note 2) and **"Shahbaz Qalandar of
Sind"** (p. 16, folio 15, note 10).

- **Saint**
  - **The book places him at Uch, and the archive row places him at Sehwan Sharif. Read the Doubts
    entry before using any of this.** The verse: "Then **Shahbaz Qalandar, the holy saint of Uch,2**"
    (p. 10, folio 9).
  - *The annotator gives Sind, with no town.* "**Lal Shahbaz Qalandar is buried in Sind.**" (p. 11,
    folio 10, note 2); "Shahbaz Qalandar of Sind" (p. 16, folio 15, note 10). **Sehwan is never
    named**, and no death date, order, ʿurs or custodian is given — the row's d. 1274 and Qalandari
    are (not stated).
- **Legends & Miracles**
  - *One of the Five Pirs; he gives Ranjha a ring, and the ring is later taken up as a relic.* "**Lal
    Shahbaz a ring**" (p. 11, folio 10); "He kissed the handkerchief of Shakarganj and **took the ring
    of Lal Shahbaz**" (p. 50, folio 49).
- **Significance**
  - Listed among the Five Pirs' "spiritual celebrities" (p. 16, folio 15, note 10).
  - *Place-level.* The book's "Uch" attaches him to the archive's five Uch Sharif rows under ruling
    (a) — but **no Uch Sharif row names Shahbaz Qalandar as its principal figure**, which is precisely
    the contradiction recorded under Doubts.

### Doubts the notes raise on this row

- **`lal-shahbaz-qalandar`: the book puts him at Uch, the archive at Sehwan Sharif.** "Shahbaz
  Qalandar, **the holy saint of Uch**,2" (p. 10, folio 9), against the annotator's own "**Lal Shahbaz
  Qalandar is buried in Sind.**" (p. 11, folio 10) and "Shahbaz Qalandar **of Sind**" (p. 16,
  folio 15) — so the book contradicts itself as well as the row. **Sehwan is never named.** None of
  the five Uch Sharif rows names Shahbaz Qalandar as its principal figure, so the name matches only
  the Sehwan row while the place matches none of it. **A human must resolve this before any bullet
  enters the row.**

- **`shrine-of-fariduddin-ganjshakar`, `shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya`, `lal-shahbaz-qalandar`: no
  death year for any of them.** The rows carry 1266, 1267 and 1274; the book gives no date for any
  saint it names. **There is no death year, no AH date and no era date anywhere in the book for any
  archive figure** — the only dates in the whole volume are 1739, 1748, 1766, `i 768`, `i1761`, 1809,
  "1180 Hijri", "the year 120 of the Bikramajit era", the damaged Karbala pair `6' AN.` / `68o`, and
  the reign lengths "28 years" and "700 years".

### Province-level material: sindh

*The book's `sindh` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: the usage of "The Arab historians", period not stated; quoted source — the Arab historians and the Chach Nameh, as reported in an unattributed note — the book itself marks a differently bounded unit): "The Arab historians speak of Hind and Sind separately. Sind, In their description, is bounded by Multan, Jaisalmere and Gujrat and Hind, or Hindustan, begins where Sind ends. The Arabic title of the famous Chach Nameh one of the earliest histories of Sind, is The History of Hind and Sind (Tarikh-ul-Hind was [text-layer?] [reading uncertain] Sind)." (p. 55, folio 54, note 1). The note apparently glosses Ranjha's blessing on the Raja, "may you rule over horses, camels, elephants, batteries, Hindustan’ and Sind.’" (p. 54, folio 53); the marker match is not confirmed. This boundary is the book's (its Arab historians'), not the modern province's: weigh accordingly.
- (book's word: "old Sind", "Sind"; refers to: period not stated; annotator's note, historical/literary): "Sassi-Punnoo is a story of old Sind." — Sassi "belonged to Bhambore. an old city", Punnoo "was a Baluch chief"; the Sohni and Sassi romances, printed "SoJtni-Mahiwal [text-layer?] [Sohni-Mahiwal] and Sossi-Funoo [text-layer?] [Sassi-Punnoo]", have "travelled beyond their original setting, being popular both in the old Punjab and Sind." (p. 12, folio 11, note 5).

## From Richard M. Eaton, Essays on Islam and Indian History

*Integrated 10 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/eaton_essays_islam_indian_history.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

### Province-level material: sindh

*The book's `sindh` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: 711; book's historical prose): "Muslim navies sailed to the coasts of western India where in 711 they conquered and occupied the densely-populated Hindu—Buddhist society of Sind. Thus began the long and eventful encounter between Islamic and Indic civilizatiens [text-layer?] [civilizations]"; the next sentence is damaged: "While Arab rule in Sind was being consis [text-layer?] [word cut off] other Arab armies continued the overland drive eastward" (p. 30, folio 18).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: 711, with crops carried west "Between the eighth and tenth centuries"; reported view of Andrew Watson): "A key event in Watson's analysis was the Arab conquest of Sind in 711, which established a direct and regular contact between India and the Fertile Crescent"; it "threw open western Asia, Africa, and Europe to the agricultural treasures of India, effectively incorporating all these regions for the first time into a single agricultural universe" (p. 39, folio 27).
- (book's word: "eighth century Sind"; refers to: the eighth century; book's historical prose): "Arab rule in eighth century Sind having weakened and died, it was left to Persianized Turks to establish a permanent Muslim presence in India from the thirteenth century" (p. 47, folio 35).
- (book's word: "Punjab and Sind"; refers to: "Beginning in the fourteenth century and continuing through the Mughal period (1526-1858)"; author's argument): Sind is one of the western "wings of India that became Muslim-majority regions", where "the growth of Muslim societies correlated with the adoption of sedentary agriculture", a "frontier" society not yet deeply penetrated by Brahmanical values, and where "shrines of great Sufis attracted and integrated pre-agrarian and non-Muslim pastoral clans" (p. 48, folio 36). The same bullet stands under punjab.
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "between the seventh and eleventh centuries", Hsuan Tsang in the seventh century, the Chach-nama in the eighth; book's historical prose, citing Irfan Habib): the Jat pastoralists "had been moving up from Sind into the Multan area between the seventh and eleventh centuries"; Hsuan Tsang "wrote of river groups in Sind who 'gave themselves exclusively to tending cattle and from this derive their livelihood,' 'have no masters,'" with "'an unfeeling temper'" and "'a hasty disposition'"; the Chach-nama "styled these groups 'Jatts', located them in the wastes of the Indus valley in Sind, and noted an absence among them of social hierarchy" (p. 224, folio 212); the essay sums this up as the Jats' "migration north from Sind to Punjab" (p. 227, folio 215).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "the eighth century"; book's historical prose, citing Y. Friedmann): "there is evidence that in the eighth century, when Arabs replaced Brahmans as rulers of Sind, the new rulers merely continued the earlier practice of requiring the Jats to associate themselves with dogs—unclean to Muslims as well as to Hindus—and in this way to affirm their lowly status" (p. 224, folio 212).

## From Saiyid Athar Abbas Rizvi, A History of Sufism in India, vol. 1

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/rizvi_history_of_sufism_india_1.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

**Four bullets, all from chunk 019 plus one index line, and all in one paragraph of the book.**

- **Saint**
  - **Personal name and native place.** "Lal Shahbaz's real name was Mir Saiyid 'Usman and he was a
    native of Sehwan in the Sind" (p. 328, folio 306). **The book writes "Mir Saiyid ʿUsman", NOT "Usman
    Marwandi"; the nisba "Marwandi" appears nowhere, and no death date is given anywhere — the archive
    row's 1274 is not in this book.** See Doubts.
  - **Initiation, as an exception to a rule.** "Another exception was Shaikh Lal Shahbaz Qalandar… Shaikh
    Baha'u'd-Din disliked qalandars intensely and certainly never encouraged their unwelcome visits. Lal
    Shahbaz was an exception and was adopted as his disciple." (p. 328, folio 306). He is introduced
    inside a run of qalandars "Among the Suhrawardiyyas" (p. 328, folio 306). **The row's `silsila` is
    Qalandari; the book gives him a Suhrawardi initiation and no silsila name of his own.**
  - **The two halves of the name, each given an origin, one a conferred title.** "Often he wore a red
    dress, hence his name lal (red). Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya gave him the title 'Shahbaz,' the
    noblest species of falcon." (p. 328, folio 306).
- **Practices** — "Following the Shari'a held no interest for Lal Shahbaz who like the malamatis, indulged
  in ecstatic dancing." (p. 328, folio 306).
- **Shrine / Darbar and Legends** — **the tomb is named as a working miracle-site, in the present tense.**
  "A large number of miracles are said to have been performed by him and even his tomb in Sind is known
  for innumerable miraculous feats." (p. 328, folio 306), sourced only as "Ma'ariju'l-Wilayat, f. 542b".
  **The book locates the tomb as "in Sind" and does NOT name Sehwan as the tomb's place — Sehwan is given
  only as his birthplace. No death date, no ʿurs, no shrine building, no custodian — all (not stated).**
- **Index** — "Lal Shahbaz Qalandar, Shaikh, 306" (p. 473, folio 449): **a single folio, no dates, no
  place, no order.** The general headword "Qalandar, 1, 5, 14, 16, 142-43, 145, 150, 193, 204, 214, 216,
  270, 271, 278, 287, 301-21, 354, 356, 389" (p. 480, folio 456) supplies the run folio 306 sits in, and
  "Rasuli Qalandars, 306" (p. 482, folio 458) and "Haidaris, 306-11, 410" (p. 467, folio 443) share it.
  The place-name is indexed separately: "Sehwan, 153, 277, 306" and "Siwistan, 153, 277" (pp. 484, 486;
  folios 460, 462) — **converted to this row and to `shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar` under ruling (a); see
  the place-level run below.**

### Doubts the notes raise on this row

| row | the book prints | the archive row | status |
|---|---|---|---|
| `lal-shahbaz-qalandar` | **no death date anywhere**, and the name is "Mir Saiyid ʿUsman", not "Usman Marwandi" (p. 328, folio 306) | `figure_died` **1274**, figure "Usman Marwandi" | **Neither the year nor the nisba may be taken from this book.** |

### Place-level material: SEHWAN SHARIF

*The book's place-level material on "SEHWAN SHARIF" (2 rows), from its list of rows attached at place or person level, copied into every existing entry that list names (ruling (a); Rauf, 10 October 2026). It is about the place in general, not about this shrine alone.*

- **lal-shahbaz-qalandar, shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar** — **SEHWAN SHARIF, the archive's 2 rows.** "Siwistan (Sehwan) in Sind" as a posting for Shaikh ʿArif (p. 172, folio 154); and, with an institution and an endowment attached to somebody else, "Sultan Muhammad bin Tughluq appointed him the Shaikhu'l-Islam and made him the head of a khangah [khanqah] in Siwistan (Sehwan) known as the Khanqah-i Muhammadi to which several villages were attached" (p. 299, folio 277). **That khanqah is Makhdum-i Jahaniyan's, not Lal Shahbaz's or Lakhi Shah Saddar's, and must never be read as either.** Index: "Sehwan, 153, 277, 306" and "Siwistan, 153, 277" (pp. 484, 486; folios 460, 462).

### Province-level material: sindh

*The book's `sindh` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "the time of the Umayyad Caliph al-Walid I (705-15)", with the conquest "between 711 and 712", as printed; book's historical prose): Rizvi calls it "a myth" that the conquest of Sind began as a punitive expedition against "the ruling Raja Dahir": "In reality, the conquest of Transoxiana and Sind was initiated by Hajjaj, Walid's governor in Iraq, and was one aspect of his expansionist policy. In 710 Muhammad bin Qasim al-Saqafi began his advance through Makran and Baluchistan with the conquest of Sind between 711 and 712" (p. 127, folio 109).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: the early Arab conquest and "the eighth century", as printed; book's historical prose): the spread of sufism seen in Transoxiana and "Khurasen" [OCR?] [Khurasan] is not matched here: "no such developments in Sind are recorded"; "Early Arab conquerors settled their families in large numbers in the various towns of Sind"; Brahmans in high office converted "to retain their positions", and Buddhists "extremely hostile to Brahman domination" converted too; "Hindu and Buddhist scholars from Sind were sent to the 'Abbasid court", and "In the eighth century Abu Ma''shar¢ [OCR?] Najih (d. 170/787) a scholar of Hadis, and the poet Abu'l-'Ata> [OCR?] (died after 158/774) were leading literary figures from Sind" (p. 127, folio 109).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "the tenth century", the Fatimid Caliphate "from 909 to 1171", and "Between 977 and 985"; book's historical prose): "Lack of information about sufis in Sind may be ascribed to two factors": land routes "between Khurasan, Transoxiana and Sind were slow and arduous", and the rise of sufism coincided with the Isma'ili Fatimid Caliphate; "Mansura, the capital of Sind, ruled by the Sumira dynasty had also accepted the over-lordship of the Egyptian Caliphs", so sufism "was naturally cut, from close relations with other areas" (p. 128, folio 110).
- (book's word: "the Lower Sind", "the Upper Sind"; refers to: "until the middle of the fourteenth century" and "After the end of the twelfth century", as printed; book's historical prose): "The Sumira dynasty continued to rule the Lower Sind until the middle of the fourteenth century. After the end of the twelfth century, the Upper Sind came under the domination of Sunni rule" (p. 128, folio 110).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "seven years" after "November 1221", and "the annexation of Multan and Sind by Iltutmish in 1228", as printed; book's historical prose): Iltutmish ("Itutmish" [OCR?] at p. 152), "seven years later routing from Sind his rival, Nasiru'd-Din Qubacha" (p. 152, folio 134); Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya "openly sided with Sultan Shamsu'd-Din Iltutmish when he wished to add Multan and Sind to the Delhi Sultanate" (p. 209, folio printed "19]" [OCR?] [191]); "After the annexation of Multan and Sind by Iltutmish in 1228, relations between the Sultan of Delhi and the Shaikh became more intimate" (p. 210, folio 192).
- (book's word: "Sind", "the lower Sind"; refers to: the time of Makhdum-i Jahaniyan and Sultan Firuz, with the Thatta campaign "In 763/1362"; book's historical prose, reporting belief): the Saiyid's "assistance was also sought by the governors of Sind"; "It is believed that the Sumirahs of the lower Sind who were Isma'ilis, adopted Sunnism under the influence of the Saiyid. A Jat chief of Sind, later named 'Abdu'llah, is also said to have embraced Islam through a supernatural feat performed by Makhdum Jahaniyan" (p. 301, folio 279).

## From Omar Kasmani, Queer Companions

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/kasmani_queer_companions.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

The book's whole subject, present in all twelve chunks. The author's running form is **Lal**; the
fuller forms are `Laʿl Shahbaz Qalandar` (p. 20, folio 3), `Lal Shahbaz Qalandar` (p. 35, folio 18),
and, in the Library of Congress data on the copyright page, both `Qalandar Lal Shahbaz` and
`Lal Shahbaz Qalander` — with `-er` — on the same page (p. 5, folio not stated). The References
print the name four further ways: `Laʿl Shahbaz Qalandar` (Boivin 2003, p. 204, folio 187),
`La'l Shahbâz Qalandar` with a straight apostrophe and a circumflex (Boivin 2012b, p. 204), and
`Qalandar Lʿal Shahbaz`, with the ʿayn displaced into "Lal" (Andhar 2010, p. 203, folio 186). The
index carries `Hazrat Usman Marwandi. See Lal` (p. 221, folio 204), `Sayyid Usman Marwandi. See Lal`
(p. 223, folio 206) and `Laʿl Shahbaz Qalandar. See Lal` (p. 222, folio 205).

- **Saint**
  - **Death year — stated once, in the author's own voice, and nowhere else in the book.** "Laʿl
    Shahbaz Qalandar (d. 1274 ce, henceforth Lal) is a thirteenth-century mystic known for his
    radical rejection of Islamic religious institutions, norms, and practices." (p. 20, folio 3). The
    era is printed lowercase `ce` throughout. **Every other passage gives only a century** — "a
    thirteenth-century qalandar" (p. 35, folio 18), "an original thirteenth-century qalandar" (p. 62,
    folio 45), "a wandering mystic in the thirteenth century" (p. 164, folio 147), "a
    thirteenth-century antinomian mystic whose tomb-shrine is located in Sehwan" (p. 176, folio 159),
    "coming close to a thirteenth-century saintly figure" (p. 172, folio 155). Chunks covering
    pp. 50–158 and the whole index give **no death date at all: (not stated)**.
  - **A birth year the author never states.** The CIP heading prints "Qalandar Lal Shahbaz,
    1177–1274—Shrines. | Shrine of Lal Shahbaz Qalander (Sehwan, Pakistan)" (p. 5, folio not stated).
    **1177 is the Library of Congress heading's, never Kasmani's.** The index prints no dates of any
    kind for him (pp. 221–222, folios 204–205).
  - **His name is contested, and the book says so twice.** "his followers cannot quite agree on his
    doctrinal persuasion, Sufi affiliations, not even his actual name, Sayyid Usman Marwandi. The
    saint is better known by the three-part epithet Lal Shahbaz Qalandar." (p. 35, folio 18)
    "Custodian families in Sehwan take the position that the saint's actual name was most likely Shah
    Hasan and not Usman, the latter commonly associated with the Sunni. The title Lal Shahbaz as
