    Sehwan, the name of the Sufi lodge." (p. 219, folio 206) **The glossary carries no entry for
    `khanqah`/`khanaqah` under any spelling: (not stated).**
  - **Mulchand Kafi as a working Hindu institution**: "Although Hindus are very few in number in Sehwan
    today, it should be noted that Mulchand Kafi works to a certain extent as a place of community
    worship." (p. 180, folio 167) **No number of Hindus is given: (not stated).** "other built
    structures can be found here. One of them is used to store different artefacts that are paraded
    during Lal Shahbaz's mehndi procession." (p. 125, folio 112) "although it does not play a ritual
    role for the Hindu community, although it is used for cremation" (p. 193, folio 180). "Concerning
    the cult of Lal Shahbaz proper, although it is true that the kafi is open to the public mainly on
    the occasion of the urs, certain ceremonies of the cycle of life are always realized there."
    (p. 180, folio 167)
  - **A disciple's dargah raised beside the master's**, of Lal Das: "He himself had disciples who raised
    a small dargah not far from his Master's. His devotees regularly receive his blessing, whether Hindu
    or Muslim." (p. 181, folio 168) **No building date, no dimensions, no custodian named for that small
    dargah: (not stated).**
  - **The shrine replicated in a Hindu village shrine in Sindh — the book's most unexpected shrine
    fact**: "Indeed, the sanctum sanctorum was but a replica of the darbar of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar in
    Sehwan Sharif, though settled in a different religious environment." (p. 184, folio 171) "the Hindu
    dargah of Tando Ahmad Khan **hosts Lal Shahbaz Qalandar's tomb**, surrounded by Hindu temples, also
    Sufi astanas, and with many inscriptions referring to an array of religious traditions, including
    the Nanakpanth." (p. 184, folio 171) **The book does not say the tomb is a cenotaph or a
    relic-grave, only that the dargah "hosts" it: (not stated).** The site is read as a graded
    pilgrimage: "It can be framed as a spiritual pilgrimage from Shiva to Lal Shahbaz, who is finally
    merged through the space's agency in Jhulelal." (p. 186, folio 173)
  - **His shrine represented on an Indian altar, as a hinge between two levels**: in the darbar of
    Rochaldas "The transition from the first level to the second level is carried out by a representation
    of the darbar of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar." (p. 133, folio 120)
  - **His presence transplanted to Ulhasnagar as a pole and a red banner**: "The darbar is not decorated
    with any tower or dome, maybe except one considered the pole of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar, which is high
    and thus can be seen from far away" (p. 80, folio 67); the alam is "a small square courtyard,
    enclosed by four walls, at the top of which floats the red banner of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar" (p. 81,
    folio 68). Figure 2.3 is captioned "Lal Shahbaz Qalandar's alam in Rochaldas's darbar." (p. 81,
    folio 68; listed at p. 10, folio not stated as `2.3 Lal Shahbaz Qalandar's alam in Rochaldas's
    darbar 68`). **The book does not say how the alam came there, who installed it, or what is done with
    it beyond the urs: (not stated).**
  - **A negative the author states himself**: of Mulchand, "even his relationship with Sehwan Sharif and
    Lal Shahbaz Qalandar is not displayed" (p. 75, folio 62). Set against the alam and the pole at
    Rochaldas's darbar, this is the book's own contrast between two of its three Indian cases, and **it
    is not reconciled.**
  - **Index**: the locator run `Lal Shahbaz Qalandar` at folios **7, 36, 37, 43, 45–7, 58, 62, 67–8,
    67–9, 105, 108, 109, 112, 113, 120, 127–8, 132–4, 153, 165, 167–9, 171–3, 176, 179–80, 187, 198 n.5,
    200 n.4, 203 n.5** (p. 245, folio 232) — **"67–8, 67–9" is printed exactly so, two overlapping runs
    in sequence.** Under `darbars (shrine)`, "Qalandar's 120, 166, 167, 171, 176, 180" (p. 241,
    folio 228); under `dhamal`, "Qalandar's darbar 127" (p. 242, folio 229). **At neither subentry does
    the index write out "Lal Shahbaz".** Under `urs`, "of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar 45, 68, 105, 112, 113"
    (p. 251, folio 238) — the first place in the index where the ʿurs is attached to his name written
    out in full. **No death date, no birth date, no silsila and no shrine description anywhere in the two
    index chunks: (not stated).**

- **Practices & Events**
  - **The ʿurs dates as printed**: "For a few years, two distinct urs were celebrated: Lal Shahbaz's urs
    in the Islamic calendar, **18, 19 and 20 Shaban**, and Rochaldas's versi. One year, the two
    anniversaries fell at the same time. From then on, it was decided to celebrate both at the same
    time, or, that is to say, for the versi of Rochaldas." (pp. 146–147, folios 133–134) **The year in
    which the two fell together is (not stated).** Earlier: "They performed the mehndi on the second
    day of the urs, **the nineteenth day of Shaban**." (p. 58, folio 45) — **a bare Hijri month name,
    with no year and no `AH` marker: (not stated).** And: "The only time in a year when it plays a
    social and religious role is the time of the mehndi procession, on **the 19th Shaban**." (p. 127,
    folio 114) The book defines the term: "The Arabic-origin word urs depicts the mystical marriage
    realized between the Sufi and the God at the time of death. It is in fact the big annual festival
    (melo) that brings together the greatest number of pilgrims and an accompanying fair, a vogue, and
    many cultural events such as contests of stories and Sufi poetry." (p. 81, folio 68) **Whether that
    description is of Sehwan's urs or of the Ulhasnagar one is not disambiguated: (not stated).** The
    glossary: "Urs: the annual fair organized for commemorating the demise of a Sufi saint." (p. 220,
    folio 207) and "Versi: see urs." (p. 220, folio 207).
  - **The three mehndi processions of the ʿurs, by day and leader**: "His alleged son, Lal Gul, performs
    the mehndi during the urs. Before the procession, there is a musical session in a house, with the
    presence of the head of the Lakkiyyaris, Murad Shah. Therefore, there are three mehndi processions
    organized for the urs: **on the 18th of Shaban**, it is the procession led by the Lakiyyaris, **on
    the 19th** it is the one led by Ramchand and **on the 20th** is that of Lal Gul." (p. 181,
    folio 168) The book's hedge on Lal Gul is "alleged son". Compare: "In Sehwan, the mehndi is led on
    the first day by the dominant group of Sayyids, the descendants of the Prophet Muhammad, and on the
    second and third days by Hindus who belong to two distinct families." (p. 147, folio 134) **Neither
    Hindu family is named on that page: (not stated).**
  - **The two Hindu mehndi families — named two different ways.** "The **Navani** family in Sehwan
    Sharif was one of the two Hindu families with the **Kanungos** who used to perform mehndi since
    centuries during the urs of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar since centuries." (p. 58, folio 45 — the duplicated
    "since centuries … since centuries" is the text layer's, or the book's). Against that: Dalpat Sufi
    "was born in Sehwan Sharif in the **Mirani** family, one of the two Hindu families whose members
    performed the mehndi ceremony during the urs of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar. The other Hindu family was
    Mulchand Faqir's one, the Navanis." (p. 118, folio 105) And a third form: "On the second day, it
    left the house of the '**Meranis**' and '**Nevanis**' under the direction of Mulchand Faqir."
    (p. 183, folio 170) **Navani/Nevani, Mirani/Merani and Kanungo: all three spellings and both
    pairings recorded side by side, none reconciled by the book.** The index's only Navani is "Navani,
    Parumal 45" (p. 246, folio 233).
  - **The mehndi duty as an inherited office — two incompatible successions.** Chunk 006's: Mulchand's
    "first successor was Chandmal Chandrani, a local Hindu from Sehwan. But, in 1971, he decided to
    migrate to India, and his son Kanya Lal, who had a vegetable shop in the Shahi Bazar in Sehwan,
    refused to take care of the Mulchand Kafi. Mohan Lal, who was Mulchand's **nephew**, finally took
    care of it. Also, he took on the duty of performing the mehndi during the urs of Lal Shahbaz
    Qalandar." (p. 125, folio 112) "Mohan Lal passed away in 1981 and his son Ramchand began to perform
    the mehndi; he is still in charge. Ramchand is an Amil, but his mother was a Thakur." (p. 125,
    folio 112) Chunk 009's: "Before partition, the first procession was led by Gul Muhammad Lakkiyyari.
    On the second day, it left the house of the 'Meranis' and 'Nevanis' under the direction of Mulchand
    Faqir. Finally, on the third day, it started from the house of the Kanungos. Now, the eldest son of
    Gul Muhammad, Murad Shah, has replaced him to lead the mehndi of the first day. The two groups
    leading the following mehndis have changed. After the death of Mulchand Faqir in 1962, the duty of
    the mehndi was transmitted to his **cousin**, Mohan Lal, who then passed it on to his son, Ramchand,
    in 1981. The Kanungos migrated to India in the same year and the burden fell to Lal Das, a Hindu
    from Kashmir. It is today his son Lal Gul who fulfils this obligation." (p. 183, folio 170) A third
    statement makes Ramchand a different relation again: "the sajjada nashin is Ramchand, Mulchand's
    **nephew**, and he performs the mehndi ritual every year, leading a procession from Mulchand Kafi up
    to Lal Shahbaz Qalandar's darbar." (p. 193, folio 180) **"still in charge" and "today" are undated:
    the year of the author's statement is (not stated).** The index carries "Lal, Kanya 112", "Lal,
    Mohan 112", "Ramchand 112–14", "Chandrani, Chandmal 112" (pp. 240, 245, 247, folios 227, 232, 234).
  - **The third-day duty and its migration, traced through five holders**: "On the third day, the mehndi
    was led by the Kanungos, but in 1962 they decided to emigrate to India and settled in Ulhasnagar,
    where the son of Rochaldas, Hari, asked them to lead the mehndi procession in the darbar. In Sehwan
    Sharif, another Hindu named Lal Das then became mehndibardar." (p. 147, folio 134) "Gandaram
    (d. 2008), the eldest of the Kanungos, inherited the charge upon his father's [text-layer? father’s]
    death in 1948, but in the 1980s he was transferred to Bangalore, far from Ulhasnagar." (p. 147,
    folio 134) "After Gandaram’s demise, it would have been his brother Murlidhar who should have taken
    on the duty. But, he was living in Baroda, and Lachhman, the youngest brother who was himself living
    in Ulhaasnagar [text-layer? Ulhasnagar] was not allowed. Thus, it was the son of Hari, Damodar, who
    led the mehndi from that point on." (p. 147, folio 134) **Why Lachhman "was not allowed", and by
    whom, is (not stated).** **Note the collision with p. 183 (folio 170), which puts the Kanungos'
    migration in 1981, not 1962.**
  - **The mehndi's meaning and its form.** "henna or mehndi symbolizes the marriage between the Sufi and
    God, which is to say that it represents death as their union" (p. 147, folio 134). "The protocol of
    the urs reflects the balance at which the communities involved in the distribution of the charisma
    have arrived. Each procession is organized upon the same pattern. It is composed of singers,
    musicians and dancers. A designated person wears the silk chador, which is deposited on the tomb of
    the qalandar, and places a tray on which the henna (mehndi) is located." (p. 182, folio 169) "The
    procession is the reproduction of the classic procession of marriage. Each time, the procession runs
    through the narrow streets of the town while the 'clients' of each party grow more and more
    numerous. The roofs of the houses are filled with onlookers who do not want to miss this show. In
    any case, the celebration of the wedding will be repeated on each of the three days of the feast.
    The only difference will be that, each time, the officials will be different." (p. 183, folio 170)
    **No attendance figure is given anywhere: (not stated).**
  - **The order of events before the Mulchand Kafi procession, as printed**: "The main event occurring
    in Mulchand Kafi is the procession of the mehndi for the urs of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar, which starts
    from the kafi." (p. 126, folio 113) "Before the procession, two main events are arranged. First is
    the dastarbandi of Ramchand. As we saw before, this is the coronation, when the ceremonial turban
    (pag) is put on his head. It is followed by a musical session with musicians and dancers from
    Punjab. The apex of the listening to spiritual music is the singing of the famous song devoted to
    Lal Shahbaz Qalandar, **Dam dama mast qalandar**. The third is the preparation of the mehndi under a
    canopy." (p. 126, folio 113) **The book announces "two main events" and then enumerates a third; the
    inconsistency is the book's own and is reproduced rather than harmonised.** Figure 5.2(b) is
    "Ramchand's dastarbandi before the mehndi procession in Sehwan Sharif, 2011" (p. 149, folio 136).
  - **The Hindu custodian's title, and the duties he does not discharge**: "Finally, Mulchand Faqir is
    known in Sehwan as a **mehndibardar**." (p. 126, folio 113) "His kafi does not work like the other
    kafis in the town. Usually, the sajjada nashin stays for welcoming the murids, and provides them
    advice and amulets (tawiz). Ramchand did not play such a role. And no faqir stays in Mulchand Kafi."
    (p. 126, folio 113) "Only Ramchand's family used to come, but the kafi is closed most of the time."
    (p. 127, folio 114) The office is indexed "mehndibardar 44, 113, 134, 139, 169" (p. 246, folio 233).
  - **A caste given a priestly function at the ʿurs**: "The Khuhnbatis, as saffron specialists, were
    attributed a priestly function in the ceremony of the mystical nuptials of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar."
    (p. 181, folio 168) Their name "comes from the Sindhi word khuhnbo, which means saffron" (p. 181,
    folio 168).
  - **The ʿurs as the moment rank is re-established, under a State protocol**: "For all categories of
    people involved in sharing the charisma, the urs is also a moment of prime importance. It is at this
    moment that the position of each one is reaffirmed. These positions, be they honorary or real, can
    be watched during the implementation of a protocol imposed by the State." (p. 181, folio 168) "This
    protocol, as well as the distribution of tasks between confessions, has not undergone fundamental
    change since the partition, with one exception." (p. 181, folio 168) — the exception being the Lal
    Kunwar custom, under Legends below.
  - **A Hindu sajjada nashin at Sehwan, in a figure caption only**: "Figure 7.1 Asan Gul, a Hindu
    sajjada nashin in Sehwan Sharif." (p. 182, folio 169; listed at p. 10, folio not stated). **Asan Gul
    is named nowhere in the body text and no shrine is attached to him: (not stated).** The caption is
    the strongest custodianship fact in chunk 009 and its least supported.
  - **A lifetime of lamp-lighting service, dated at its end**: "Lal Das was a Hindu from Kashmir who came
    to make the pilgrimage to Sehwan Sharif. He devoted nearly forty years of his life to serving Lal
    Shahbaz Qalandar. He was in charge of lighting the lamps (chiraghi wala) before extinguishing
    himself 'in the smell of sanctity' in 1993." (pp. 180–181, folios 167–168) **His birth year is (not
    stated); "nearly forty years" is the only span given.**
  - **The dhamal at the shrine, timed and described**: "In Sehwan Sharif, where his shrine stands, the
    dhamal is performed **every day at sunset**. It is performed by professional drummers in the main
    courtyard and **hundreds of devotees** start to dance to reach ecstasy. Also, a number of women
    participate, but in a different manner." (p. 140, folio 127) **"hundreds of devotees" is the only
    attendance figure for Sehwan; no exact number: (not stated).** Restated in a note: "The dhamal is an
    ecstatic dance performed every day before the sunset in the precinct of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar's
    darbar, in Sehwan Sharif." (p. 210, folio 197, Ch. 1 n. 18) The glossary's shorter form: "Dhamal: a
    Sufi ritual where a dance is performed with drums." (p. 218, folio not stated)
  - **The ʿurs exported to India by a dream instruction, and then merged.** The alam "was added to the
    darbar after Rochaldas had a dream in which Lal Shahbaz Qalandar asked him to celebrate his urs
    every year, as it was celebrated at Sehwan Sharif in Sindh" (p. 81, folio 68). Table 5.1 is headed
    "Table 5.1 Daily programme of Rochaldas’s versi in 2012" (p. 146, folio 133), and the text under it
    reads "urs of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar. In the late 1950s, they were celebrated separately, but now they
    are celebrated together. The programme clearly shows both the death anniversaries of Lal Shahbaz and
    Rochaldas have been harmoniously mixed." (p. 146, folio 133 — the opening words are the tail of a
    sentence that begins in the preceding chunk.) "It seems that the first celebration took place in
    **1959**, two years after the death of Rochaldas, who had left orders." (p. 146, folio 133)
    **Rochaldas's own death year is not printed there, only "two years" before 1959; it is not
    computed: (not stated).**
  - **The Indian dhamal, dated and compared**: at Rochaldas's darbar "the dhamal was adapted: it is a
    simple beating of drums practised in front of the samadhi, which plays the rhythm of the devotional
    song Mast qalandar devoted to Lal Shahbaz" (p. 140, folio 127); "**The ritual has gone on unchanged
    since 1952.**" (p. 141, folio 128) "It starts with the dhamal, the ritual related to Lal Shahbaz
    Qalandar. Nonetheless, the dhamal performed in Ulhasnagar is different. It is reduced to the
    drumming, while in Sehwan, the drumming is played for the ecstatic dance of the devotees." (p. 145,
    folio 132) "The purpose of the ritual is clearly to make the darbar of Rochaldas a territory that
    extends from an original sacred territory which is located in Sindh, the holy city of Sehwan
    Sharif." (p. 141, folio 128)
  - **The Indian mehndi's route and its Hindu close**: "At that time, the mehndi procession went as far
    as the river. There, a puja was performed just as it is in the shrine, ending with Hari Om
    Jhulelal." (p. 147, folio 134) **The river is not named: (not stated).** "The mehndi of Lal Shahbaz
    makes it possible to maintain a direct link through the ritual with the lost territory of Sindh."
    (p. 147, folio 134) Figure 5.2(a): "Damodar's mehndi procession at Rochaldas's darbar, Ulhasnagar,
    2012" (p. 149, folio 136).
  - **The Indian rituals in retreat**: "For more practical reasons, other rituals have been simplified –
    for instance, the mehndi procession at Rochaldas’s darbar, since the mehndibardar left Ulhasnagar."
    (p. 152, folio 139) After the Ayodhya Affair "the main impact was the retreat and/or disappearance
    of major Sufi rituals, especially the dhamal, the alam and the mehndi" (p. 201, folio 188); "The
    mehndi procession is performed inside Rochaldas's darbar, while previously, at the time of the
    Kanungos, it was made from the darbar to the river." (p. 201, folio 188) **"the Kanungos" is not
    glossed or dated there: (not stated).**
  - **A possible further presence, recorded without being claimed**: at Dadi Dhan's versi "his Sakhi
    sabajhal was sung before Dam mast qalandar, in the version authored by Qutub Ali Shah" (p. 149,
    folio 136), and in Mulchand's path "other followers were dancing on the Mast qalandar rhythm"
    (p. 149, folio 136). **The book does not state whom the song names: (not stated).**
  - **Index**: `mehndi (henna) 45, 132, 197 n.16`, with "procession 134, 136, 139, 168–70, 180, 188";
    "**Qalandar, ceremony of 45, 47, 58, 105, 112–14**"; "in Ulhasnagar 136"; "wedding and 131"
    (pp. 245–246, folios 232–233). The `urs` subentry's folios (45, 68, 105, 112, 113) overlap the
    `mehndi` subentry's at **45, 105, 112**; **the index does not state that the mehndi ceremony and the
    ʿurs are the same observance: (not stated).**

- **Legends & Miracles**
  - **The origin of the Hindu mehndi right, attributed by the book to local tradition**: "According to
    local tradition, Meru and Nao were two brothers who used to take care of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar by
    providing food for him. Very grateful to them, Lal Shahbaz decided they would lead the mehndi ritual
    after his death, when his own urs would be celebrated." (p. 58, folio 45) **The brothers' religion
    is not stated and the link between them and the Navani/Kanungo families is not spelled out: (not
    stated).**
  - **The dhamal's origin, given as tradition and not as the author's claim**: "This ritual is directly
    related to Lal Shahbaz Qalandar because tradition says that, under this name, he practised an
    ecstatic giratory [text-layer? gyratory] dance in which he saw the supreme form of meditation
    (zikr), a position that brought him closer to Jalal al-Din Rumi (d. 1275), the celebrated founder of
    the brotherhood of the whirling dervishes. Through the dhamal, Lal Shahbaz Qalandar reached the
    final stage of absorption into God (fana fi Allah)." (p. 140, folio 127) **"under this name" is not
    glossed; the name it contrasts with is not given: (not stated).**
  - **The Lal Kunwar, "the fiancée of the red" — a woman married annually to the saint's grave, in two
    irreconcilable versions.** Burton's: "In the middle of the nineteenth century, Richard Burton
    mentioned the following custom: 'Every year, a daughter of the Khonbati caste (saffron flower dyers)
    was married to the grave, with music, dance and all the solemnities due to her' (Burton 1851: 211).
    This custom, known as Lal Kunwar (the fiancée of the red), **disappeared during the partition in
    1947**." (p. 181, folio 168) "Burton specifies, however, that the bride was released after a year,
    though she had to remain forever unmarried." (p. 181, folio 168) The rival: "according to other
    sources, the young Lal Kunwar belonged to the caste of Kanungos. The origin of the custom comes from
    the fact that a young woman of the caste had fallen in love with Lal Shahbaz. The latter being a
    renouncer, she threw herself from a balcony at his feet, thus sacrificing her life for him. Lal
    Shahbaz would have been touched by this sacrifice and he would have authorized that, after his
    death, a young virgin of the caste should be married to his grave. The Lal Kunwar lived for a year
    in a room where she was venerated as a devi, a goddess. When she was released, she was forced to
    accept celibacy." (p. 182, folio 169) **The author then refuses it as unevidenced: "Despite a
    relatively thorough investigation, with Pakistani and Indian informants, both Muslim and Hindu, it
    was not possible to obtain evidence of this chain of events." (p. 182, folio 169)**
  - **The saint's handprints in the henna overnight**, attributed with "It is said": "It is said that the
    act of depositing the chador on the grave amounts to putting the fiancée in her wedding clothes. It
    is also said that, the next morning, palms and fingerprints are visible in the henna that was
    deposited the day before. This suggests that, during the night, the qalandar coated his hands with
    henna and is therefore ready to be united to his divine lover." (pp. 182–183, folios 169–170)
  - **A saint appears in a dream and commissions his own ʿurs in a foreign country**: "Shortly before his
    death, Rochaldas had a dream in which Lal Shahbaz Qalandar appeared. He pointed out that although the
    Hindu Sindhis had forgotten him, Lal Shahbaz always watched over them in India. He asked him to
    celebrate his urs, as at Sehwan." (p. 146, folio 133) **No date for the dream: (not stated).**

- **Significance**
  - **One of the book's three case studies**: "this chapter is based on the study of three different
    cases. The first case is that of Sehwan Sharif, a main Sufi pilgrimage centre of Sindh, where the
    darbar of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar is located." (p. 179, folio 166); "Sehwan will be one of the case
    studies in this chapter." (p. 178, folio 165)
  - **The book's type case for a shared shrine under two names**: his is the second of four categories,
    "the place of worship is divided, but where the saint in focus is venerated under two different names
    by the Hindus and the Muslims" (p. 50, folio 37); and, restated, "In other places, there is only a
    tomb, but the Hindus give the saint a distinct identity… In this configuration, there is only one
    annual fair, in which the Hindus play a leading ritual role." (p. 178, folio 165)
  - **One of the three Sufi traditions the Hindu Sufis were affiliated to**: "the Hindu Sufis were
    affiliated to three main Sufi traditions of Sindh: Lal Shahbaz Qalandar in Sehwan Sharif, Daraza and
    Tando Jahaniyya in Hyderabad." (p. 60, folio 47) **He is named here as a tradition, alongside two
    toponyms.**
  - **A saint whose shrine drew a Hindu into Sufism without a local master**: "It is interesting to
    observe that Mulchand was not initiated by a local Sufi Master. He was thus independent from the
    local Sufis, and his only connection with Sufism in Sehwan was directly through Lal Shahbaz
    Qalandar." (p. 59, folio 46) "Mulchand nevertheless performed the mehndi ceremony of Lal Shahbaz
    Qalandar in Sehwan until his death" (p. 59, folio 46) — Mulchand Faqir's dates are printed
    "(1883–1962)" (p. 55, folio 42), **but the book does not say the mehndi ran to 1962 and no such
    inference is made here.** "Until his death in 1962, Mulchand lived as a recluse, probably leaving his
    dwelling place only for performing the mehndi of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar" (p. 71, folio 58 — the hedge
    "probably" is the book's own). His "family was perfectly integrated in the dominant groups of the
    town through the main ritual role they were playing in the mehndi procession during the annual
    festival devoted to Lal Shahbaz Qalandar" (p. 71, folio 58). **That festival's date, month and
    duration are (not stated), and the book does not there call it an urs or a melo.**
  - **A Muslim master's homage visit, the occasion of a Hindu boy's initiation**: "Once, Rakhiyyal Shah
    (1846–1940), a high-level Sufi of the Qadiriyya, came to pay homage to Lal Shahbaz Qalandar."
    (p. 59, folio 46)
  - **A Hindu Sufi who chose to die at Sehwan rather than migrate**: Sobhraj Faqir "was often in company
    with Mulchand Faqir and they were both fond of dhamal, as they used to perform together. Sobhraj
    wanted to stay for the ending of his life in Sehwan Sharif, which he did." (p. 179, folio 166)
  - **Hindus still holding a ritual role under Islamist pressure, and the author's reading of why**: "In
    a Pakistan under the pressure of Islamist extremists, it may seem surprising that Hindus can still
    play a ritual role in the Sufi set-up of Sehwan Sharif. This resilience can be explained primarily by
    the desire to preserve a historic heritage. But beyond this integrative dynamic of Sufism, it must be
    understood that the Hindus do not exercise this function as Hindus, but as Hindu Sufis. This is
    obvious from the fact that the Hindu processions cannot be distinguished from the Muslim ones, and
    furthermore, nothing especially Hindu can be observed in these devotional events. The Hindu
    processions are quite similar to all of the processions that take place in the Sufi mausoleums."
    (p. 183, folio 170)
  - **The 2017 attack, with its date, its ritual moment and its casualty figures**: "While the terrorists
    have targeted Sufi places in Pakistan for years, a huge terrorist attack took place in the darbar of
    Lal Shahbaz Qalandar in Sehwan Sharif on **16 February 2017**. It was **during the dhamal** and
    **ninety people were killed; more than 300 were injured**." (p. 217, folio 204, Ch. 7 n. 6) **No
    perpetrator is named: (not stated).** In the body: "since the Sufi dargahs are now targets of
    Islamist terrorism,6 one can wonder about the future of the Hindu dargah. More pragmatically, it is
    difficult not to think that the Hindu dargah would have been temporarily closed after the terrible
    terrorist attack which occurred in Sehwan Sharif in February of 2017." (p. 186, folio 173) **There
    the attack's target is not named beyond "Sehwan Sharif", no casualty figure is given, and the closure
    is the author's inference, not a reported fact: (not stated).**
  - **His rising fame dated to a political era**: "As Pakistani citizens, the local Lohanas could have
    reacted to the growing fame of the saint of Sehwan Sharif, especially since Bhutto's rule in the
    1970s." (p. 193, folio 180) **"Bhutto" is given no first name: (not stated).**
  - **Sehwan as a town without communal violence — and as a town whose Hindus left**: "In a small town
    such as Sehwan Sharif, Mulchand could have not felt any threat, and therefore, there was no reason
    for him to leave the place where his ancestors had been settled since centuries. Contrary to other
    cities in Northern Sindh – for instance, Sukkur, following the Manzilgah riots – no communal violence
    had ever been registered. Maybe this is due to the spirit of toleration which prevailed in Sehwan"
    (p. 71, folio 58 — "could have not" and "Maybe" are the book's own hedges). **Yet: "the large
    majority of the Hindu population of Sehwan migrated to India" (p. 71, folio 58) — the book states
    both and does not explain the tension.**

### Place-level material: Sehwan Sharif / Sehwan

*The book's place-level material on "Sehwan Sharif / Sehwan" (2 rows), from its list of rows attached at place or person level, copied into every existing entry that list names (ruling (a); Rauf, 10 October 2026). It is about the place in general, not about this shrine alone.*

- **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**, **shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar** (the book's "Sehwan Sharif", also "Sehwan")
  — **place-level.** The town appears throughout the book with no shrine, tomb, saint, ʿurs or custodian
  attached to the clause: "to be born in Sehwan Sharif, in central Sindh where **Vicholi** was spoken (a
  Sindhi dialect which was officially proclaimed to be the official Sindhi language by the British), was
  different from being born in Hyderabad" (p. 30, folio 17); "Nandirani Merani, an Amil from Sehwan
  Sharif who translated many books on different matters such as history, geography and so on" (p. 45,
  folio 32); "In his teens, he [Bedil] went to Sehwan Sharif, where he is reported to have been
  vouchsafed spiritual visions" (p. 97, folio 84) — **what he saw is not described: (not stated)**; "his
  native town of Sehwan Sharif, **a very reputed centre of Sufi pilgrimage**" (p. 113, folio 100);
  "through his family, Mulchand was extremely well integrated into the mystical network of Sehwan
  Sharif" and "the **Shahi Bazar** in Sehwan" (p. 125, folio 112); "Sehwan Sharif, where Mulchand decided
  to stay" (p. 128, folio 115); Paru Chawla "was born as Lalwani in Sehwan Sharif in 1932. **Despite the
  strong Sufi ambiance of the city**, she left at about fifteen years old" (p. 161, folio 148); and two
  clauses naming no saint at pp. 210, 211 (folios 197, 198). **Two substantive place-level findings
  belong here rather than to either shrine**: "Before partition, the Hindus made up about half of the
  total population, and the town hosted **two main Hindu temples. The first one was devoted to Shiva and
  settled in the north-eastern part of the town. The other one was devoted to Jhulelal and was
  established on the north-western side of the town.** From 1947 onwards, almost all of the Hindu
  population migrated to India." (pp. 179–180, folios 166–167) — **neither temple is named, dated or said
  to survive: (not stated)**; and "All the **Thakurs**, the priestly caste of Jhulelal, left Sindh to
  settle in India, especially in Bombay and Ulhasnagar. In Sehwan, they could have been the most powerful
  Hindu group, and in Bombay, there is still a **Sehwani panchayat** which has published several
  directories of the Sehwanis in India and overseas since partition. For some time now, the Sehwani
  panchayat has had a Facebook page." (p. 180, folio 167) Also here: "the Thakurs who were running
  Jhulelal's temple in the northwestern part of the town" migrated (p. 71, folio 58), and "Here, the
  Thakurs came from Sehwan Sharif." (p. 210, folio 197, Ch. 2 n. 6) Sehwan Sharif is also the book's
  example of a small city, against Karachi as a big one (p. 67, folio 54). **The Sehwan material that
  names Lal Shahbaz Qalandar is in his own subsection above and is not repeated here.**

### Place-level material: the Jamshoro district

*The book's place-level material on "the Jamshoro district" (2 rows), from its list of rows attached at place or person level, copied into every existing entry that list names (ruling (a); Rauf, 10 October 2026). It is about the place in general, not about this shrine alone.*

- **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**, **shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar** (the book's "the Jamshoro district") —
  **place-level, district-level.** "It is in the Jamshoro district and the seat of a taluka." (p. 184,
  folio 171) — said of **Thano Bula Khan**, not of Sehwan; **whether Tando Ahmad Khan shares that
  district is not stated.**

### Province-level material: sindh

*The book's `sindh` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: before the migration "from 1947 onwards", as the book contrasts it; author's argument): "I was well aware that the Sindhi Hindus of Sindh had gone to Sufi shrines, and that they shared their passion for Sufi poetry with Muslim Sindhis and others, especially through Shah Abd al-Latif's Shah jo Risalo" (p. 14, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "the Sufi culture of Sindh", "the region"; refers to: "the turn of the nineteenth and twentieth centuries", "as early as the middle of the nineteenth century"; author's argument): it is necessary "to understand why and how it constituted the dominant form of the episteme of the region at the turn of the nineteenth and twentieth centuries. The historical context is fundamental: it is dominated by British colonization". The emergence rested on "interaction between new local elites and colonial administrators, as early as the middle of the nineteenth century" (p. 17, folio 4). "The Sufi culture of Sindh was nonetheless the most shared product, but this does not prevent the society from being highly fragmented" (said of the Sindhicate area, p. 19, folio 6). "Sufi poetry is the backbone of Sufism in Sindh, including for the Hindu Sufis" (p. 26, folio 13).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: "As early as 1851" and 1856, and "During British colonization" (Burton's book "published in 1851") with the present tense for the dual identities; quoted source: Burton 1851, Raikes 1856, and author's argument): "Richard Burton pointed out that certain populations of Sindh could not be considered a Muslim or a Hindu (Burton 1851: 307, 323)". Raikes "spoke of populations that are not recognized by Muslims or Hindus (Raikes 1856: 4)". This "neither…nor" category "met the credo of the Sufis of Sindh, whose works known as kalams constituted the foundations of Sufi culture" (pp. 19–20, folios 6–7). Also: "As Richard Burton noted as early as 1851, it is indeed the worship of the intercessors that constitutes the common religious idiom of Hindus and Muslims (Burton 1851: 325)" (p. 51, folio 38). Restated in Ch. 7: "In Sindh, we already know that a number of sacred figures have a dual religious identity, Muslim and Hindu. The Muslim identity is always related to Sufism, while the Hindu side usually belongs to the Nathpanth. During British colonization, there was much evidence of the flexibility of religious belonging in Sindh". Burton "stated that different populations of Sindh were 'neither Hindoos nor Moslems'", applied explicitly to the Shikaris and the Dheds, "while other authors consider the Shikaris as Muslims, and the Dheds as Hindus" (p. 187, folio 174). The Pithoro Pir case shows "the variety of the negotiations between Sufism and Hinduism in Sindh" (p. 192, folio 179).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "the Muslim Sindhis"; refers to: "before the eighteenth century" and "the early eighteenth century"; book's historical prose): "Historical sources provide evidence that, before the eighteenth century, the people who identified themselves as Muslims were few", "probably" because the denomination "was restricted to the sayyids and their dependents". In the early eighteenth century, Naqshbandi treatises "were written in Sindh by Naqshbandi scholars from Lowari Sharif". These scholars showed "that the Muslim Sindhis did not know the Quran", wrote paraphrases and then "a Sindhi translation of the Arabic Quran", and "condemned some rituals, especially coined as Hindu or Shia, as non-Islamic" (pp. 22–23, folios 9–10).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: a long-term sequence: an "Ismaili step", then "From the thirteenth century onward" classical Sufism, then "the colonial step"; author's argument after Pollock): "Manuscripts and early lithographed books provide evidence that the religious culture of Sindh had been vernacularized". "In Sindh, the first phase was usually a linguistic translation", from "Arabic and Persian into Sindhi". The colonial step meant that, "because of a number of new factors (including the census), the people had more and more to identify themselves according to their affiliation to 'great religions'… Before, they had not identified themselves through a religious persuasion, but according to their locality, caste and ethnicity" (p. 23, folio 10).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "the Khairpur emirate in Sindh"; refers to: "after 1857"; quoted source: Dahnhardt 2002): after 1857 "many Kayasht migrated to work in the Princely States, including the Khairpur emirate in Sindh". Despite Brahmashankar Mishra's years in Karachi and Hyderabad, "no formal relation between Sindh and the Hindu branch of the Naqshbandiyya has been found" (p. 26, folio 13).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "the literati in Sindh"; refers to: "the nineteenth century", "During the Raj"; quoted source: Narsain 1932): Narsain "clearly states that the integration of Sufism inside of the religious environment of the Hindu Sindhis dates back to the nineteenth century, and that the main actors involved were the Amils, the literary class". "In the context of Sindh, the Sufi Master shares many features with what Weber names a guru, but his followers, the Amils, belong to the literate class" (p. 27, folio 14). "while the Amils were the bulk of the literati in Sindh, they were also instrumental in incorporating Sufism in the religiosity of the local Hindus" (p. 45, folio 32).
- (book's word: "Sindh, Pakistan"; refers to: present day as of Thapan 2005; quoted source: Thapan 2005): Thapan "rightly observed that the spiritual masters (sajjada nashin or pir) are still in Sindh, Pakistan", namely those of Daraza, Tando Jahaniya and Jhok Sharif (p. 27, folio 14).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: period not stated; quoted source: Kothari 2009, and author's argument): Kothari "finds explanations in the localization of Sindh as a frontier. This means there was a permanent influx of migrants… Sindh was localized far from the main imperial centres" (p. 28, folio 15). Boivin: "It is a fact that Sindh, because of its geographical location both as an isolated and a buffer zone, has hosted many unorthodox versions of normative religions, especially Hinduism and Islam. This plays out as a breeding ground for new religious elaborations that flourished especially in the nineteenth century" (p. 29, folio 16).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "colonial Sindh"; refers to: "the nineteenth century", "from 1850 onwards"; author's argument): "In Sindh, another Persian word was mostly used, that of darbar"; "In Sindh, the darbar was the sacred territory in which the Sufi culture was at work". "I argue that Sindh hosted a specific shared religious culture resulting from a process of vernacularization, which is not contradictory of the fact that some of its features can also be found in neighbouring provinces". The result of colonization was "the spread of intelligentsia from 1850 onwards" and a renewed religiosity "among the Sindhis, whether they were Muslim, Sikh or Hindu", performed in the darbar, a term "used by the Sikhs, the Muslims and the Hindus" (pp. 37–38, folios 24–25; announced at p. 30, folio 17, as "the darbar culture" of "colonial Sindh").
- (book's word: "Sindh", "a Muslim-dominated region"; refers to: "the late nineteenth century", up to Partition; author's argument): "the Sindhis of the late nineteenth century were more and more inclined to identify themselves with a religion as it would be acknowledged by the colonial power" (p. 51, folio 38). The migrants came "From a Muslim-dominated region… While through the late census in Sindh, they identified more and more as Hindu" (p. 33, folio 20).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: period not stated, with colonial rule as the renewing force; author's argument): "Frequently, the claim arises that there was no caste system in Sindh, or at least not so rigid a caste system as seen in other parts of India, because of the very small number of Brahmans. Indeed, it is possible that Sindh hosted a specific social fabric", but the colonial power "operated a deep renewal of the social fabric, in Sindh and of course everywhere else in India" (pp. 36–37, folio not stated / folio 24).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: "the first census of 1872"; quoted source: Hughes 1876): "Sindh's population statistics are not available in the first census of 1872. The population was 2,333,527 million. Sindh had 1,718,688 Muslims, 393,092 Hindus, 5,034 Christians and 86,663 persons put in a category titled 'Other castes such as Sikhs, Parsis, Jews, etc.' (Hughes 1876: 878)". The figures are kept as printed. See Sweep notes (p. 38, folio 25).
- (book's word: "the society of Sindh", "the Muslim society of Sindh"; refers to: "1843", "Before 1843", Talpur and earlier Kalhora rule; book's historical prose citing Ansari 1992, Westphal-Hellbusch 1964, Burton 1851): asking "How was the society of Sindh organized in 1843?", the book says Muslim society "was segmented according to ethnic criteria that distinguished the Ashrafs and the Ajlafs". "Given the political domination of the Talpurs, the Baluchis were at the top of local society", having provided since the eighteenth century "the bulk of the troops of the Kalhoras, who had ruled Sindh before them", and holding jagirs. "In Sindh, the Sayyids had two characteristics. The majority of them were landowners (zamindar), but they were also pir". "The pirs of Sindh play the role of mediators between the tribes, and between the rulers and the governed". "The power of the pir was reinforced in the eighteenth century when the Kalhora developed the irrigation system" (Ansari 1992: 28–9). "Apart from a few groups of Afghans and Pathans in the northwest, the bulk of the Muslims were Sindhis, most of whom were landless farmers (hari)". The trading Muslims were "the Memon, the Khojah and the Bohrah", and excluded groups such as the Shikaris and the "Shidi" stood at the bottom. "Before 1843, Muslims were divided between being Sunnis and Shiites. The Sunnis were in the majority, but the sovereigns, the Talpur, were themselves Shiites"; Burton "asserted that Arabic was known only to Muslim scholars" (pp. 38–41, folios 25–28).
- (book's word: "the Hindu society of Sindh", "Eastern Sindh", "rural Sindh"; refers to: Talpur and British periods, as stated; book's historical prose citing Aitken 1907, Thakur 1959): "In the Hindu society of Sindh, the British knowledge is often limited to mercantile castes from which two points arose: the absence of a real system of castes and the small role played by the Brahmans". The Saraswat Brahmans "accepted food from the Lohanas". The Lohanas "represented half of the total Hindu population" and were divided into Amils and Bhaibands. "In Eastern Sindh, the Rajput had retained their feudal prerogatives under the Talpur, and the British had reinstated them in exchange for their loyalty". "The panchayat system prevailed among the Hindus of Sindh. In rural Sindh, the panchayat of the Lohanas could have authority over other castes" (pp. 40–41, folios 27–28).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "the east of the province"; refers to: "From the beginning of the nineteenth century onwards" and "between 1843 and 1872"; book's historical prose citing Thakur 1959 and the first census): "Contrary to popular belief, there were many untouchables in Sindh", among them "Bhils or Kohlis" and "the Menghwar, mostly settled around Umerkot". Then: "it is certain whether these populations were established in the east of the province, which is none other than the ultimate extension of the great Thar Desert" (printed so; the sense seems to need "uncertain", not supplied). "The first census reports that many would have migrated from Gujarat or Rajasthan between 1843 and 1872"; most "worked in agriculture", and "crafts were largely in the hands of Muslims" (p. 40, folio 27).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "the Naqshbandis of Sindh"; refers to: the eighteenth-century manuscripts, period otherwise not stated; book's historical prose): "The Naqshbandis of Sindh followed the reforming enterprise led by Shah Waliullah in Delhi". Of Shia devotional poetry (marsiya): "It is noteworthy that, in Sindh, this devotional poetry is cherished by both Muslims and Hindus, the latter being performers as well as authors" (p. 42, folio 29).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "nineteenth-century Sindh"; refers to: tradition "in the mid-tenth century", a sanctuary "dating back to the seventeenth century", "nineteenth-century Sindh"; book's historical prose citing Thakur 1959, Fuller 1992): "Among the Lohanas and related mercantile groups, two main cults were followed: the Nanakpanth and the Daryapanth". The Daryapanth "is in all probability the oldest of the two. It is based on the deification of the Indus River", run by "a priestly caste, the Thakurs". The Lohana practised animal sacrifice at Navratri and for Khetrapal. "The Rajputs did not practise in either". "In Sindh, the most popular deified heroes were Rama Pir (Ramdeo or Ramdev), Pithoro Pir, Pabuji and Guga Pir" (pp. 42–43, folios 29–30). "In Sindh, many Nanakpanthis belonged to a subgroup known as the Udasis" (p. 53, folio 40). "In nineteenth-century Sindh, the Nanakpanth, or 'the path of Nanak', was probably the dominant religious path among the Hindu Sindhis" (p. 90, folio 77).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "peripheral regions like Sindh", "the rural areas of Sindh"; refers to: "the last quarter of the nineteenth century", "the end of the nineteenth century"; author's argument citing Cheesman 1997): "Sindh, like most of the regions of India, was subject to this change", the "objectification of the Sufi legacy in Sindh". "the complex process through which new classes appeared in peripheral regions like Sindh has not yet gotten such attention", except "the domination of new Hindu groups in the rural areas of Sindh". After "the growth of new emporium in Sindh, especially Karachi", the Lohanas invested "in the agricultural lands, mainly cotton, and they became powerful landowners, locally known as zamindars, at the end of the nineteenth century" (pp. 43–44, folios 30–31).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "the commissioner in Sindh"; refers to: "the Talpur rule (1783–1843)", "when the British conquered Sindh", "the 1850s"; book's historical prose): the Amils, "probably from the eighteenth century onwards" in administration, "almost monopolized the function of kardar" under the Talpurs. "when the British conquered Sindh, they had already formed a separate social class". "When Persian as official language was given up in the 1850s, Sindhi and English were the two languages used in the administration". The Amils led "the huge campaign of translation launched by Sir Bartle Frere (1815–84), the commissioner in Sindh" (p. 44, folio 31). Conclusion: "In Sindh, the main social structure was a quasi-feudal system, with a civil servant class and the groups involved in trade, but the policy implemented by the British commissioners, especially Sir Bartle Frere, gave birth to the Sindhi intelligentsia" (p. 63, folio 50).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: "1843", 1885, 1896–1907; book's historical prose citing Gidumal 1932, Hughes 1872, Aitken 1907, Sadhu Vaswani 2001): of European spiritual movements, "The first to reach Sindh was that of Freemasonry, since the conqueror and first governor of Sindh, Sir Charles Napier, built a Masonic lodge in 1843 in Karachi". After Reverend Bambridge's Karachi lectures, "In Sindh, the lectures led to a revival of religious and theological studies" (p. 46, folio 33). Annie Besant "was known in Sindh as Basanti Devi" (p. 47, folio 34).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: "the 1850s", "1866", "the 1890s"; author's argument): "In Sindh, a printing press was brought to Karachi in the 1850s, but the main event in relation to Sufism came with the printing in 1866 of the Shah jo Risalo". This was followed by the "rationalization" of the poetry "initiated by Hindu literati in the 1890s" (pp. 47–48, folios 34–35).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: Parsram's own day (book published 1924) and an unstated past; quoted source: Jethmal Parsram, *Sind and its Sufis*, 1924): Parsram summarized "the many religions that have flourished in Sindh, like Buddhism, Jainism and Hinduism. More recently, he added, two other religions had become dominant: Sikhism and Sufism". He wrote: "there is hardly a country in the whole of Asia, including India, in which the mystic thought of two great civilizations, the Indian and the Arabic-Iranian, is seen as in so beautiful a union as in Sind" (Parsram 1924: 83–4). He also gave "what he called the mystic doctrine of Sind" (p. 49, folio 36).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "rural Sindh"; refers to: period not stated ("for centuries"), with "the late nineteenth century" as the turning point; author's argument citing Thakur 1959): "In rural Sindh, they [Muslims] worshipped the smallpox goddess Sitala when epidemics struck the village". "In the religiosity of Sindh, the devotional relationship of a follower with a spiritual Master was prevalent, rather than the focus being on affiliation to an institutionalized religion" (p. 51, folio 38). "Sindh had drawn – for centuries – a kind of specific religiosity based on devotion, and which largely considered faith as being located beyond any formal belonging to an institutionalized religion" (p. 66, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "the Sindhi area"; refers to: "before on the eve of British colonization" (printed so) and "the Sindh of the nineteenth century"; author's argument): the pothi "allow[s] us to bring to light a first characteristic of the episteme of Sindh before on the eve of British colonization". "Devotional literature in Sindhi thus formed the basis of a culture that transcended social, religious and ethnic divisions". The culture "was not transmitted by the Ulemas or the pandits… this culture was primarily passed on to the villages by the caste musicians (manghanar or mirasi)" (pp. 52–53, folios 39–40). (The "Sindhian culture" area sentence is under `punjab` and `not attached`.)
- (book's word: "colonial Sindh", "Sindh"; refers to: "the late eighteenth century", "the second half of the nineteenth century", "the late nineteenth and early twentieth centuries", "the last decade of the nineteenth century", and "the late nineteenth century and early twentieth century" with the "Gurudwara Reform Act of 1925" (General conclusion); book's historical prose, and author's argument plus an endnote citing Jatt 2016): the recomposition of the religious scene "is attested to by the creation and the development of several important Nanakpanthi darbars throughout Sindh, the attempt to centralize the Daryapanth and the making of Hindu Sufi paths". "although some darbars had been created in the late eighteenth century, there was a renaissance of the Nanakpanthi darbars in the second half of the nineteenth century". In colonial Sindh there was "the attempt made to create a centralized path devoted to Jhulelal, then known as Amar Lal or Udero Lal", with "an attempt to impose a specific script, the Khudawadi", and "a meeting organized by several Hindu panchayats of Sindh at Udero Lal" (pp. 53–54, folios 40–41). Restated in the General conclusion: "Sindh was submitted to a religious recomposition in the late nineteenth century and early twentieth century, as is attested by the building of many new Nanakpanthi darbars all over Sindh" (p. 199, folio 186). After the Singh Sabha process, "The Sindhi Nanakpanthis were thus not included in the Sikh community, and they identified themselves as being a Hindu religious sect". Also: "A main factor in the religious renewal in Sindh was the new formulation of Hinduism that spread all over India in the late nineteenth century: the Neo-Vedanta as built by Ramakrishna" (p. 200, folio 187). And: "Due to the spread of the Nanakpanth in Sindh, the Hindu Sufis were very familiar with Sikh terminology … in Sindh, the groups of Sufis and Nanakpanthis did not have a central organization ruling an encompassing body of followers" (p. 202, folio 189). Endnote: "The Udasis of Sindh are coined as being a heterodox Sikh tradition, in one of the very few, if not the sole, academic papers devoted to the Nanakpanth in Sindh (Jatt 2016)" (Ch. 1 n. 10, p. 209, folio 196).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "Northern Sindh"; refers to: "sometime after the British conquest"; author's argument, hedged "could be a clue"): since two of the Hindu Sufis came from Northern Sindh (Shikarpur, Rohri), "This could be a clue suggesting that the devotional centres of Sindh had shifted north sometime after the British conquest, despite the role played by the Hyderabadi Amils in the spread of the Sufi culture" (p. 60, folio 47).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "the province"; refers to: Partition, "1947", "between December of 1939 and February of 1940" for the precedent; book's historical prose and author's argument, citing Kumar and Kothari 2016, Zamindar 2007): "it is a common trope to claim that Sindh did not suffer the violence experienced by Punjab". Yet "dramatic reports about Punjab were reaching the province", and "there was a precedent of communal violence in Sindh, this being the 'Sukkur riots'". Those riots "occurred beyond the city, in other cities such as Rohri, and also in other parts of Sindh, such as in Lakki" (p. 61, folio 48). "even if it never reached the level of violence in Punjab, it is irrelevant to claim there was no communal violence in Sindh" (p. 62, folio 49). The Rochaldas narrative "clearly alludes to the fact that the spread of violence throughout Sindh was not perpetrated by the Muslim Sindhis, but by the Mohajirs who clearly wanted to take over the houses of the Hindus, at least in Karachi". Also, "a number of Hindus had sent their wives and children to India already, while they were staying in Sindh" (pp. 70–71, folios 57–58). "From the 1920s onwards, there was an increase of the riots between Hindus and Muslims, a growing tension that culminated with partition" (p. 64, folio 51).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: "1941" and "1947"–"14 January" [1948]; book's historical prose citing Khuhro 1998, Dawn): "In 1941, the Hindus made up about 21 per cent of the total population of Sindh, including 6 per cent of the scheduled castes; therefore, they would have numbered at less than 1 million – exactly 0.9 million". The migrants "would have been closer to 0.8 million, if not less… there is a kind of consensus that they numbered around 1 million" (p. 61, folio 48). "By early November of 1947, 246,000 Hindus had already left Sindh by sea, air or rail. As of 1 January 1948, it was estimated that nearly 500,000 people had sought refuge in India. Hindus from interior Sindh enjoyed special trains chartered by the Sindh government" (p. 62, folio 49). "in December, with the addition of a train, 140,000 Hindus crossed the border". "on 14 January, the government of India appointed a director general of evacuation to formally assist non-Muslims who wanted to migrate from Sindh to India" (p. 63, folio 50). The Hindu Sindhis "started leaving Sindh especially after the Karachi riots in January 1948" (p. 66, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "colonial Sindh", "Sindh"; refers to: British colonization through to Partition; author's argument): "The impact of British colonization in Sindh was twofold. First, it contributed to the objectification of Sufism, enthroning it as the fundamental of Sindhi culture at large. The second consequence was a renewal of religiosity in relation to the spread of a composite middle class. It was marked by the emergence of the darbars, spreading a darbar culture" (p. 64, folio 51).
- (book's word: "the darbars in Sindh", "the Sufi places in Sindh, now in Pakistan"; refers to: after 1947; book's historical prose): "The darbars in Sindh were most of the time preserved with different arrangements regarding their management", often with Indian gaddi nashins or followers paying keepers. "despite the fluctuant diplomatic relations between Pakistan and India, it was always possible to travel across the border" (p. 68, folio 55). In contrast, "After partition, Molchand was worried that it was difficult for Hindu Sindhi Sufis to pay a visit to the Sufi places in Sindh, now in Pakistan" (p. 124, folio 111).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "eighteenth-century Sindh"; refers to: Shah Latif's time ("eighteenth-century Sindh"), otherwise period not stated; author's argument citing Dalpat Sufi 1965, Briggs 1998): "In Sindh, the name of sami is generally given to a group of wandering faqirs"; "Verangins (or Berangins)" is "the local Sindhi name for the Nathpanthis"; "the role played by the Nathpanthis in Sindh comes in many forms" (p. 77, folio 64). Shah Latif's vocabulary for the jogis "gives evidence that they were Nathpanthis, and that they were therefore an important religious community in eighteenth-century Sindh". His poetry also shows "the importance of music both to him personally and, historically, to the region" and "the role played by the musician in the regional society" (p. 92, folio 79). The bayragins are relics: "In Sindh, once again irrespective of religious belonging, they are the mark of renunciation" (p. 120, folio 107).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "Northern Sindh"; refers to: "the sixteenth century" (Ajwani) and the Sami period; quoted source: Ajwani 1970, "Motwani 1986" as printed, and author's argument): "it is easy to find many shared characteristics between the Bhakti movement and Sufism, especially as it stood in colonial Sindh". Ajwani states "that in Sindh, due to its geographical isolation, the Bhakti was spread only in the sixteenth century (Ajwani 1970: 52)", but gives no evidence "of the influence of these sants on the spiritual life of Sindh" (p. 90, folio 77). Jotwani called Shah Latif and Sachal "the late participaters in the Bhakti movement which had a major impact in Sindh through the spread of Guru Nanak's teaching" (cited as "Motwani 1986: 155") (p. 91, folio 78). "one can surmise that Sami's slokas were widespread among the Hindus of Northern Sindh" (p. 104, folio 91).
- (book's word: "the landscape of Sindh"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; author's argument): "the Sufi brotherhoods that are dominant in the landscape of Sindh, such as the Qadiriyya or the Sohrawardiyya" (p. 98, folio 85).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: period not stated for the popularity, "905" for the visit; author's argument, an endnote and the glossary): "In Sindh, Hallaj is a very popular figure, and many Sufi poets mention his name in their verses" (p. 102, folio 89). Mansur Hallaj "was very popular in Sindh" (Ch. 3 n. 8, p. 212, folio 199). The glossary adds: "He visited Sindh in 905" (p. 219, folio 206).
- (book's word: "the Muslim Sufis of Sindh", "Upper Sindh", "the wujudi Sufism of Sindh"; refers to: "the nineteenth century, during colonial domination"; author's argument): "the Muslim Sufis of Sindh were mostly attracted by the Vishnuite school of Hinduism" (p. 107, folio 94). The Sufis to whom the Hindus refer "mostly belong to Upper Sindh, with Siraiki as a language used for Sufi poetry besides Sindhi", and "all belong to the wujudi school of the Sufism of Sindh, and thus the Naqshbandis are excluded" (p. 108, folio 95). "this trend in the wujudi Sufism of Sindh seems to have developed in the nineteenth century, during colonial domination" (p. 109, folio 96).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: the fourteenth century — "1351", "1367", "1397" and "the following year", as printed; book's historical prose): of Pithoro Pir's lifetime, "the period when he lived was turbulent". "At the regional level, this period was that of the transition between the Somras and the Sammas. Although the latter officially took power from the first in 1351, the Somras had preserved their control of certain outlying areas of Sindh, including, precisely, the area of Umarkot. In 1351, the sultan of Delhi, Muhammad Shah Tughluq, arrived in Sindh in pursuit of a rebel". In 1367 Firuz Shah Tughluq "returned to lay siege to Thatha", and "Jam Juna II, who controlled Sindh then, sought alliances with the sultan of Gujarat" (p. 189, folio 176).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "the Indus Valley"; refers to: "from the thirteenth century onwards" for the Sohrawardiyya, "modern Sindh", and otherwise period not stated; author's argument, in the main text, an endnote and Annex 2): "three main Sufi tariqas were dominant among the Hindu Sufis of Sindh (Boivin 2015)":
  - the Qadiriyya, "the most influential";
  - the Qalandariyya, which "in the case of Sindh … reflects the radiation of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar and the pilgrimage centre of Sehwan Sharif";
  - the Sohrawardiyya, "the oldest Sufi order that settled in Multan and was predominant in the Indus Valley from the thirteenth century onwards".
  "Maybe because of the Sohrawardiyya's hold on Sindh, the Chishtiyya could not spread in Sindh as it did in the rest of South Asia. Notwithstanding, music appeared very early in the Sufism of Sindh" (pp. 227–228, folios 214–215). See also "the Chishtiyya was not widespread in Sindh" (p. 217, folio 204). Elsewhere: "The Sufis of Sindh we have been dealing with were mostly in touch with the Sohrawardiyya and the Qadiriyya" (p. 139, folio 126). Breath discipline is "attested to in other Sufis of modern Sindh, such as the Qadiriyya" (p. 138, folio 125). And: "in Sindh, Sufi practice was more based on a personal allegiance with an individual than any affiliation with a Sufi tariqa. Furthermore, Sufis could be affiliated with several different tariqas … This topic is one of the numerous ones that deserves to be studied further in Sindh" (p. 200, folio 187).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "Southern Sindh"; refers to: "from the sixteenth century onwards", "From the end of the seventeenth century onwards", "after the beginning of the eighteenth century", and the present tense; author's argument, Annex 2):
  - The Naqshbandiyya "came to Sindh from the sixteenth century onwards, with the coming of the Tarkhans and the Arghuns, two Central Asian dynasties that ruled Sindh".
  - It has "several important centres in Sindh, especially that of Lowari Shari [text-layer?] [Lowari Sharif] located in Southern Sindh, near Badin".
  - Its later treatises on how to follow Islamic rules lead to the conclusion that "the Sindhis, even when they were officially Muslim, did not practise 'Orthodox Islam'".
  - A Hindu Naqshbandi branch "is hardly the case in Sindh", and "the majority of the Naqshbandis in Sindh are strongly opposed to music and to the singing of devotional songs" (p. 228, folio 215).
  - In the main text: "In Sindh, save the Naqshbandis, Sufism is dominated by poetry expressing the concept of wahdat-e wujud" (p. 162, folio 149).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: the time of "Fath Ali Shah (d. 1824)", Talpur rule; endnote reporting the view of Ingvild Flaskerud): Flaskerud "thinks the first portrait of Ali was first drawn in Sindh and then brought as a gift to Fath Ali Shah (d. 1824), the shah of Persia who had friendly relations with the Talpurs, the emirs of Sindh, who were themselves Shia (Flaskerud : 25)" (Ch. 4 n. 5, p. 213, folio 200).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: "1226" according to the Sodhas' own tradition, and "The Gazetteer of 1907"; book's historical prose reporting the Sodhas' tradition and the Gazetteer): "The Sodhas form the dominant group among Rajputs of Sindh and, according to their tradition, they would have come from Ujjain, in Malwa, in 1226. Their chief, Parmar Sodha, would have been established as king (rano) at Umarkot. The majority of them became landowners and farmers. The Gazetteer of 1907 describes them as worshippers of Shiva" (p. 191, folio 178).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "the Hindus of Sindh"; refers to: "since the nineteenth century" and the present day; author's argument / ethnographic present): "In the field of Sindh studies, however, there is no published research related to Sufism and the Hindu Sufis in Sindh". "In Sindh, there are different ways through which the Hindus are related to Sufism", and the book sorts them into three categories, each illustrated by one site:
  - "the most common": a Sufi tomb "visited both by Muslims and Hindus" (example: Bhit Shah);
  - "also relatively common": a saint with "a double identity, Muslim and Hindu" (example: Udero Lal);
  - "a Sufi darbar … managed by the Hindus" (example: Pithoro Pir) (pp. 177–178, folios 164–165).
  The book adds: "the Hindus of Sindh are coming to the Sufi mausoleums since the nineteenth century" (p. 183, folio 170). "The large majority of the Hindus used to pay a visit to the Sufi shrines, but does it mean they are Sufi? Of course not. It does mean that, for them, the Sufis are a part of their religious legacy" (pp. 192–193, folios 179–180). The Hindus of Sindh in Pakistan incorporate the Sufi legacy through processes that "depend on the social milieu, the caste system and the urban/rural context" (p. 194, folio 181).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: present day, contrasted with the post-1947 darbars in India; author's argument / ethnographic present): the Sindhi darbars of India "are not centred on tombs, unlike those of Sindh" (p. 131, folio 118). In India the photographic portrait takes over the sacredness of the space "in place of the tomb, as is the case in the dargahs of Sindh" (p. 132, folio 119). The shahnais are "very common in any religious ritual performed in Sindh" (p. 145, folio 132). In India "a number of typical Sufi rituals performed in Sindh have been suppressed, such as the alam or the dhamal" (p. 152, folio 139).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: period not stated, in the discussion of the Lal Kunwar custom that "disappeared during the partition in 1947"; author's argument): "The colour saffron, which corresponds in Sindh to a bright red, is both a religious symbol and a symbol of marriage for the Daryapanthis" (p. 181, folio 168).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: "late-nineteenth-century Sindh", "the nineteenth century", "nineteenth-century Sindh"; author's argument with quoted sources Narsain and Kalhoro): "Krishna's worship had already developed in late-nineteenth-century Sindh among the Amils. Narsain states that, in the nineteenth century, the Vallabhachari sect was accepted by some members of the Amil community (Narsain 1932: 1939)" (p. 135, folio 122). "A number of Sufis from nineteenth-century Sindh, such as Sachal, Qutub Ali Shah and Bedil, themselves include Krishna in their Sufi poetry … despite the fact that Sindh was Muslim dominated, the Krishnaite Bhakti was influential among wujudi Sufis" (p. 198, folio 185). An endnote adds that wall paintings of Krishna in Hindu temples "could mean there was a kind of Krishnaite revival in the nineteenth century among the Sindhi Hindus (Kalhoro 2014: 238–44)" (Ch. 3 n. 19, p. 213, folio 200).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "a modern Sindhi province"; refers to: period not stated beyond the past tense of the argument about why Hindus took Muslim Sufi masters; author's argument): "Sindh was a largely Muslim-dominated country, making up about 75 per cent of the whole population, where the power had been in the hands of Muslims for centuries. Therefore, there was a kind of 'cultural islamization' working beyond the religious affiliation itself, to which the Hindus were automatically and unconsciously surrendering". Also: "the Muslim Sufi market was far more developed than the Hindu market". And: "to be a Sufi for a Hindu was to participate fully in the process of building a modern Sindhi province, so far as the Sufi poetry was itself very open, if not integrative" (p. 199, folio 186).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: "the late nineteenth century", "late nineteenth-century Sindh", "before partition", "up through partition"; author's argument): on how the Sufi legacy "was constructed in Sindh", where Hindus could still play a ritual role at Sufi places: "In Sindh, it was possible thanks to an ideological framework provided by the wujudi ideology, reformulated in the Theosophical perspective through its congruence with advaita vedanta. Consequently, this construction was expressed in the late nineteenth century" (p. 204, folio 191). "As it was for the majority of the Sindhi literati, be they Hindu or Muslim, the ideology of the society" (the Theosophical Society, named in the preceding sentence) "provided the framework for a reinterpretation of the Sufi legacy of Sindh". This held "for most of the Sindhi intellectuals before partition" (p. 157, folio 144). The book also notes "the key role played by the Hindus of Sindh in the transmission of the Sufi legacy up through partition" (p. 170, folio 157), and that in publishing Sufi books the Hindu Sindhis "already played a leading role in this process in late nineteenth-century Sindh" (p. 173, folio 160).
- (book's word: "pre-partition Sindh"; refers to: before Partition and the present ("still"); "Since the eighteenth century onwards" in the endnote; author's argument): "The Lohanas, once the dominant Hindu community in pre-partition Sindh, are still mostly involved in trade. Many Lohanas, especially those belonging to the subcaste of the Amils, have played a leading role in integrating Sufism as a main component of Sindhi identity" (p. 193, folio 180). Endnote: "The Amils were a subcaste of the Lohanas. Since the eighteenth century onwards, they specialized in becoming the official scribes in the administration of the Kalhoras and the Talpurs" (Ch. 4 n. 10, p. 214, folio 201). This note does not itself name Sindh.
- (book's word: "Sindh", "Southern Sindh"; refers to: pre-Partition, "from 1947 onwards", and "April of 2016"; author's argument, endnotes, author's anecdote): "Jhulelal was already worshipped by some segments among the Hindus of Sindh, but as we saw before, they were in majority Nanakpanthis" (p. 158, folio 145). Two endnotes on the same page: "the pre-partition temples devoted to Jhulelal in Sindh were built with a dome, as were some Sikh samadhis" (Ch. 2 n. 5), and "The Thakurs were the priests of Jhulelal. In Sindh, they were settled in different cities but they migrated to India from 1947 onwards" (Ch. 2 n. 6) (p. 211, folio 198). In April 2016 the author and a team "toured in Southern Sindh to map out the different shrines devoted to Jhulelal in Southern Sindh" (p. 184, folio 171).
- (book's word: "pre-partition Sindh"; refers to: before Partition, contrasted with "independent India"; author's argument): "While in pre-partition Sindh, there are many stories where businessmen decided to give up all of their worldly goods and activities to become renunciants, and not only in relation with Sufism, I have never heard such a story in independent India" (p. 205, folio 192).
- (book's word: "Norther [text-layer?] [Northern] Sindh"; refers to: Sachal Sarmast's following before Partition, period not stated, up to the migration "in the wake of partition"; author's argument): Sachal "had a large number of followers among the Hindu trade castes of Norther [text-layer?] [Northern] Sindh, and some of them transferred their love for singing Sachal's poetry to India when they migrated in the wake of partition" (p. 150, folio 137).
- (book's word: "Northern Sindh"; refers to: present day, with "the Middle Ages" in the endnote; author's argument): Siraiki is "the Sindhi dialect mostly spoken in Northern Sindh, and especially among the Baluchi population", and "many Sindhi Sufi poets, especially from Northern Sindh, composed Sufi poetry in several languages" (p. 171, folio 158). The glossary gives Siraiki as "a language spoken in Northern Sindh and Southern Punjab" (p. 220, folio 207). An endnote says the Siraiki-speaking area of Southern Punjab "was a part of Sindh during the Middle Ages" (Ch. 6 n. 11, p. 216, folio 203). That is the book's own statement of a differently bounded historical Sindh. See Doubts.
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: present day, the author's 2016 visit; author's anecdote): Thano Bula Khan "is located in the Kohistan, the western part of Sindh that stands close to Baluchistan" (p. 184, folio 171).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: present day; ethnographic present, reporting the pir of Pithoro): among Pithoro Pir's disciples, "Among the Muslims of Sindh, one can take note of Sindhis like Sammas, Panhwars and Baluchs like the Maris and the Legharis". "The disciples of Pithoro Pir mix in Sindh, whereas in Kuch [text-layer?] [Kutch], the untouchable ones practise separate worship" (p. 191, folio 178). Ramdeo Pir is "still known in Sindh as Rama Pir … In Sindh, he is today integrated into the temples dedicated to the pan-Indian divinities" (p. 192, folio 179).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "the province of Sindh", "Pakistani Hindus of Sindh"; refers to: "from 1947 onwards", "Since partition", and the present ("now"); author's argument): "Although a large majority of Hindus have migrated to India from 1947 onwards, Sindh is the province of Pakistan with the most significant Hindu population. The province of Sindh now belongs to Pakistan, a country that has been through different steps of islamization" (pp. 176–177, folios not stated and 164). "In Pakistan, despite the mass migration, the Sindhi Hindus number several millions, with a majority of them belonging to the so-called scheduled castes, mostly Menghwars, Bhils and Kohlis" (p. 175, folio 162). "Since partition, the majority of the Pakistani Hindus of Sindh have belonged to the low castes. They are Dalits, as they are named by some, and some even claim they are not Hindu", and "the Sufi legacy is still alive among the Dalits of Sindh" (p. 194, folio 181). "In Sindh now, there is a debate among the Hindus to determine if the term 'Dalit' is relevant" (p. 187, folio 174).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: present day, "Seventy years after partition", with Pakistan's islamization "since the late 1980s"; author's argument): "A possible explanation is that the Hindu population of Pakistan, including Sindh, is more and more under the threat of the radical Muslims of the country" (p. 127, folio 114). "Despite the islamization, though, and despite the spread of Islamist terrorism in Sindh as in other parts of Pakistan, I argue that the Sufi culture still pervades Sindhi society". He aims "to balance the representation suggesting that the integrative function performed by Sufism is currently weakened in Sindh". He also writes: "Sufism does not organically imply tolerance towards other religions. For example, the most active people working for the conversion of young Hindu girls to Islam are well-known Sufi pirs" (p. 177, folio 164). Elsewhere: "the Sufi dargahs are now targets of Islamist terrorism" (p. 186, folio 173).
- (book's word: "the Hindus of Sindh", "the Lohanas of Sindh"; refers to: present day, "for some years"; author's argument / ethnographic present): the formula Sab ka malik ek hai "has for some years been very popular among the Hindus of Sindh" and "signifies a new policy implemented by the Hindus of Sindh" (p. 186, folio 173). The Hindu dargah "shows that the Hindus of Sindh are strongly related to their Sufi legacy". The book places this "case that concerns the middle and high Hindu classes of Sindh" (p. 187, folio 174).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: after 1947, present day; author's argument about Sindh as seen from the darbars in India): Rochaldas's darbar is made "a territory that extends from an original sacred territory which is located in Sindh, the holy city of Sehwan Sharif". Invoking Jhok Sharif "contributes to the construction of a network of sacred territories which are all located in Sindh" (p. 141, folio 128). The mehndi of Lal Shahbaz "makes it possible to maintain a direct link through the ritual with the lost territory of Sindh" (p. 147, folio 134). Singing Sindhi Sufi poetry keeps "a link, through language and music, with Sindh as a fatherland" (p. 205, folio 192). The "'living' link" is "with the Master who stays in Sindh, nowadays in Pakistan" (p. 203, folio 190).
- (book's word: "Sind", "Sindh"; refers to: Ram Panjwani's view, "in 1945, thus before migrating to India" and in his 1987 book; quoted source Ram Panjwani, *Sindhi ain Sindhiyaat*, 1987, with the author's gloss): "Already, in Sindh, Panjwani was convinced that Sufism was the core of Sindhi culture, a culture shared by all creeds and social milieus" (p. 157, folio 144). His preface aims to acquaint Sindhis "with their past and the glorious cultural heritage of Sind which was inhabited by their forefathers" (p. 159, folio 146). "Panjwani's vision of Sindh is that of 'a land of the mystic poets, who are humanists as well'" (p. 160, folio 147). "When Panjwani referred to the religious culture of Sindh, he was referring to the spirit of Sufism" (p. 161, folio 148).

## From Jürgen Schaflechner, Hinglaj Devi

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/schaflechner_hinglaj_devi.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

The book's forms: `Lal Shahbaz Qalandar`, then `Lal Shahbaz`, `Shahbaz`, `Qalandar`; index
`Lal Shahbaz Qalandar, 148` and, separately, `Qalandar, Lal Shahbaz, 97–98, 179` (pp. 107, 108, 160,
191, 351, 357, folios not stated).

- **Saint**
  - **No death date, no dates of any kind and no silsila for him anywhere in the book**: **(not
    stated)**.
- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **The shrine is named with a district that disagrees with the archive.** "One example is provided
    by the dargāh (Urd. shrine, tomb of a saint) of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar in Sehwan Sharif in the Dadu
    district." (p. 107, folio not stated) **`shrine_index.tsv` gives the row's location as "Sehwan
    Sharif, Jamshoro District, Sindh". Both are recorded; neither is corrected. See Doubts.**
