  - **No tomb description, no building, no date, no custodian and no revenue**: **(not stated)**.
- **Practices & Events**
  - **Shared Hindu–Muslim use.** "Today, local and translocal Hindu and Muslim communities alike visit
    this shrine regularly, and both take part in the worship of the saint (Boivin 2012, 290)."
    (pp. 107–108, folio not stated)
  - **Two Hindu families in the ʿurs ritual.** "During the curs[text-layer?] [ʿurs] (Urd. celebration,
    wedding of the saint with God) of Lal Shahbaz, two Hindu families play a crucial role in the
    arrangement of the henna (Hin. mehaṁdī) ceremony at the shrine. According to the legend, it was
    Shahbaz himself who arranged this process and appointed, in addition to the local Muslim elite, two
    Hindu families to take part in it (Boivin 2012, 290)." (p. 108, folio not stated) **Neither family
    is named and no ʿurs date is given**: **(not stated)**. **This is the only ʿurs named anywhere in
    the book.**
  - **An annual walking pilgrimage to it, abandoned for Hinglaj in 2004.** "Before this, he walked
    annually to the `curs`[text-layer?] [ʿurs] of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar where, according to his own
    account, he had gone for eleven subsequent years. Raj frequently went together with members of the
    Christian and Hindu paṃcāyat from his neighborhood. Eventually he stopped going to Sehwan and
    instead started to go to Hinglaj." (pp. 160–161, folios not stated) In Raj Kumar's own words, from
    a 2012 interview: "We set out walking from Liari [on our way to Sehwan Sharif] and kept our luggage
    on a camel, [and also] rations [Engl.], water . . . It was my first year. We went with the
    Meghwarji—the Meghwarjis from next to our neighborhood. We were five guys. So I thought to myself:
    'Man, forget Qalandar! Again we are climbing the mountains for Noorani. The Mother's [Hinglaj] road
    is all straight. It is by road [Engl.].' I said: 'We have to go this way!' So the Qalandar was out
    [lit.: died] and I left from there [to Hinglaj].'" (p. 160, folio not stated) The route: "The foot
    journey from Liari to Sehwan meant a long and grueling trek through the Pab mountain chain, a
    relatively arduous and exhausting path in comparison to the route to Hinglaj along the highway."
    (p. 161, folio not stated) The author's reading: "Raj Kumar and his fellow travelers changed not
    only their route but even their final destination in 2004 due to the existence of an easier, 'by
    road' walking route to Hinglaj." (p. 161, folio not stated) And: "As confirmed by Raj Kumar, one of
    the walking pilgrims from Narayanpur, the easy road along the highway was an incentive to change
    his annual pilgrimage from Lal Shahbaz to Hinglaj Devi." (p. 191, folio not stated) **The
    untranslated original of the interview is printed in Chapter 4's note 2** (p. 192, folio not
    stated) — see Doubts, since the book states no referent for `qalaṃdar` or `nūrānī` there.
- **Significance**
  - **The head of a pilgrimage route into Balochistan.** "Lal Shahbaz is also the starting point for
    the pilgrimage to Lahoot la Makan, the abode of the saint Shah Noorani in the Khuzdar district in
    Balochistan." (p. 108, folio not stated) A 2010 encounter confirms the route is still used: "In
    2010, I met a Punjabi pilgrim in Lahoot la Makan who had just arrived from Sehwan Sharif and
    confirmed that this side of the pilgrimage route is still frequented; however, he mentioned that
    the longer path to Hinglaj is no longer used due to the dangerous political situation in the area."
    (p. 147, folio not stated)
  - **Index evidence only, and the two runs must not be merged.** `Lal Shahbaz Qalandar, 148` (p. 351,
    folio not stated) and `Qalandar, Lal Shahbaz, 97–98, 179` (p. 357, folio not stated), **with no
    cross-reference between them and no overlap in the runs.** See Doubts.

### Doubts the notes raise on this row

For **`shaktipeeth-shri-hinglaj-mata-mandir`**: its district, tehsil, road address or coordinates; any
foundation date, architect or patron for the temple, the enclosure, the platform, the tunnel masonry
or the outdoor yajña kuṃḍ; the date of the tiling of the oblong stone; the date the eyes and noses
were attached to the two round stones; the date of either inscription and the donor's name; the exact
age of the enclosure, the mud hill and the tunnel; the HSM's officers, membership and legal form
beyond "Trust"; Vasrup Chand Tulsi's dates and anything else about him; Gopal Maharaj's dates, caste,
lineage, predecessor or appointment beyond 2008; Dost Mohammad's given name, office title, birth year
and exact year of death, and Hussain's dates; any sajjāda nashīn, mutawalli, waqf, Auqaf, endowment,
land grant or revenue total — **the book names no Auqaf or waqf involvement of any kind, only the
Balochistan provincial government as funder and private Hindu donors**; any absolute annual visitor
count; any calendar date, in any era, for the phālgun/caitra melā, or its duration, or the old and new
dates the Hingol bridge changed; the names and dates of the three unnamed annual festivals; which ʿīd
the autumn "`cīd` celebrations" are; the month of the 2004 sacrifice ban, the body that passed it and
its terms; the "functional yet ultimately contingent events" that prompted it; who introduced the
ear-cutting substitute and when; the price of the bhag'vā paṭṭī; the fate of the 100-rupee police
levy after 2013; any named politician or sum behind the bhanḍārā donations; the completion date of the
Makran Coastal Highway, the Hingol bridge and the dam proposal; which three holy places WAPDA offered
to relocate; who made the spackle-filled sun and moon; the year of the child's drowning at Sharan Kund
and of the mother-and-four-children drowning at Til Kund; the year the Melari Mata booth was begun and
the year of the Momai Mata vision; the name and location of the Kutch Ashapura temple; the text, date
and location of any Thatta inscription; and any translation of the Sindhi of notes 17 and 24 or the
Hindi/Urdu of notes 2, 8, 28, 29, 31, 32 and 42. For **`chandragup-baba-chandragup`**: any deity,
temple, image, custodian, priest, district or date of its own. For **`bhit-bhit-shah`**: his silsila,
tomb description, ʿurs, custodian, and what in his work concerns Hinglaj. For **`lal-shahbaz-qalandar`**:
his dates, silsila, ʿurs date, tomb description and the names of the two Hindu families. For
**`shah-noorani-shrine-syed-bilawal-shah-noorani`**: his death date, silsila, tomb and any ʿurs. For
**`sadh-belo-sadh-belo-island-temple`**: the mahaṃt's name, dates and lineage, and any date for the
visit. For **`ramapir-temple-tando-allahyar`**: Ramdevji's death year, silsila or sampradaya, and any
description of the temple. For **`shri-swaminarayan-mandir-karachi`**: its founder's name, its
foundation date, its custodian and any ritual. For **`shrine-of-pir-mangho`**: the saint's name, dates,
silsila, custodian and any rite. All **(not stated)**.

- **`lal-shahbaz-qalandar`: the index files him twice and the runs must not be merged.**
  `Lal Shahbaz Qalandar, 148` (p. 351) and `Qalandar, Lal Shahbaz, 97–98, 179` (p. 357), folio not
  stated, **with no cross-reference and no overlap.** Whether this is one entry split by an indexing
  error or two deliberate entries is **(not stated)**. Note also that `shamsabad` (Shams Ali
  Qalandar), `shrine-of-qalandar-baba-auliya` and `abul-faiz-qalander-ali-suharwardi` all contain the
  string "Qalandar", **so a string match on that word alone would fire four rows**; the full name here
  is unambiguous.

### Place-level material: Sehwan Sharif / Sehwan

*The book's place-level material on "Sehwan Sharif / Sehwan" (2 rows), from its list of rows attached at place or person level, copied into every existing entry that list names (ruling (a); Rauf, 10 October 2026). It is about the place in general, not about this shrine alone.*

- **Sehwan Sharif / Sehwan** — **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**, **shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar** (2 rows)
  - Sehwan is the dargāh town of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar (p. 107, folio not stated), the destination Raj
    Kumar abandoned for Hinglaj (pp. 160–161, folios not stated), a landscape reference in "the
    mountains of Sehwan" (p. 113, folio not stated), the starting point of a 2010 pilgrim met at
    Lahoot la Makan (p. 147, folio not stated), and the index headword `Sehwan, 97, 104, 148–149`
    (p. 359, folio not stated). **`shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar` takes the id at place level only: its
    `location_short` reads "Laki (Lakhi), near Sehwan Sharif", i.e. near the town rather than in it,
    and the book never names Lakhi Shah Saddar. See Doubts.**

### Province-level material: sindh

*The book's `sindh` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: "Today", the fieldwork present; author's argument, with NGO figures): Sindh is "home to the majority of Pakistan's Hindus", and "The country's instability, the feudal environment in Sindh ... or perhaps a simple lack of interest" have kept scholars away. Of Pakistan's Hindus ("between 6 and 10 million people—an estimate that varies widely according to the source"), "over 90 percent—belong to the underprivileged Hindu tribes or sub-castes, such as the Meghvar, the Bhil, and the Kohli, who frequently work as manual laborers for the powerful landowning elite in Sindh" (p. 13, folio not stated). The 6-to-10-million estimate comes from "the Human Rights Commission of Pakistan (HRCP) in Sindh" (p. 28, folio not stated, endnote 1). "In Sindh, the Kohli community is mainly a disempowered scheduled caste whose members work as agricultural peasants on the fields owned by Sindh's feudal elite"; Kohlis, Meghvar and Bhil make up most festival visitors, "which also reflects the overall population of Hindus in Sindh, and more than 90 percent originate from these communities" (p. 154, folio not stated). "according to nongovernmental organizations (NGOs) in Sindh, the biggest bulk of Pakistani Hindus, over 90 percent, consist of so-called scheduled caste communities" (p. 276, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "the Sindh province", "Sindh"; refers to: "Currently" for the seats and "within the past decade" for the restorations, i.e. the fieldwork years; endnote and author's argument): "the Sindh province has 9 minority seats out of 168" (p. 28, folio not stated, endnote 5). Government funding for minority community places "is seen in a wave of restorations of temples across Sindh within the past decade"; "While in Sindh, I visited many temples that were renovated within the past decade, coinciding with changes in the political landscape" (p. 16, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "rural Sindh", "Sindh"; refers to: the fieldwork present, and March 2014 for the rumour; author's argument and reported rumour): "In rural Sindh, in particular, the clan or the barādarī serves as a guarantee of security rather than the nation state" (p. 18, folio not stated). In one of the rumours about the March 2014 Larkana temple attack, recounted by NGO friends in Hyderabad: "According to tradition in Sindh, the culprits took the Quran and asked the Hindu family for remission" (p. 17, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "the province of Sindh"; refers to: Partition and "Today in Pakistan"; author's argument): "The partition of the subcontinent brought major changes to the province of Sindh. The departure of the Hindu elite and the influx of Muslim refugees (“Muhajirs”) from India dramatically altered the local demography, and the Sindhi community became increasingly defined by its perception of being ostracized and subjugated by Muhajir power. Today in Pakistan, one commonly hears Sindhi patriots and nationalists lamenting the degenerated state of the province"; in these discourses, "found on both sides of the border", Sindh is "presented as a distinct entity lacking any cultural links to other provinces or the state of Pakistan generally" (p. 87, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: after Partition, from an exile's point of view; quoted source, K. R. Malkani, *The Sindh Story* (1997), "a Sindhi in exile who, as a Hindu, left Hyderabad after Partition and settled in New Delhi"): "As for the state of Pakistan, Sindh rejects it wholly. Sindh has always been there, Pakistan is a passing show. Sindh is a fact, Pakistan is a fiction ... [sic]" (Malkani 1997, 134); "A Sufi Sindh and an Islamic Pakistan cannot coexist ... If Pakistan continues, Sindh will die" (Malkani 1997, 136). The author calls the book full of "rants against Pakistan" (p. 87, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: "the time before the British invasion", as nationalist writers describe it; quoted source, Hussain Soomro 2009, and author's argument): Sindhi nationalist discourse also blames "British rule, which they view as having victimized Sindh"; Soomro: "Sindh was so prosperous that it was commonly described that there is abundance of milk and honey in Sindh ... [sic]" (Soomro 2009, 3). These narratives make Pakistan and the British "the culprits in the case of lost harmony and affluence in Sindh" (pp. 87–88, folio not stated). In the chapter conclusion, Sindhi nationalist discourse links Hinglaj "to a “real” Sindh that prospered before outsiders began suppressing the province" (p. 97, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sindhi nationalism", "Sind"; refers to: "the 1920s and 1930s" and "the 1947 publication", as reported from Verkaaik 2010; quoted source, Oscar Verkaaik): Sindhi nationalist narratives are organized around the "Sindhi language, the poetry of [national poet] Shah Abdul Latif, the Indus, the peasant or hari, and above all, Sufism and mysticism" (Verkaaik 2010, 197), plus the link to Mohenjo-Daro. Verkaaik "describes Sindhi nationalism as a relatively new phenomenon that began with the struggle for independence from the Bombay Presidency in the 1920s and 1930s"; important to its later growth was "the 1947 publication, Save Sind, Save the Continent, by Ibrahmin Joyo[text-layer?] [Ibrahim Joyo?]" (p. 88, folio not stated). (The book's own phrase for this unit is "the Bombay Presidency"; the book adds no boundary history.)
- (book's word: "the province of Sindh"; refers to: G. M. Syed's lifetime "(1904–1995)" and the present ("to this day"); quoted source, Verkaaik 2004 and 2010, on Syed): Syed, a politician "who spent many years in prison for his separatist views on Sindh and Pakistan, directly linked the province of Sindh with a tradition of Sufism"; he finds Sufism "in its purest form in the province of Sindh", proven "by the various shrines of Sufi saints that are, to this day, visited by Hindus and Muslims alike" (pp. 88–89, folio not stated). Verkaaik: "both Sindhi Hindus and Muslims were influenced by mysticism and religious tolerance. This was the Sindhi essence that both Hindus and Muslims shared" (p. 89, folio not stated). According to Verkaaik 2004, it was Syed "who held primary responsibility for generating Bhittai's role as the most important poet of Sindh" (p. 101, folio not stated, endnote 52).
- (book's word: "the history of Sindh"; refers to: "today", as a common notion; author's argument): writers stress "the common notion among Sindhis today that in the history of Sindh, there is no religious tension between the two communities—Muslims and Hindus. This notion of the harmonious coexistence of the religions, however, is merely a nationalist utopian ideal, as Aggarwal (2013) has shown" (pp. 90–91, folio not stated). The chapter plan names the shrine as "a hallmark of Sindh's “golden past”" for "modern-day Sindhi nationalism" (p. 51, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: "five thousand years ago" and "the ancient civilization of Sindh", as the Sindhi writer claims; quoted source, Ishtiaq Ansari 2001, reported critically): Ansari "directly associates the ancient civilization of Mohenjo-Daro with today's Sindhi people"; he claims "the composition of the Veda occurred on the shores of the Indus", that Shiva and the Mother Goddess "were already known and honored in the ancient civilization of Sindh", and that Goddess veneration "was ... already in place in Sindh before the Aryans conquered the area “five thousand years ago”" (Ansari 2001, 154) (p. 93, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "real Sindh" (Sin. aṣal sindh); refers to: an undated ancient past, as Abro claims, and the present; quoted source, Badar Abro 1991): "Sindhis once lived in the valleys between Rohri and east Balochistan, an area he defines as the “real Sindh” ... that was once “prosperous”" (Abro 1991, 22); "Since Sindh remained under the influence of Sufi mysticism ... the area is “fortunately in terms of faith, secular [in English]”" (Abro 1991, 23), so the religious places of the region belong to "the collective heritage of a “real Sindh”" (p. 94, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sindhi", "the Sindhi"; refers to: "this modern age" and Latif's eighteenth-century journey; quoted sources, Abdul Qadir Mangi in Kanasro 2007, Khadim Soomro 2002, Agha 2004): Mangi: "Every Sindhi, of whatever faith he may be, believes in the greatness of Bhitai. From a Kohli Bheel to a Syed, every one considers Bhitai his spiritual guide" (p. 95, folio not stated). Sindhi nationalists see Latif "as the ideal Sindhi—the perfect example for the tolerant and “syncretic” history of Sindh", while "more orthodox writers aim to explain away Latif's journey as “youthful recklessness”" (p. 95, folio not stated). Khadim Soomro: "A Sindhi has a special place in human civilization because he stands for good and liberal attitude and behavior" (p. 96, folio not stated). Latif is dated "(1690–1751)" here (p. 95).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "The south of Pakistan, and especially Sindh"; refers to: the fieldwork present; author's argument): "The south of Pakistan, and especially Sindh, offers a whole range of salvific spaces that are frequently visited by different religious communities and form a network of pilgrimage routes or ritual journeys. Sindhi nationalists often emphasize these characteristics of Sindh as a landscape of “coexistence”" (p. 107, folio not stated). Legends linking Latif and the Goddess "connect Hindu and Muslim sacred spaces in Sindh and show that imagined landscapes do not necessarily have to be separated using clear-cut religious taxonomies" (p. 109, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sindh Hindoos", "Sindh", "Sind Hindus"; refers to: Burton "1851", McMurdo via Mubarak 1985, Thakur 1997, and "the past few centuries" up to today; quoted sources): Burton: "a description of them [the Lohanas], therefore, will be a general portrait of the Sindh Hindoos" (Burton 1851, 314); McMurdo: "The Lowannas or Lohannas compose the great body of Hindus in Sindh" (Mubarak 1985, 55); "Over the past few centuries, the Lohana community absorbed many other groups and clans, now encompassing a significant portion of the upper classes of Pakistani Hindus" (p. 79, folio not stated). Thakur: Lohanas form "the general body of the Sind Hindus" (p. 80, folio not stated). "Due to the heterogeneity of the Lohanas in Sindh, it is not possible to make any universal claim for the whole community" (p. 216, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: "the eighteenth century" (Kalhora rule), "the nineteenth century", and "Around the eighteenth or nineteenth century"; book's historical prose, citing Lachaier 2008, Cook 2010, Lari 1994, and Zahid-Khan quoted in Cook): "in the nineteenth century, the Lohanas were the most important trading community in the areas of Sindh, Kutch, and Saurashtra (Lachaier 2008, 82). One reason for this was the established rule of the Kalhoras in Sindh in the eighteenth century, which brought new business opportunities for merchant professions (Cook 2010, 140)" (p. 80, folio not stated). Zahid-Khan "attributes this merging of different groups to the small number of non-Muslim Sindhis who united in the face of the majority"; "Sindh's location between trading routes connecting Afghanistan, Khorasan, Arabia, Persia, Turkey, and other places made the province appealing to a wide array of merchants from the entire continent"; "Around the eighteenth or nineteenth century, a separation occurred within these merchant groups of Sindh": Lohanas working for local rulers became "Amils", "initially 10 to 15 percent of the Lohanas (Lari 1994, 211)", and those who kept trading were "Bhaiband" (p. 81, folio not stated). (Kutch and Saurashtra are named alongside Sindh; see `not attached`.)
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: the fieldwork present, plus the censuses of "1881" and "1891"; author's argument citing Lari 1994 and Thakur 1997): "Many Lohanas in Sindh follow the teachings of Nanak, somewhat obscuring their religious affiliation for the colonial officers. Hence, in the 1881 census, Lohanas from Shikarpur and Hyderabad were counted as Sikhs, while ten years later, in the 1891 census, they were classified as Hindus (Lari 1994, 211f.)" (p. 80, folio not stated). "many middle- and upper-class Hindus in Sindh are followers of the Nanak paṃth" (p. 257, folio not stated); "Many upper-caste Hindus in Sindh, especially from the Lohana community, refer to Nanak and his teachings (Thakur 1997, 141), and it is common to cover one's head before walking into a tikānā (Sin.), the temple containing the Guru Granth Sahib" (p. 286, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sind", "Sindh", "other provinces of Pakistan"; refers to: Thakur's *Sindhi Culture* "(1951)", cited as Thakur 1997, and the fieldwork conversations; quoted source and ethnographic present): Thakur writes that the "[c]aste system is almost absent in Sind" (Thakur 1997, 25), a perception the author links to "the assimilative role of the Sindhi Lohana jāti" and to "Sindhi nationalistic discourses, which portray Sindh as a place of religious harmony and tolerance". Devoted Sindhi Hindu nationalists told him caste discrimination "exists today only in India or other provinces of Pakistan"; "Many upper-caste Sindhi Hindus often deny or ignore acts of segregation", which "Dalit organizations" fiercely oppose (pp. 277–278, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: the fieldwork present; author's argument and ethnographic present): the HSM's idea of progress and education "stands in sharp contrast to low-caste rituals in Sindh such as the sacrifice of animals or the consumption of alcohol as prasād" (p. 278, folio not stated); "many other Hindu groups in Sindh, and in fact all over Pakistan, sacrifice animals for the fulfillment of certain wishes or during particular rites of passage" (p. 310, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: Burton, "1851", colonial; quoted source, Burton, via endnotes): Burton "confirms the unusual use of the term “Vaishiya” in Sindh, which is mainly applied “in a limited sense to signify operatives and mechanics, opposed to merchants and shopkeepers”" (Burton 1851, 237) (pp. 98–99, folio not stated, endnote 16). Burton says the Saraswat Brahmins, many of whom were thought to be low caste, "were known for their devotion to the Devi who, in most areas of Sindh, was called Hinglaj (Burton 1851, 312)" (p. 100, folio not stated, endnote 28).
- (book's word: "the province of Sindh", "the Sindh", "the people of Sindh"; refers to: mythic time in the Brahmakshatriya story, and for the Charani Avar a birth "in the year 888 CE" and Huns "dated to the eighth century by Samaur", both as the sources state; mytho-historic narrative, as reported by the author): in the Brahmakshatriya story, Parashurama "arrives in the province of Sindh" to kill Kshatriyas, and "The mighty king Ratnasena, the ruler of Sindh" flees (pp. 61–62, folio not stated). In the Charan story of Avar, famine drives her family "to go to Sindh, then ruled by Umar Sumra who had recently converted to Islam and was now known by the name of “Hamir”". The Samma and Bhati Rajput kings, who "had wanted to occupy the Sindh for some time", help Avar kill him and "then divided the Sindh between them and ruled in peace". Another story has Avar kill "fifty-two of these Hun-demons" and so save "the people of Sindh" (Samaur 1999, 509) (p. 72, folio not stated). These are legends, not historical statements.
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: "for many generations" before Partition, and after it; ethnographic present, Vagri oral history): "According to Vagri history, the community migrated back and forth between Sindh and Gujarat for many generations and, like other nomadic groups, were eventually forced to settle on one side of the border after the Partition. Since there is no written account of their movement, the clan's detailed history can only be based on speculation" (p. 308, folio not stated).

## From Pnina Werbner and Helene Basu (eds.), Embodying Charisma

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/werbner_basu_embodying_charisma.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

- Named **once in the whole book**, as a poster in the langar of Mama Ji's darbar in Rawalpindi:
  "Other religious posters include portraits of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar (Sehwan) and Sain Kanwan Wali
  (Gujrat)" (p. 160, folio 149).
- **That is the whole evidence**: no date, no silsila, nothing about Sehwan beyond the parenthesis —
  all **(not stated)**. The value of the bullet is as evidence of his cult's reach into a Rawalpindi
  majzub's shrine, not as information about Sehwan.

### Doubts the notes raise on this row

- **`lal-shahbaz-qalandar` — one poster on a wall** (p. 160, folio 149). Useful as evidence of cult
  reach, not as information about Sehwan.

### Province-level material: sindh

*The book's `sindh` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: period not stated; contributor's argument, Jamal Malik, ch. 9): the holy-man cult is "particularly evident in the countryside, where major landlords and large-scale land concentrations are crucial features, especially in Punjab and Sindh"; "the local pîr represents the local landlord or is at least closely related to him" (p. 199, folio 188).
- (book's word: "the Sind"; refers to: British rule — "against the British" — with pir Pagara's present authority "in present-day Pakistan"; no years given; contributor's endnote, Charles Lindholm, ch. 10, n. 12): to his argument that in South Asia under indirect rule "Sufis were usually equally content to maintain the peace as long as they were allowed to continue their religious routines and keep their estates and authority" (p. 237, folio 226), Lindholm notes: "Exceptions include the two Hur rebellions against the British in the Sind by the followers of pir Pagara, a landlord, political leader and hereditary head of a Sufi brotherhood who still retains great authority in present-day Pakistan (see Lambrick 1972 for a fictionalized account)" (pp. 241–242, folios 230–231). "the Sind" is the book's form, carried as printed; no boundary is supplied.

## From Shemeem Burney Abbas, The Female Voice in Sufi Ritual

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/abbas_female_voice_sufi_ritual.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

The book's most fully described shrine. Its names for it: "Hazrat Lal Shahbaz Qalandar", "the Hazrat
Lal Shahbaz Qalandar shrine at Sehwan Sharif", "the Lal Shahbaz Qalandar shrine in Sehwan", "the
Shahbaz Qalandar shrine", "Qalandar Lal Shahbaz".

- **Saint**
  - "Hazrat Lal Shahbaz Qalandar, a Sufi mystic of Sind" (p. 57, folio not stated). **No death date,
    no birth date, no silsila, no lineage and no other name are given anywhere in the eleven chunks:
    all (not stated).** "Usman Marwandi", 1274 and "Qalandari" appear nowhere and must not be imported.
  - "The Sufi saint is known as a jalālī pir, a mystic with intense supernatural energy" (p. 57, folio
    not stated); "Hazrat Lal Shahbaz Qalandar's shrine is reputed for the jalālī or intense
    supernatural energy of the Sufi", as against the "jamālī or aesthetic mystics" (p. 96, folio not
    stated); and, from an endnote, "Hazrat Lal Shahbaz Qalandar of Sehwan is also known for his jalālī
    or supernatural powers, and his shrine is called a jalālī shrine" (p. 197, folio not stated).
  - He is filed by the index under `Suﬁ Saints belonging to Pakistan-Indian silsilās`, with printed
    locator `xix` and sub-entries `dhammāl`, `faqīrs`, `jalāl`, `jalālī`, `''mast qalandar''
    invocation` and `Sehwan Sharif` — **every Arabic locator destroyed** (p. 239, folio not stated).
- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - Named among the five shrines where "qawwālī is performed every evening after the prayers" (p. 42,
    folio not stated); and sufiānā-kalām "is a feature of the shrines in Sind, especially those of
    Hazrat Lal Shahbaz Qalandar and Shah Abdul Latif at Bhit" (p. 85, folio not stated).
  - "Faqīrs (devotees who wear orange robes) perform the ritual dhammāl (dance) every evening at the
    Hazrat Lal Shahbaz Qalandar shrine in Sehwan Sharif" (p. 93, folio not stated). "The large drums
    (naubats), which are played for the dhammāl, can be seen lying in the large courtyard of the
    shrine during the day" (p. 93, folio not stated). The glossary confirms the instrument's use here
    by name (p. 186, folio not stated).
  - "The passionate whirling dance of the faqīrs in a circle is said to express the immense jalāl
    (energy) of Hazrat Lal Shahbaz Qalandar. His devotees believe that many prayers are answered at
    the shrine and many wishes granted" (pp. 93–94, folios not stated).
  - The comparison the book's informants draw: "Informants at Radio Pakistan in Hyderabad compare the
    dhammāl of the faqīrs at this shrine with that of the whirling dervishes of Maulana Rumi in Konya"
    (p. 93, folio not stated); "The dhammāl that the qalandars or the faqīrs perform at the shrine is
    similar to the dance of the whirling dervishes at Maulana Jalaluddin Rumi's shrine in Konya"
    (p. 64, folio not stated).
  - **Gender arrangement of the dhammāl, and its therapeutic use.** "Both men and women participate in
    the dhammāl. Women perform the dance in a certain section of the courtyard. Many times, families
    will bring women who are emotionally disturbed to participate in the dhammāl, which devotees claim
    has healing powers" (p. 66, folio not stated). **Attributed to devotees, not asserted by Abbas.**
  - "I discovered that one can buy cassettes of healing music for emotionally disturbed persons in the
    bazaar outside the shrine" (p. 66, folio not stated).
  - **A woman holds a service role here — the book's one worked example.** "For instance, at the
    Hazrat Lal Shahbaz Qalandar shrine at Sehwan Sharif, the woman faqiriānī or caretaker gives out
    water to the devotees" (p. 160, folio not stated); earlier, "women participated actively in the
    support services at the Lal Shahbaz Qalandar shrine in Sehwan: a female muridīānī gave water to
    the devotees" (p. 20, folio not stated). Abbas frames the act religiously: "In the Islamic
    tradition giving water to the thirsty is a savab (charitable deed). The act of giving water to the
    thirsty is eulogized in the oral Sufi traditions, especially in relation to the Battle of Kerbala"
    (p. 160, folio not stated). **The prose says "faqiriānī", the plate caption for the same service
    says "Murīdīānī"; the book never reconciles them — see Doubts.** Two plates, **figure numbers
    dropped**: "Serving devotees: Murīdīānī at Hazrat Lal Shahbaz Qalandar, Sehwan Sharif" (p. 161,
    folio not stated) and "Guluband of Hazrat Lal Shahbaz Qalandar, Sehwan Sharif" (p. 164, folio not
    stated).
  - **A relic and the ritual around it.** "Among the rituals that I observed was one in which women
    devotees held up glasses of water to seek ritual blessings from the guluband or heart-shaped
    necklace that belonged to Hazrat Lal Shahbaz Qalandar and that hung over his tomb in Sehwan. The
    glasses of water were then shared with their kinswomen and men. To orthodox Muslims this may seem
    to be a fetish, but devotees draw strength from the ritual" (p. 20, folio not stated).
  - **The only Sindhi shrine where the loṭevālī tradition survives**, on Naseer Mirza's testimony:
    "These were gypsy women who only sang at the Shahbaz Qalandar shrine—certainly, that is the only
    place in the Sind now where the loṭevālīs come—to sing there… no loṭevālī passes by these
    shrines—except at Qalandar Lal Shahbaz" (p. 61, folio not stated). Their takings: they "come to
    the Shahbaz Qalandar shrine with a loṭa—and sing there—and make a livelihood on a rupee, two
    rupee—the alms they get there" (p. 61, folio not stated).
  - Abbas's guide on her first visit "was a senior librarian at the Institute of Sindhology at Sind
    University in Jamshoro, Hyderabad. Although an orthodox Muslim, he was a devotee of the Sufi
    saint" (p. 20, folio not stated). **He is not named: (not stated).**
  - **Her fieldwork year here is destroyed.** "During my fieldwork in Sind at the Hazrat Lal Shahbaz
    Qalandar shrine in [text-layer?: the year did not extract — a blank stands where a numeral
    belongs], I studied women roving minstrels who sing devotional narratives to the Sufi saints"
    (p. 56, folio not stated).
  - No custodian is named, no sajjada nashin, no Auqaf, no endowment, no building date, no ʿurs date
    and no visitor number: all **(not stated)**.
- **Practices & Events**
  - **The ʿurs and what it does to the town.** "during the urs celebrations, which last for more than
    a week, it is almost impossible to walk in the narrow streets of the city", reported by "Women
    informants who live around the heavily populated area of the shrine" (p. 56, folio not stated).
  - **The shrine as a labour market.** "Sehwan, the historic city built within a large fortress from
    the time of Alexander the Great, is flooded with large numbers of female musicians who converge on
    the city to earn a living" (pp. 56–57, folios not stated). "Most of these women also sing at the
    Sufi shrines such as Hazrat Lal Shahbaz Qalandar in Sehwan, where they make a living. Here, they
    find an audience at any time of the night or day that pays them for their performance" (p. 33,
    folio not stated). At the shrine there are "other women—women who are looking for a livelihood—they
    join in the singing of sufiānā-kalām—and this happens every year" (p. 61, folio not stated).
  - Where the women come from: "most of the women musicians come from the Panjab and the neighborhoods
    of Sind itself. They perform in every nook and cranny and attract huge audiences" (p. 57, folio not
    stated).
  - **A reported disagreement between two sets of informants about when women sing, kept as a
    disagreement.** "Female informants in the city of Sehwan Sharif confirmed that many female
    performers come from the Panjab during the urs of Hazrat Lal Shahbaz Qalandar and sing at the
    shrine with the utmost devotion. Their audiences are men as well as women. My female informants did
    not have much information about women singers performing at other times of the year. However,
    professionals at Radio Pakistan in Hyderabad reported that women musicians come to the shrine only
    at the time of the urs celebrations" (p. 95, folio not stated).
  - Unamplified singing: "Performers like Reshma sing the melody in their high-pitched, full-throated
    voices at the shrine without the support of microphones" (p. 57, folio not stated).
  - **Careers begin here.** Reshma: "I first started at the Shahbaz Qalandar shrine—"; "Every year we
    go to the Shahbaz Qalandar shrine—"; "I was young at the time when I went there and made a
    mannat—for my brother" (p. 58, folio not stated). She still returns: "I go there every year… I go
    even now—I just returned from there—"; "The Sufi Shahbaz Qalandar calls for me at least three or
    four times—I go there and sing for him—I sing for my master—he listens to my songs—" (p. 61, folio
    not stated). She "sang the popular melody… at the shrine in Siraiki, when she was discovered by the
    media. This was during an urs of the mystic in the sixties" (p. 94, folio not stated) — **"in the
    sixties" is the book's entire dating and survives because it is spelled out; elsewhere the same
    fact is given as "the late sixties" (p. 56, folio not stated) and as "in the sixties" (p. 57,
    folio not stated), and the two are kept side by side.** Zeena Bai and Taj Mastani likewise: "Some
    of the women, like Zeena Bai, who sang as roving minstrels at the Lal Shahbaz Qalandar shrine,
    evolved into trained classical and semiclassical musicians in Hyderabad" (p. 63, folio not stated);
    Taj Mastani "affirmed that she belongs to Sehwan Sharif where the Lal Shahbaz Qalandar shrine is
    located" but "she moved to Hyderabad because it is a large urban center" (pp. 34, 63, folios not
    stated) — **the book thus records a musician leaving a major shrine town for a media market.**
  - The melody Reshma sang, as printed with the book's own facing gloss: "Lāj merī pat rakio bhalā" /
    "O, keep my prestige"; "Jhule Lālan," / "O Lalan, protector of the cradle,"; "Sindhṛi dā," / "O thou
    of Sind,"; "Sehwan dā," / "O thou of Sehwan,"; "Sakhi Shahbaz Qalandar" / "Benevolent Shahbaz
    Qalandar" (p. 94, folio not stated). **Within the same text the book prints `Jhole Lālan` at one
    line and `Jhule Lālan` at another, and `Sakhī Shāhbāz Qalandar` against `Sakhī Shahbaz Qalandar`
    (p. 57, folio not stated); both pairs are kept as printed.**
- **Legends & Miracles**
  - Performers "also sing poetic texts that glorify the karāmāt (mystical prowess) of Hazrat Lal
    Shahbaz Qalandar" (p. 57, folio not stated).
  - **Bestower of children.** "Hazrat Lal Shahbaz is venerated in the lyric as the bestower of
    children" (p. 57, folio not stated); the lyric itself, **with characters lost from two lines**:
    "M v n pira bachṛe denā [text-layer?: characters missing from this transliterated line] / Thou
    giver of children to mothers" and "Behn n denā-e vīr bhalā [text-layer?: characters missing] /
    Thou giver of brothers to sisters" (p. 57, folio not stated). Restated at p. 94 (folio not
    stated): Reshma's melody "honored Hazrat Lal Shahbaz Qalandar's jalāl or spiritual powers as a
    bestower of children", and she "believes that Hazrat Lal Shahbaz Qalandar has blessed her".
  - **A verse attributed to the saint himself, given as report.** "Hazrat Lal Shahbaz Qalandar is
    reported to have said of the dhammāl": "For the sake of the love of the friend / I dance over fire
    / Sometimes I roll in the dust / And sometimes I dance on the thorns / I have become notorious in
    your love / I beseech you to come to me / I am not afraid of this disrepute / To dance in every
    bazaar" (p. 65, folio not stated). **Abbas marks it "is reported to have said", not as a
    documented text.**
  - The "Mast Qalandar" melody: "The lyric, although dedicated to Ali, is also dedicated to Hazrat Lal
    Shahbaz Qalandar" (p. 57, folio not stated), with "Alī dam dam de andar / Ali's name is the
    healing breath" (p. 57, folio not stated). **The glossary, however, attaches `mast qalandar` to
    Ali alone: "Intoxicated mendicant; a melody sung to Ali, the fourth caliph of Islam" (p. 185,
    folio not stated) — recorded as printed, not reconciled.**
- **Significance**
  - Named as a fieldwork site in the book's own list: "Hazrat Lal Shahbaz Qalandar and Shah Abdul
    Latif in Sind" (p. 20, folio not stated).
  - A monograph devoted to him is cited: "Inam Muhammad, *Hazrat Lal Shahbaz Qalandar of Sehwan*
    (Jamshoro: Institute of Sindhology, [year dropped, text-layer])" (pp. 199, 229, folios not stated).
  - Used as the comparative standard for Gujarat: the Sidi dhammāl lasts "seven nights, in the manner
    of the dhammāl at the Lal Shahbaz Qalandar shrine" (p. 74, folio not stated).

### Place-level material: Sehwan / Sehwan Sharif / Jamshoro

*The book's place-level material on "Sehwan / Sehwan Sharif / Jamshoro" (2 rows), from its list of rows attached at place or person level, copied into every existing entry that list names (ruling (a); Rauf, 10 October 2026). It is about the place in general, not about this shrine alone.*

- **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**, **shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar** (the book's "Sehwan", "Sehwan Sharif" and "Jamshoro" as places — 2 rows)
  - **City-level material about Sehwan that is part of the place's tradition rather than of one shrine.**
    "Sehwan, the historic city built within a large fortress from the time of Alexander the Great, is
    flooded with large numbers of female musicians who converge on the city to earn a living" (pp. 56–57,
    folios not stated). **No date for the fortress beyond "from the time of Alexander the Great".**
    "during the urs celebrations, which last for more than a week, it is almost impossible to walk in the
    narrow streets of the city", reported by "Women informants who live around the heavily populated area
    of the shrine" (p. 56, folio not stated). Alan Faqir "is reported to have sung on the buses and trains
    between Rohri and Sehwan Sharif in his youth" (p. 78, folio not stated). Two fieldwork interviews are
    placed in the town without a shrine attached: "Asif, Muhammad. Medical doctor and informant. Personal
    interview. Sehwan Sharif, February , ." and "Saeeda, Asif. Informant. Personal interview. Sehwan
    Sharif, February , ." — **both years destroyed** (pp. 214, 217, folios not stated).
  - **Jamshoro** is named only as the seat of Sind University and the Institute of Sindhology (pp. 20,
    23, 64–69, 72–73, 79, 81, folios not stated), whose staff organised the author's Sindhi fieldwork and
    published two of the works she cites. `lal-shahbaz-qalandar` carries "Jamshoro District" in its own
    location field, and `shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar` carries "Jamshoro"; both take the Jamshoro material
    at place level.

### Province-level material: sindh

*The book's `sindh` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "the Sind province", "Sind"; refers to: "the coming of Islam in [numeral dropped] when Muhammad bin Qasim conquered the region", and then "After his conquest"; book's historical prose): "The Sufi mystical traditions came to the subcontinent of Pakistan and India through the Sind province"; Muhammad bin Qasim "came to Sind through the land route from Iran. After his conquest, a long line of Sufi mystics came to the region through Baluchistan, Mekran, Multan, and Sind itself" (p. 50, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "the province of Sind"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; author's statement, unsourced — "are said to be"): "In the province of Sind alone there are said to be more than a hundred thousand Sufi shrines" (p. 50, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "the Sind region"; refers to: Shah Abdul Latif's time (his death year is dropped, `(d.  )`) and "even today"; book's historical prose): "In the Sind region Shah Abdul Latif (d. [numeral dropped]) and other Sufi poets composed poetry in the vernacular languages and dialects in which they sang as roving mendicants within an Islamic framework. For fear of the clergy mystic poetry sung to music remained confined to the minstrel class" (p. 50, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "the Sind-Rajasthan area", "the Sind-Rajasthan continuum"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; author's argument): "There is evidence of roving minstrels in the Sind-Rajasthan area. Among them are the jogī snake charmers and flute players" (p. 77, folio not stated). The unit is the book's own cross-border compound; only the Sind half is attached here.
- (book's word: "Sind", "the Sind"; refers to: the author's fieldwork "in Sind in [numeral dropped]" and present tense; ethnographic present, glossary definition, endnote): "The Kolhis are the indigenous peasant communities of Sind who are sometimes migratory. Usually they are temporary farm laborers in the large feudal holdings. They are non-Muslims, and together with the Bhils they are the native inhabitants of the Sind-Rajasthan continuum" (p. 46, folio not stated); because Shah Abdul Latif wrote "in the Sindhi vernacular, the Kohli and Bhil male and female peasants can understand and relate to it" (p. 47, folio not stated). The Glossary defines "Bhīls Aboriginal landless roving peasants in Sind" (p. 179, folio not stated) and "Kolhis Aboriginal landless roving peasants in the Sind" (p. 184, folio not stated). An unpaired endnote reads "Non-Muslim migratory peasants who work on large landholdings in Sind and Indian Gujrat" (p. 200, folio not stated). The text prints both "Kolhi" and "Kohli".
- (book's word: "Sind", "the Sind"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; author's argument, glossary definition): "In Sind there can be no ritual without the Manganhar, be it a birth, a wedding, or a death. They are the designated community musicians, and each community has its own Manganhars" (p. 77, folio not stated); "In Sind, the Sidis of African descent and the Manganhar musicians render the ceremonial singing of devotional narratives" (p. 169, folio not stated). The Glossary gives "Manganhār Hereditary professional musicians in the Sind and Rajasthan", and "mangiārī" and "mangtī" as "A female musician in the Sind and Rajasthan" (p. 184, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "the Sind province of Pakistan", "the Sind"; refers to: "now", the author's fieldwork among the Sidi in Karachi Mori (year dropped), with the arrival placed "through the ages"; ethnographic present, plus the informant's statement of Qalandar Shah): Sidis "were initially brought as slaves from Africa to work in the subcontinent of Pakistan and India. Although they are now free, some of them are attached to the feudal households in the Sind province of Pakistan. Their native language is Sindhi" (pp. 67–68, folios not stated). Qalandar Shah says: "the Africans are present in the Sind in large numbers—because of the slave trade—they were brought here in large numbers—and then—with the abolition of slavery—here too they were set free"; "They had been coming continuously—through the ages" (p. 69, folio not stated). Also: "the Sidis in Sind and Kacch claim descent from Hazrat Bilal" (p. 167, folio not stated). The Glossary gives "Sidi Also known as Sheedi or Syedi in Sind and Gujrat" (p. 188, folio not stated), and the mugarmān drum is "held sacred by the Sidi communities in Sind, Baluchistan, Rajasthan, and Gujrat who use them in their shrines and for rituals" (p. 185, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "the Sind"; refers to: the author's fieldwork present (years dropped); informants' statements reported by the author, and author's argument): "The Sidi, Mohana, and Manganhar women associated with feudal households in the Sind said that they sing devotional Sufi poetry at their mentor's shrine only on special occasions, such as his urs or a religious event", "only perform before female audiences in their murshid's shrine", and "their female patrons pay them well" (p. 95, folio not stated); these musicians "sing in the Syed households in the Sind among women audiences only" (p. 112, folio not stated); "Sidi women render ritual musical services in the households in the Sind" (p. 167, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sind", "the Sind"; refers to: the author's fieldwork present, and present tense; informant's statement of Taj Mastani, Alan Faqir's own words, glossary definition): Taj Mastani can negotiate "with television stations and her clients from the affluent feudal households in Sind, who can reach her for ritual ceremonies" (p. 34, folio not stated); the Glossary defines "vaderā A feudal landlord in the Sind" (p. 189, folio not stated); Alan Faqir: "you see the vaḍerās [landowners] have disputes with each other", and they come to the faqīr for a duā (p. 78, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "In Sind", "the Sind", "Sindhi Sufi poetry"; refers to: the author's fieldwork present (the Radio Pakistan interview year is dropped); author's argument, informants' statements of Sikander Baloch and Naseer Mirza, glossary definition, endnote): "In Sind this form of choir singing is called sūng" (p. 40, folio not stated); Baloch and Mirza "used the indigenous term sūng for the practice in the Sufi poetics and music where the lover woos the estranged beloved through a serenade on the capṛī", and "The speech and song create ecstasy, which is a very special context of some shrines in Sind" (p. 55, folio not stated); Baloch: "that is why there is this school of thought in Sindhi Sufi poetry—the sūng—in which the lover sings to win back the estranged beloved—this is called manānā" (p. 63, folio not stated). The Glossary has "sūng A choir that sings at the Sufi shrines in Sind" (p. 188, folio not stated). An endnote says "A native Sindhi informant corrected this term to mean a choir of mendicants who sing mystical poetry, but I retain the context in which I interviewed Baloch and Mirza" (p. 198, folio not stated). These three senses are kept as printed.
- (book's word: "the shrines in Sind"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; author's argument): sufiānā-kalām "is usually sung in the vernacular and is a feature of the shrines in Sind, especially those of Hazrat Lal Shahbaz Qalandar and Shah Abdul Latif at Bhit" (p. 85, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "the Sindhi shrines"; refers to: the author's fieldwork present; author's argument): among the areas of female participation she identified: "Women as singers/musicians/participants of Sufi songs, sometimes even called the faqīrīanī in the Sindhi shrines" (p. 21, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "the Sind"; refers to: "now", the speaker's twenty years with Radio Pakistan; informant's statement of Naseer Mirza (`M:`), with Reshma's answer): the loṭevālī gypsy women sang only at the Shahbaz Qalandar shrine, "certainly, that is the only place in the Sind now where the loṭevālīs come"; "I have not seen such a tradition at Bhit Shah—not at Sacchal Sarmast—not at Rikhail Shah—or any of the Sufi dargāhs [shrines] in the Sind—no loṭevālī passes by these shrines—except at Qalandar Lal Shahbaz" (p. 61, folio not stated). Asked whether it is "more of a tradition in the Sind—in the Rajasthan area that women will sing at the Sufi shrines", Reshma answered "Yes—this is so at the shrines—not so much in Rajasthan—but it is at the Shahbaz Qalandar shrine" (p. 60, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "the Sind"; refers to: the time of the Radio Pakistan interview, year dropped; informants' claim, Baloch and Mirza): Abida Parvin uses the ḍohra or bait within her narrative, a style she took from her male mentors, and "They claim that no other woman musician in the Sind has this style, which is essentially a unique male style" (p. 55, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "the Sind", "Sind"; refers to: present tense; author's argument): "Among musician communities in the Sind, Shah's Risālo is treated with great respect because of its sacred Quranic context, and the book is not allowed to lie around on the floor" (p. 51, folio not stated); "Since large sections of the Quran are quoted in his Risālo, musicians in Sind hold Shah's written text in the utmost veneration" (p. 166, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sind", "Baluchistan"; refers to: present tense, from ethnomusicologists the author met, period not stated; author's argument reporting researchers, and an endnote citing a film): the mystical lore of "the Kacch-Rajasthan-Sind-Baluchistan continuum": "The mystical lore of the region that includes Sind, Baluchistan, and the coastal belt of Iran shares myths, melodies, and linguistic codes", among them "the famous 'Mast Qalandar' dhun" (pp. 47–48, folios not stated); the dhun is "a tune reported to be sung all along the Rajasthan, Sind, Baluchistan, and Iran continuum", and "Almost every folksinger in the region can sing it" (p. 57, folio not stated); an endnote reports that Michael Wood's film *In the Footsteps of Alexander* portrays "the Indus along the Baluchistan coast back through Persia as a route that seems to have been used for centuries" (p. 210, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sind", "Baluchistan"; refers to: the myth's origin, period not stated, and present-day singing; author's argument): the Sassi myth is "told in the lands of Sind and Baluchistan. In the oral traditions of the region the legend is sung with all the emotion that manifests Sassi's grief" (p. 118, folio not stated); "Performers love to sing the Sassi myth in the regional languages and dialects of Sind, Baluchistan, and the Siraiki-speaking regions" (p. 120, folio not stated); "the Sassi myth that originated in Baluchistan and Sind, the area currently referred to as a continuum in ethnomusicology" (p. 156, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "all the regions of the Sind"; refers to: present tense; author's argument): the Sohni myth "is sung in Sufi poetry in all the regions of the Sind, the Siraiki-speaking area of the Panjab" (pp. 126–127, folios not stated).
- (book's word: "northern Sind areas"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; author's argument): "In the Sufi poetry sung in the oral traditions in the Siraiki belt around the Multan, Bahawalpur, Mianwali, and northern Sind areas, the female voices are again prominent" (p. 148, folio not stated). Only the "northern Sind" part attaches here; the Punjab side is named by towns only.
- (book's word: "the Sindhi devotional contexts"; refers to: present tense; author's argument): "virāhīnī poetry is also sung in the Sindhi devotional contexts, such as the mauluds that celebrate the Prophet's birth. In these songs the devotee is a young bride or bride-to-be who awaits her bridegroom, who is equated with the prophet Muhammad" (p. 149, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sind", "Sindhri"; refers to: centuries of invasion, "when the Mongols came—the Tartars came—the Sassanids came", spoken at the author's interview (year dropped); informant's statement of Alan Faqir, glossed by the author in endnotes): "Alan Faqir asserted in his interview about Sind: … It has been looted—it has been plundered—it has been assaulted—but it goes on—it has not died—it will not die—it has existed for centuries and it shall exist—Sindhri is beaten—and yet Sindhri goes on" (pp. 156–157, folios not stated). On his recited line "Punnu is not in the Kec / Sind is not the amir" (p. 156, folio not stated), the author's notes say "the reference is to the fact that Sind does not lead the land politically" and "'Sind is not the amir' means that Sind is not in the position of leadership or amirship"; "Sindhri" is "A name used for Sind in the poetic traditions"; and "These are all Alan Faqir's political interpretations of the female myths" (pp. 210–211, folios not stated).
- (book's word: "Sind", "Sindhi"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; glossary definitions): ceremonial music and song of the region: "gīc Ceremonial songs usually sung by females at births and weddings in the Sind" (p. 182, folio not stated); "jamālo A folk tune in the Sind; also, a dance" and "kapātī Cotton-spinners' songs, in Sind" (p. 183, folio not stated); "olang A ḍhol or drum rhythm played at the bridegroom's home in Sind" (p. 186, folio not stated); "sādmānā Melody played on ceremonial occasions in Sind" (p. 187, folio not stated); "candar A ceremonial Sindhi melody played on auspicious occasions" and "capr" / "ī" [text-layer?: capṛī] "Wooden castanets that are played for rhythm in Sindhi folk music" (p. 180, folio not stated); "haswārī A ceremonial Sindhi tune usually played with drums and bagpipes; Sindhi musicians claim to play it for the pirs and murshids when they appear at festive ceremonies such as a wedding" (p. 182, folio not stated); "lāon A Sindhi nuptial song" (p. 184, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Panjab and Sind"; refers to: present tense; glossary definition): "dhammāl The vigorous ritual dance performed at Sufi shrines in Panjab and Sind" (p. 180, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "a traditional Sindhi lunch"; refers to: the day of the Karachi Mori visit (year dropped); author's anecdote): "fish cooked in curry, a traditional Sindhi lunch eaten with rice and flatbread" (p. 71, folio not stated).

## From Robert Rozehnal, Islamic Sufism Unbound

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/rozehnal_islamic_sufism_unbound.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

### Province-level material: sindh

*The book's `sindh` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: British colonial rule, as above; quoted source: Katherine Ewing): "British policy, especially in the Punjab and Sind, was to treat them in essentially the same way that landlords and tribal leaders were treated" — the hereditary saints (sajjada nishin) (p. 33, folio 23); note 10 cites Ansari, *Sufi Saints and State Power: The Pirs of Sind, 1843–1947* (p. 241, folio 234; bibliographic only).
- (book's word: "Sindhu Desh", glossed in the book "the ethnic Sindhis’ country"; refers to: period not stated — a statement of Shaykh Wahid Bakhsh, who "passed away in 1995"; quoted source: Wahid Bakhsh, in the book's translation, n. 99): "Nationalist parties are raising slogans of “Pashtunistan” [“home of the ethnic Pashtuns” ], “Azad Balochistan” [“a free home for the ethnic Baluchis”], and “Sindhu Desh” [“the ethnic Sindhis’ country”]"; "They do not realize that four smaller countries, which they wish to create, will be swallowed by India overnight." The author: "In Wahid Bakhsh’s view, the homegrown divisions of ethnic rivalry are as destructive as any external enemy" (p. 127, folio 118). The book's word is the slogan, not "Sindh"; attached because the slogan names the Sindhis' country — weigh accordingly.

## From Annemarie Schimmel, As Through a Veil

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/schimmel_as_through_a_veil.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

- **Evidence base:** two chunks (012, 015) — two statements inside a single endnote and one index
  headword. Short, but the endnote is substantive: it reports a living devotional practice rather than a
  bare name.
- Saint: index headword printed `Lal Shahbaz Qalandar (13th cent.)` (p. 357, folio 339) — a century, not
  a year.
- Shrine / Darbar: named with his site as "Lal Shahbaz Qalandar of Sehwan" (p. 287, folio 269); no
  building, ʿurs, custodianship or date is described.
- Practices & Events:
  - "in Sind, where the women often use endearing diminutives (Piral, Ghauthal) in addressing the saints,
    we find poems in honor of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar of Sehwan, who is often implored when a woman wants a
    son (Annemarie Schimmel, "Hochzeitslieder der Frauen im Industal")" (p. 287, folio 269) — women's
    petitionary devotion for a son, the genre being women's wedding songs.
  - The devotion crosses languages: "examples for Lal Shahbaz and Pir Murad of Thatta are also found in
    Balochi because Baloch tribes migrate in winter into the Indus Valley; see Longworth Dames, Popular
    Poetry." (p. 287, folio 269)
- Significance: he is the book's instance of a saint addressed in vernacular women's poetry and carried
  by seasonal migration into a second language community (p. 287, folio 269).

### Doubts the notes raise on this row

**Seven death years in the book disagree with the archive rows.** All seven are printed headwords in
the `Index of Proper Names`, i.e. a 1982 Columbia monograph disagreeing with the sheet, not OCR
noise. The archive's `figure_died` is given only for comparison.

- `Lal Shahbaz Qalandar (13th cent.)` (p. 357, folio 339) against 1274 — a century where the archive
  has a year.

- **Names that could match two rows.** "Abdul Latif": `bhit-bhit-shah` against `bari-imam` (Sayyid
  Abdul Latif Kazmi) — resolved once on "Shah" plus "in Sind" (p. 43, folio 33) and once on the date
  plus the `Risal6` sub-entry (p. 349, folio not stated), but the bare name reads either way. "Shah
  Inayat": `dargah-roza-sufi-shah-inayat-shaheed` against `shrine-of-shah-inayat-qadiri`, settled by
  the book's own "of Jhok" and 1718 (p. 175, folio 157; p. 285, folio 267; p. 338, folio 320).
  "Shams" is refused for `shrine-of-shah-shams-ud-din-sabzwari`, `shamsabad` and `sial-sharif`
  wherever it is Rumi's companion (p. 118, folio 108; p. 143, folio 125; p. 365, folio not stated;
  p. 376, folio 358) — and, separately, the book **does not identify** "Shams Tabriz of Multan"
  (p. 285, folio 267; p. 326, folio 308) or the venerated Multan shrine of "Shams-i Tabriz" (p. 175,
  folio 157) with any archive figure; `shrine-of-shah-shams-ud-din-sabzwari` sits in the Multan head
  **because of the place only**, and the distinction must not be collapsed. "Omarkot" →
  `umarkot-amarkot-shiv-mandir` on spelling alone (p. 224, folio 206; p. 359, folio 341), and was
  refused elsewhere (p. 172, folio 154). "Rohri": no archive row is *in* Rohri, both candidates name
  it as adjacent, and the chunks split between parking it (p. 224, folio 206) and heading it (p. 361,
  folio 343). "Sehwan": taken as the saint's epithet in one chunk, claiming only `lal-shahbaz-qalandar`
  (p. 287, folio 269), and as a bare toponym in another, adding `shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar`
  (p. 361, folio 343). "Hyderabad": the book prints "Hyderabad/Sind", "Hyderabad/Deccan", "Hyderabad
  AP" and one bare, undisambiguated "Hyderabad" (p. 301, folio 283; p. 321, folio 303; p. 355,
  folio 337) — only the first carries `guru-gurpat-mandir-db-80-sirey-ghat`. "Hinglaj" is headed with
  `shaktipeeth-shri-hinglaj-mata-mandir` only; `chandragup-baba-chandragup` (Hingol National Park) is
  the other candidate and is not claimed (p. 354, folio 336). A bare "Zakariya" occurs twice and must
  not be merged with the Multan saint: the prophet in al-Kisāʾī (p. 285, folio 267) and a bare index
  headword (p. 363, folio 345), against "Baha'uddin Zakariya Multani" (p. 286, folio 268; p. 351,
  folio 333).

### Place-level material: Sehwan

*The book's material headed "Sehwan (place-level: 2 rows)", copied into every existing entry that heading lists (ruling (a); Rauf, 10 October 2026). It is about the place in general, not about this shrine alone.*

**Kind of evidence: INDEX HEADWORD ONLY — not an imprint, but not the book discussing Sehwan either.**
No shrine, saint, practice or date attaches to the place at this reference.

- `Sehwan` stands as its own headword (p. 361, folio 343).
- Form discrepancy, kept as the chunk left it: both archive rows give "Sehwan Sharif" in
  `location_short`; the book prints the bare form `Sehwan` (p. 361, folio 343).

### Province-level material: sindh

*The book's `sindh` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "in the centuries after his death", i.e. after Bada'uni, no dates given; book's historical prose): mystical poets "in Sind and the Punjab" developed "the art of utilizing simple folk tales as media for mystical instruction" (p. 154, folio 136).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "the first half of the sixteenth century" (Qadi Qadan) to "the eighteenth century" (Shah 'Abdul Latif), as printed; book's historical prose): "In Sind, the range of folk tales utilized for mystical purposes is even wider." Early allusions appear "however veiled, in Qadi Qadan's verse"; they "become more obvious in Shah 'Abdul Karim of Bulrri's [text-layer?] short verses, and reach full bloom in the Risiilo [text-layer?] [Risālo] of his great-grandson, Shah 'Abdul Latif of Bhit"; "Since the original tales were known to everyone, he usually begins his poetical renderings with the most dramatic moment" (p. 171, folio 153).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "shortly after 900", as printed; book's historical prose): Sind is "the country which he [Hallaj] traversed shortly after 900 to call people to God" (p. 175, folio 157).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: the author's own time, date not stated; author's anecdote): "It is moving to listen to illiterate folk musicians in remote corners of Sind ... when they repeat his name [Hallaj's] in ecstatic songs, and claim that he was the true lover who paid with his life for his love" (the same sentence names "qawwals in the dargah of Gulbarga"; that part is not Sind) (p. 175, folio 157).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: period not stated, "contemporaries" of "the urban poets in India"; book's historical prose): poets "in the plains of Sind and the Punjab wrote tender little poems or instructive Golden Alphabets to instil love for him [the Prophet] into the souls of the villagers" (p. 222, folio 204).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: period not stated; author's argument): "The great mystical poets of Sind have devoted wonderful songs to the Prophet, who appears as the dawn between the night of created human life and the Divine day" (p. 223, folio 205).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: the author's own time, date not stated; author's anecdote in endnote 48): "Incidentally, I have learned more about Islamic piety from women in Anatolia and Sind than from most of my learned colleagues or from sophisticated books—they still cling to 'the faith of the old women' as it was praised in early Islam, and represent the values of a moderately mystical Islam" (p. 281, folio 263).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: present tense, and "in more recent times" for the Pir Pagaro verses; author's argument in endnote 109, citing Annemarie Schimmel, "Hochzeitslieder der Frauen im Industal"): "in Sind, where the women often use endearing diminutives (Piral, Ghauthal [text-layer?]) in addressing the saints, we find poems in honor of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar of Sehwan ... and, in more recent times, verses devoted to the Pir Pagaro" (p. 287, folio 269). Only the region-wide part (the women's diminutives and the existence of saint-poems in Sind) belongs here. The Lal Shahbaz/Sehwan detail ("often implored when a woman wants a son") is a site statement under ruling (a).

## From Annemarie Schimmel, Mystical Dimensions of Islam

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/schimmel_mystical_dimensions_of_islam.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

Printed `Lai Shahbaz` [text-layer?: Lal Shahbaz], `Lai Shahbaz Qalandar` [text-layer?: Lāl Shahbāz
Qalandar], and called `Qalandar` and `the saint of Sehwan` (pp. 385–386, 520, 523).

- **Saint**
  - **Two readings of the death year, kept side by side.** The index prints `Lai Shahbaz Qalandar
    (d. 1262), 354-55` (p. 520, folio 489). **The archive row records 1274.** No other date is given
    in running text.
  - He is introduced at a chunk boundary, in a sentence that breaks off: `Another aspect of mystical
    life developed in the same period during which Muclnuddln Chishtl and BahjPuddin Zakariya were
    preaching divine love and union: its representative is Lai Shahbaz,` (p. 385, folio 354). So he is
    placed in the same period as Muʿīnuddīn Chishtī and Bahāʾuddīn Zakariyā and announced as the
    representative of a **different** `aspect of mystical life`; the predicate falls on the next page.
  - The book never prints `Usman Marwandi` (not stated). A separate index entry `Marwand, 355` (p. 520,
    folio 489) stands on its own, with a locator adjacent to his; **the book does not join them.**
  - Cross-referenced at `Qalandar. See Abu CA1I Qalandar, Shah; Lai Shahbaz Qalandar` (p. 523,
    folio `4Q2` [text-layer?: 492]) — the other name in that cross-reference, `Abu CA1I Qalandar, Shah`
    [text-layer?: Shāh Abū ʿAlī Qalandar], is **not** an archive row and is not mapped.
- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **The book's only statement that a Muslim shrine of this archive stands on a Hindu site**: the
    saint of Sehwan settled `at the site of the old Shiva sanctuary at the west bank of the lower
    Indus` (p. 386, folio 355); and `A lingam close to the actual tomb proves the Shivait background of
    some of the rites.` (p. 386, folio 355). **`proves` is the book's own word, and the claim is made
    without a source.**
  - The book never prints `Sehwan Sharif`; a separate index entry `Sehwan, 355` stands alone (p. 525,
    folio 494).
- **Practices & Events**
  - `Around his sanctuary strange groups of bl-shar^` [text-layer?: bī-sharʿ] `, "lawless," Sufis
    (malang) gathered, dressed in black, and the mystical current connected with Sehwan often shows
    Sufism in its least attractive aspects.` (p. 386, folio 355). **Schimmel's own valuation is part of
    the sentence and is kept rather than paraphrased away.**
  - **The fair, with its dates**: `The "fair" of Sehwan—celebrated between Shawwal 18 and 20—has become
    notorious for its rather illicit events.` (p. 386, folio 355). The book writes `"fair"` in
    quotation marks and **does not use the word *ʿurs***; `Shawwal 18 and 20` [text-layer?: clean] are
    days of a Muslim month, not years. No attendance figure, no organiser and no year — (not stated).
  - The `INDEX OF SUBJECTS` glosses the attendants: `Mdlang (dervish at the sanctuary in Sehwan), 355`
    (p. 536, folio 505) — see `## REVIEW (worker A)`.
- **Significance**
  - Footnote 14 at the foot of the same page carries Schimmel's own assessment of the standard English
    book on saint worship in Sind, and is worth carrying to any Sindhi entry: `Peter Mayne, Saints of
    Sind (London, 1956` [text-layer?: clean] `), gives a vivid and often ironical account of the
    customs of so-called saints; yet his brilliantly written book contains much valuable information
    about little-known facts of saint worship and mystical thought in the Indus Valley.` (p. 386,
    folio 355).

### Place-level material: Sehwan

*The book's place-level material on "Sehwan" (2 rows), from its list of rows attached at place or person level, copied into every existing entry that list names (ruling (a); Rauf, 10 October 2026). It is about the place in general, not about this shrine alone.*

- **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**, **shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar** (the book's bare `Sehwan` — both rows whose `location_short` contains Sehwan, ruling (a);
  `toponym_map.txt`'s key is `sehwan sharif` and the book prints `Sehwan` alone, so the join was made on
  the `location_short` column as ruling (a) directs):
  - The place-level material is the *malang* sentence, the "fair" with its Shawwal dates, and the
    Shiva-sanctuary and lingam sentences — all quoted in full under **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**
    (p. 386, folio 355). **The book names no shrine at Sehwan other than the tomb of its own saint**,

    (p. 386, folio 355), quoted under **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**.
